## SWAP 2025 Online Resources

The final approved SWAP 2025 from April 2026 (PDF format), related tables, data visualization tools (e.g., dashboard or web-map viewer), and information about how to request SWAP data is available and/or linked on the WDFW SWAP webpage wdfw.wa.gov/species-habitats/at-risk/swap.

## Why SWAP?

## Background

Creating and maintaining a SWAP is optional unless a state or territory wants to receive federal State Wildlife Grant (SWG) conservation funding. 
1
 Currently, every U.S. state and territory has a SWAP that is developed, maintained, implemented and updated by a state fish and wildlife agency or other state natural resources agency with fish and wildlife conservation responsibilities.
The USFWS Office of Conservation Investment (OCI) is responsible for SWG delivery and SWAP review and approval. Each SWAP is reviewed by an RRT to ensure the plan meets the administrative requirements outlined in the 2017 USFWS and Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (AFWA) Guidance 2 with a focus on the Eight Required Elements related to Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) and engagement during the revision 3 :
1. species distribution and status; 2. related habitats and ecosystems; 3. threats and issues; 4. conservation actions; 5. monitoring and adaptive management; 6. plan review and update interval and process; 7. agency, tribe, and organization coordination; and 8. public engagement.
To demonstrate this plan meets those elements, Appendix A provides the RRT a 'Road Map' (USFWSrequired template) to find those Elements within the plan and summarize Element changes since the last version of the SWAP (2015). Revision-required administrative correspondence is in Appendix B.
The Guidance does not prescribe the methods states use to meet the Elements. States may organize SWAP content in any way and expand the plan to communicate other state-specific conservation values, 
1
 USFWS 2001, USFWS N.d. 
2
 USFWS and AFWA 2017 3 AFWA N.d.a. needs, and actions. SWAPs can connect conservation priorities across political boundaries, include mechanisms to help face difficult or rapidly emerging conservation questions, illustrate conservation planning best practices 4 and the current state of conservation, foster partnerships, and broaden funding for implementation.
AFWA maintains extensive online resources about the SWAP program including its importance and success across the nation, progress reports, states' plans, best practices, and a SWAP Learning Series for coordinators and partners to share lessons learned and good ideas. 
5
 Using these resources, experiences from the SWAP community of practice, extensive natural resources expertise in Washington state, and networks of experts across the region and the world, the Washington SWAP has communicated fish, wildlife, and habitats conservation needs for more than 20 years.
2025 is a milestone year for SWAPs and the conservation network that engages with these plans. This is the 20 th anniversary of the first SWAPs and the 25 th anniversary of the State and Tribal Wildlife Grants programs. In September of this year, the AFWA Wildlife Diversity Conservation and Funding Committee submitted a resolution that was passed by the AFWA State Directors reaffirming the value of this third generation of SWAPs and the importance of secure and dedicated funding for implementation. 
6
 Washington Department of Fish and Wildlife (WDFW) has created two previous SWAPs (2005 and 2015) 7 ; this is the third edition.
As with both of the previous SWAPs, the 2025 plan is a high-level descriptive and informational document that contains no "actions" as defined in WAC 197-11-704. The plan provides recommendations for conservation actions and does not require the development of a series of connected actions. The plan does not contain or direct standards controlling use or modification of the environment. In summary, the 2025 SWAP does not require a State Environmental Policy Act (SEPA) determination under RCW 43.21C.030 because it is not an agency action under WAC 197-11-704. Specific proposals conducted under the SWAP should undergo SEPA review as appropriate. 8

## Purpose

For humans, species and their habitats provide food and forage, cultural and spiritual touchstones, creative inspiration and well-being, clean air and water, recreation and livelihoods. Conserving biodiversity -the full range of life in all forms, including habitats and systems -is important to all of us. 9 Conservation ensures that plants, fish, wildlife, and the habitats on which they depend can continue to support and inspire humans and be enjoyed by future generations, in whatever way they may legally Competitive/Multi-state-SWG, and TWG can meet. alone. In all years to date, SWG and TWG support has been bipartisan. 
13
 In federal fiscal years 2024 and 2025, the total amount of SWG was just over $72 million for all states and territories. Federal funding levels have remained largely flat for over a decade which, with inflation, actually represents a decrease in spending capacity. Washington received approximately $1.3 million in SWG funds in 2024. 
14
 To put that amount in perspective for WDFW's conservation needs, WDFW requested $47.6 million in biodiversity conservation funding in the 2023-25 operating budget from the state legislature for partial SWAP implementation and other key biodiversity actions. 
15
 The agency received partial funding, just over half of the request to increase the agency's and partners' abilities to recover at-risk species and their habitats, develop efforts to evaluate and manage SGCN, and engage in conservation-crucial public education and outreach. 
16
 Unfortunately, a tough state budget cycle in 2025 and a reduced state revenue forecast permanently reduced WDFW's funding for that work. 
17
 The state fiscal near-term outlook is unknown for restoring and increasing the biodiversity funding, but this continues to be a need that WDFW and other conservation organizations will pursue.
Through implementation and partnerships that have jelled around SWAP conservation actions, these plans have grown into well-accepted guides to good conservation action, acknowledged by and invested in by other state, federal, and private funding sources and partnerships. Most states, including Washington, cobble together many funding sources to implement a fraction of their SWAP. Conservation needs are outpacing the current level of funding and conservation partners' abilities to respond to emerging issues, gain traction on tough issues, and maintain progress.
Nationally, a broad coalition supports increased and sustained conservation funding for diverse fish and wildlife and their habitats, using SWAPs as good blueprints to deliver conservation. 
18
 Having a SWAP is more important now than ever.

## Implement and Support the WDFW Strategic Plan

The SWAP contains many conservation recommendations, some of which are led and owned by other agencies, organizations, Tribes, and communities. The SWAP is a key plan that informs WDFW work, however the SWAP cannot make a commitment to action in a timeframe -there are not enough resources for full SWAP implementation. The SWAP alone is not work-planning or resources allocation for the Department. This plan highlights many important issues and the conservation recommendations in the SWAP speak to those, even if they are not WDFW's to lead or if WDFW cannot begin the work

 right now. This plan supports the conservation community at large of which WDFW is a part. WDFW has identified the SWAP as a tool to implement a portion of the agency's 25-Year Strategic Plan.

 Related to the Strategic Plan, the SWAP provides

## Improve Conservation Communication

Our best chance of long-term, effective, and durable conservation progress is to connect people to opportunities to participate in and affect conservation outcomes. This version of the SWAP will look and feel different from the 2015 SWAP to provide easier-to-find information and make meaningful connections among people who want to use the SWAP and the ecologists, biologists, and communities that recognize and define conservation needs.
Despite the name, the SWAP isn't just limited to "wildlife." The Washington SWAP highlights information and conservation actions for wildlife, fish, plants, habitats, ecosystems and landscapes in which people live, work, and play. We are all connected by our lands and waters, a sense of place that feeds us literally and culturally, traditionally and in new ways every day. We eat, breathe, drink, and play, benefitting from all that Washington's natural resources offer. Much of our economy is resourcesbased in agriculture, forestry, fisheries, technology, green energy, open spaces and outdoor recreation. 
20
 All of this is dependent on beneficial and well-communicated connections among people and biodiversity.

## Conservation Compass Points

Effective conservation is founded on scientific integrity, data, and information. Effective conservation is also influenced by values and big picture themes that overlay more localized issues, human engagement and behavior, and the relationships among humans and the natural environment. WDFW applies the principles in Policy 5004 to our actions, and introduces the following conservation themes and values in this section as Compass Points to guide conservation actions throughout SWAP. Like a compass heading, these provide direction along the way to our destination: resilient ecosystems and habitats with sustained plant, fish, and wildlife populations.
Conservation Compass Points are conservation principles to improve the pace, scale, and effectiveness of conservation. These are not conservation actions or prescriptions by themselves, but they inform and shape the species, habitat, and system conservation actions throughout the Plan.
Ten SWAP Compass Points are described in this section:
1. increase the pace and scale of biodiversity conservation 2. start 'upstream' to identify and address issues 3. acknowledge conservation complexity 4. document and report progress as an important part of success 5. promote scientific integrity and transparency 6. expand and continue engagement 7. prioritize actions that address resilience, connectivity, and OneHealth 8. facilitate effective voluntary conservation action 9. value inter-governmental conservation opportunities at all scales, and 10. monitor, learn, adapt and communicate.

## Increase the pace and scale of biodiversity conservation

People in Washington are connected by our lands and waters, and by a sense of place that feeds us literally and culturally, traditionally and in new ways every day. Beneficial connections among people, fish and wildlife, plants and habitats, air, water, and soils create a natural collaboration that supports us when we eat, breathe, drink, work and play in all that Washington offers.
Washington State has recognized strong connections among biodiversity and our healthy and vibrant Washington communities, economy, and recreation opportunities. 
21
 In 2002, the Legislature directed the State to "develop a comprehensive framework to safeguard Washington's rich biodiversity heritage for the benefit of current and future generations." The resulting Biodiversity Conservation Strategy 22 paired well with Washington's Comprehensive Wildlife Conservation Strategy (the first Washington SWAP). 
23
 Since the 2015 version of the SWAP 24 , the conservation, natural resources management, and development communities have better tools (e.g., remote sensing, cloud-based computing) to detect and understand the pace and scale of changes in temperature, water availability, ecosystem shifts and connectivity, invasive species and diseases, and the distribution of development associated with human populations and industry. These are key factors affecting species and habitat sustainability. NatureServe reports that 34% of plant species and 40% of animal species in the United States are at risk of extinction 
21
 Van Deynze 2024 
22
 Washington Biodiversity Council 2007b 
23 WDFW 2005
 and that 41% of ecosystems are at risk of range-wide collapse. In the same report, more than 18% of assessed ecosystems in Washington state are at risk. 
25
 Losses in habitat availability, usability, and connectivity influence species' ability to adapt and/or recover. Those threats have not changed much in the last 25 years although community awareness, public support, partnerships, and opportunities have improved. 
26
 Recommendations from the 2007 BCS continue to be relevant today:
• include landscape approaches to conservation,
• enhance incentives for voluntary conservation by private landowners, and
• engage citizens in monitoring and conservation action.
We aren't necessarily doing the wrong things for conservation; we're just not doing enough of the right things at a scale and pace to stay ahead of habitat loss, system degradation, and species loss. Strategies for conserving biodiversity have not changed that much in 25 years: applying good stewardship on owned lands and waters; well-crafted and durable conservation easements; conservation regulation of lands, waters, and wild native species uses; habitat conservation plans and mitigation banking; voluntary conservation influence and incentives; and addressing invasive species. 
27
 But, the available opportunities to apply those solutions and the emergence of threats to system resilience have changed.
In 2023, the conservation community and the state Legislature agreed that new solutions are needed to conserve biodiversity, improve conditions for resilient and sustainable habitats in a cumulative way, better preserve opportunities to stabilize and recover declining fish and wildlife populations, and conserve ecological functions that support us all. 
28
 To exceed current efforts to protect and restore ecosystems across the state, we need to identify options to galvanize, incentivize, or in some circumstances require projects to increase biodiversity and/or resilience in affected ecosystems.
This SWAP presents biodiversity conservation strategies at statewide and ecoregional scales to expand single-species or single-resource protection programs to conservation support at many scales (sites to landscapes) of conservation work, much of which can also improve ecosystem services valued by people (e.g., timber production, flood control, water quality and quantity, recreation, creativity).
Non-habitat interventions (e.g., hatcheries, predator management, reintroductions, captive rearing) may be needed to provide critical support to species on the brink of losing genetic diversity or that are not yet completely supported in natural habitats. These interventions may also help preserve human rights, needs, and cultural heritage. These interventions must be paired with systems-level restoration, habitat enhancement, and natural process protections and improvements. Our ability to stem the tide of natural system and species loss and improve habitat and species sustainability relies on the 
25
 NatureServe 2023 
26

## Start 'upstream' to identify and address issues

To create long-term and sustainable improvement in habitats and systems that support species and people, conservation challenges must be addressed as far 'up-stream' as possible: identify root causes of existing issues and think about early warning signs for potential problems, in addition to reacting or responding to site-specific and 'down-stream' symptoms and consequences.
Getting to the headwaters of an issue requires that the project developers keep asking questions to understand what could create a durable solution, name what is creating a barrier to progress, define who is affected and can help. Identify the parts of the problem that are technical (more information or data could solve the problem) and which are adaptive (cultural, social, or system influences). It could be different information, people, or places to solve each of those parts.
Example. Northern Leopard Frogs (NLF) have all but disappeared from their historic range in Washington state. A site-based solution would be to only reintroduce frogs where they used to occur. An 'upstream' solution applies the site-based solution and asks questions about why they are in trouble in that site. The 'upstream' solution improves the context of the reintroduction for more durable outcomes: improves habitat sustainability, removes invasive species, and works with land and water managers to ensure long-term stability in the habitats required by NLF. In the NLF recovery work, the reintroduction is paired with upstream solutions to improve progress and the effectiveness of the reintroduction (Figure 
1
). Often, complex problems are influenced by values and tradeoffs, need a system-level approach, and require social and cultural support before ever getting to the ecological or technical work. These tougher solutions often have more risk: failure is possible, testing and learning will need to be part of the project plan, available information may be insufficient, and new information may come from nontraditional sources, documentation and communicating progress are necessary and take more time, and decisionmaking transparency is essential. Knowledge, shared priorities and direction, optimism and grace are helpful but sometimes conflicts are deeply embedded in culture, economy, or social values that project biologists and working groups are not well-equipped to address. In addressing complex problems, the conservation actions could be to develop a diverse community-based working group, apply structured decision-making, resolve conflict, or build a coalition before defining new or further action on the ground.

## Promote scientific integrity and transparency

"a river can't be known, … the river is not a thing, in the way a Saturn V rocket engine is a thing. (The river) is an expression of biological life, in dynamic relation to everything around it -the salmon within, the violet-green swallow swooping its surface, alder twigs floating in its current, a mountain lion sipping its bank water, the configurations of basalt that break its flow … this suggests a horizon rather than a boundary for knowing, …." -Barry Lopez (2001) Sustainable fish and wildlife rely on habitat and ecosystem health, some of which we can see, study, and understand and some of which will always be a mystery … a horizon.
And yet, conservation action must be based on what we can know even if that knowledge is incomplete so that we act before habitats and the species that depend on them are unrecoverable or lost. The best information we have is supported by scientific integrity, well-applied science -policy interface, guidance from communities of practice, monitoring and adaptation, and documentation.
Science provides critical insights for conservation decision-making, offering data, models, and projections that help assess risk, evaluate options and inform conservation. Actions (decisions) in response to scientific findings ultimately depend on the ability to apply those findings and may include assessments of risk tolerance, opportunity, policy, and cultural values. Science informs but does not prescribe action; data, information, models, and reports state what we think we know, can help us evaluate and propose next steps, and identify uncertainty. That information can also be used to test potential consequences of action or inaction. A final decision may also depend on weighing potential benefits and trade-offs, and it's important that the decision-making process and outcomes are transparent.
WDFW is committed to implementing a robust science-policy interface. The science-policy interface (SPI) includes the social processes between scientists and decision-makers (defined broadly), and is intended to facilitate productive exchanges, joint construction of knowledge, and transparent and inclusive natural resources decision-making. 
30
 The most important attributes of an effective SPI include saliency, credibility, and legitimacy: 31
• Salience (or relevance) reflects the ability of the SPI to be responsive to issues on which decision-makers focus and over which they have some control.
• Credibility reflects the believability of jointly constructed knowledge to interested parties, that is, the extent to which affected participants perceive SPI products (facts, theories, ideas, models, and scenarios) as valid.
• Legitimacy reflects perceived fairness, as measured by how well the SPI has considered stakeholders' divergent values and beliefs, and the degree to which the SPI is unbiased in its conduct and fair in its treatment of opposing views and interests.
The policy states:
Scientific integrity is the adherence to professional practices, ethical behavior and the principles of honesty and objectivity when conducting, managing, using the results of and communicating about science and scientific activities. Inclusivity, transparency and protection from inappropriate influence are hallmarks of scientific integrity. 
33
 Scientific integrity is a model of behavior, and a series of practices grounded in ethical principles and professional standards. It focuses on how science is conducted and communicated, ensuring that the process and findings are credible, transparent, and unbiased.
As a model of behavior, scientific integrity means that individual scientists, institutions, and policymakers uphold these principles in how they gather, analyze, and present information, while fostering an environment where trust in science is built through ethical conduct.
The SWAP provides well-vetted information from expertise, references, resources, plans, reports, and data to identify, scope, inspire, and implement conservation actions. These actions are crafted by a network of ecologists, biologists, peers, and communities. Being transparent about the information considered and why helps maintain trust and accountability. When conservation action decision-makers clearly communicate the scientific basis for actions, uncertainties they face, and influences and constraints on applying the information, this can demonstrate that management decisions are scientifically robust and aligned with policy priorities.

## Expand and continue engagement with purpose

Conservation doesn't happen without people. Durable and effective conservation solutions need people's support and participation. 
34
 Gaining social support begins with an effort and willingness to understand a place including its politics, culture, patterns of human use, and local values, not just the ecological foundations of what we need to do for species, habitats, and systems. Engaging people effectively in conservation can
• identify multiple perspectives, potential partners, and affected stakeholders;
• reveal information, opportunities, barriers, and potential sources of conflict;
• improve equitable access to resources (sites, staff, funds) and outcomes;
• create shared objectives, acquire consent, and continue communication;
• empower stakeholders to identify and be responsible for the outcomes;
• share the success and accomplishment;
33 National Science Foundation 2024 
34
 AAAS N.d., Bassler et al. 2008, Sterling et al. 2017, Conservation Measures Partnership 2025 • improve accountability for decisions and actions; and
• influence longer-term funding to maintain progress and apply lessons learned.
The most durable conservation solutions include human social and cultural values alongside ecological function and species/habitat restoration and conservation. A broad spectrum of western scientific, indigenous, and local ecological knowledges can be helpful to define a future vision and achieve desired outcomes.
The SWAP lays out conservation actions that depend on people to make good choices: where ecological knowledge meets peers, experts, governments, organizations, and regional and local communities at the table. The perfect recipe for conservation success does not exist in science and information alone. 
35
 It is important for people to be able to see the relationship of proposed conservation to their work, lives, and values for better conservation outcomes.
Champions are important catalysts in conservation action but cannot sustain an effort alone. People all over Washington need to believe in the work and support it, explore participation based on their existing circumstances and experiences-what interests them, what and who they know, who is working on it now, what information is available and vetted, who are the potential partners, and how could social or cultural issues they're already interested in be connected to the conservation problem or action? Exploring connections among people is a fundamental part of planning and implementing a conservation action and can lead to more effective campaigns, products, partnerships, and outcomes. Project planners can ask, "Who can help? Who needs to know? Who might be affected and what do they care about? What other questions should I ask? Who makes the decisions? What will we do if there is conflict?" And, because conservation success takes time and the people change across the lifetime of a project, it's important to document partnerships, strategies, ideas, and progress from the beginning.
Washington is well-positioned to work at all scales: natural resources managers promote the importance of landscape and system conservation actions for resilient and sustainable species and habitats; communities are engaged in local and regional planning that includes care for the natural world and/or locally relevant conservation solutions; and Washington has a strong network of active conservation allies -Tribes, land trusts, hunters and anglers, resource agencies, wildlife watchers, outdoor enthusiasts, conservation organizations, community organizers, and more. Several initiatives such as the Washington Shrubsteppe Restoration and Resiliency Initiative, Pacific Northwest Coast Landscape Conservation Design, and Yakima Basin Integrated Plan are leading the way with multiple partners and at many scales, only possible because of the engagement of people. The potential to share interests, access information and resources, and scale conservation action is unprecedented.
Many of Washington's natural resources-related agencies -local, state, and federal -have missions and/or goals that are supportive of species and habitat conservation and human enjoyment and use of natural resources. When humans' alteration of the system could put a species or habitat at risk, action should be taken to avoid, minimize, or compensate for the harm. Humans are part of the ecosystem, and ecosystem-based management approaches seeks to ensure those impacts are not grossly exploitative or detrimental to the ability of the system to sustain life and meet the needs of its human and non-human inhabitants. Care should be taken that historically underrepresented or marginalized communities do not have inequitable access to the benefits or inequitable share of the burden of environmental harms. This SWAP's revision engagement is just the start (Engagement section). The Coordinator and WDFW leadership provided briefings (webinar-delivered presentations and discussion opportunities), materials to review and comment opportunities, and surveys from December 2023 through November 2025.
Through more engagement, WDFW received more participation in the Plan's scoping and development and wants to carry that interest into implementation, reporting progress, and plan updates before the next required major revision in 2035. Engagement with and influence of many conservation partners and groups is evident throughout the conservation actions presented in the SWAP, not just in the outreach events conducted during the revision. See also Compass Point: Monitor, Learn, Adapt, and Communicate and Reporting Progress and Update Opportunities sections.

## Prioritize actions that support resilient, connected, and healthy systems for people and wildlife

Effective natural resource management in Washington depends on sustaining the capacity of ecosystems, species, and communities to adjust to changing conditions. The SWAP integrates three linked themes: resilience, connectivity, and OneHealth. These themes guide actions that maintain ecological processes, reduce risk, and support ecosystems, species, and communities as environmental conditions continue to change.

## Resilience

In ecology, resilience is the capacity of a system to absorb disturbance, reorganize, and continue to function. Washington ecosystems are already experiencing changes in air temperature, hydrology, and disturbance regimes. Air and water temperatures are rising, droughts are occurring more frequently and lasting longer, snowpack is declining with earlier melt, and extreme precipitation events and flooding are becoming more common. Hotter, drier summers will increase the potential for large wildfires in Washington's forests. These drivers of change will alter hydrology, vegetation patterns, habitat structure, shorelines, and species ranges, creating new pressures on both wildlife and the people who depend on natural resources.
Building resilience in Washington's natural systems focuses on enhancing their capacity to adjust and sustain ecological functions under changing conditions, rather than attempting to reestablish historical states. The SWAP applies resilience principles through conservation actions and strategies that sustain key ecological processes and promote adaptation. Examples include maintaining riparian vegetation to shade streams and moderate stream temperatures, restoring floodplains to absorb peak flows, managing forest structure to increase resilience to wildfire, and identifying and protecting refugia that remain suitable for species during extreme events.
Current adaptation science shows that conservation management actions that restore or protect ecological processes can directly support habitat and species resilience. Actions that improve hydrologic connectivity, reestablish natural disturbance regimes, increase structural complexity, and reduce chronic stressors strengthen the mechanisms that allow ecosystems to adjust as conditions change. These process-based strategies are widely identified in adaptation guidance as among the most robust under future conditions because they focus on ecological function rather than attempting to recreate historical states that may no longer be viable. This does not mean that all conservation actions are resilient by default. It means that work grounded in ecological processes is more likely to sustain its effectiveness as climate conditions shift. The accelerating rate of change underscores the need to advance these strategies at greater pace and scale so ecosystems and species are better positioned to absorb disturbance, reorganize, and continue to function in a warming climate.
Adaptation and resiliency tools and practices -assessing vulnerability how best to respond, how to monitor impacts and responses -are evolving. 
36
 When conservation project managers build in monitoring and a nimble process to understand that information, conservation practices can adapt to new information. This may require rethinking tools and strategies that were built on the assumption of stable conditions and systems, shifting project work toward more dynamic, process-based adaptive approaches to accommodate change and still reach conservation objectives and goals.

## Connectivity among habitats and systems

Ecological connectivity is a broad term for the movement of species and flow/function of natural processes without barriers, and it has profound impacts on biodiversity. 
37
 Habitat -lands and watersconnectivity is what many people think of because it's what we can most readily see, influence, and measure. Habitat connectivity provides fish and wildlife the opportunity to move throughout the landscape for seasonal movement, like elk moving from winter to summer range or salmon moving between ocean and river habitats. Habitat connectivity is also important for daily movement and for less mobile species, following foods, colonizing new areas, finding mates, and being able to move in response to changes such as urban expansion or ecosystem shifts due to wildfire, drought, or sea level rise. Landscape to local connectivity is also essential for plant communities, habitats, and ecosystem natural functions including plant gene flow, pollination, and seed dispersal. Connectivity can promote population resilience, recolonization, and reestablishment after disturbances. It is vital for surface and groundwater functions, quality, and quantity, and supports crucial riparian and floodplain functions.
Connectivity also sustains species important to cultural traditions like hunting and gathering.
Washington's conservation communities have been working on the crucial issue of habitat connectivity for decades: information gathering, complex analyses and ecological modeling, and the development of field-tested best practices. This work is operating at species, ecoregion, state and transboundary scales.
This dedication is fueled by urgency to respond to major, immediate threats such as large-scale habitat loss, increased disease transmission, and devastating wildfire events. Ultimately, this information and expertise are used to strategically inform information delivery and future decisions in land use planning, ecological restoration projects, and long-term conservation priority planning.
Recently, WDFW and WSDOT coordinated a significant review of habitat connectivity data, convened experts in ecology and connectivity, and sought input from Tribes and land use planners to develop and publish the 2025 the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan (WAHCAP) and companion spatial data. 
38
 The WAHCAP identifies priority locations for road crossing structures, maps landscape connectivity for species, ecosystems, and climate connectivity, and provides connectivity conservation implementation strategies. Because the conservation recommendations cross many jurisdictions, include land and water management types, and are influenced by local support, progress relies heavily on collaborative, voluntary action. Additionally, the WAHCAP recommends including local and finerscale connectivity resources from long-standing well-vetted sources when planning and taking conservation action; some examples of those sources include the 

## One Health

The term "One Health" emerged around 2009 and gained more recognition in Washington state between 2016 to 2019 with a re-energized One Health Collaborative: Washington Department of Health, Washington State Department of Agriculture, Washington State Department of Fish and Wildlife, and the U.S. Department of Agriculture -Animal and Plant Inspection Service. One Health is a cooperative effort across disciplines working locally, nationally and globally to improve the health of our shared ecosystems: humans, animals, plants, and our environment (Figure 
2
). Using a One Health framework can establish an early warning system and apply proactive actions to protect the health and resiliency of people and wildlife when we don't yet understand cumulative effects and enable people to more readily respond to horizon issues. This framework helps us identify and view conservation actions that deliver multi-sector benefits, expand constituencies (conservation and human environment advocates), and build stronger legislative and funding support through those relationships. Such a framework includes prevention, preparedness, surveillance, and coordinated outbreak investigation(s), response(s), and recovery efforts. Including this framework in the lays the groundwork for a One Health approach to help ensure healthy, resilient, future-ready wildlife populations in Washington.
Example. Recent work demonstrates that eelgrass meadows reduce pathogenic bacteria transfer from land into the nearshore and into filter-feeding bivalves. 
40
 Protection and restoration of seagrass meadows benefits humans that forage for and eat shellfish and enjoy coastal shoreline environments. This is how One Health framing can bring together human health experts, marine conservation advocates, and shellfish growers into a coalition that supports eelgrass protection and restoration.
Within this One Health Framework, a Determinants of Health model can be adapted to fish and wildlife health, with the goal of robust species' populations with the capacity to satisfy daily needs of survival and adapt to or cope with a changing environment -in other words, resiliency. 
41
 This approach recognizes the array of factors that influence healthy populations and can be used to highlight key actions to address future health risks. 
40
 Dawkins et al. 2024 While often thought of as a management framework for diseases, three components of OneHealth are most relevant in plant, fish, and wildlife conservation: ecosystem services 
42
 , diseases that affect wild species, and invasive species. The OneHealth approach recognizes the interconnection among people, animals, plants, and their shared environment. This section is only a brief discussion of the topic and then current issues will be related to species and habitats in relevant geographies.

## Nature's contributions to people

Making the connection between conservation action and benefits to people is a value that can engage more people in conservation action, beyond those directly interested in a particular species or habitat. These connections among fish and wildlife sustainability, environmental health, natural systems function, and human well-being are known as ecosystem services. Using this framework in conservation project development and reporting helps people assess and communicate how natural resources deliver social, cultural, and economic value. 
43
 It is important to understand that this valuation is not to define only an economic value of nature in our lives, but rather helps us describe and measure the degree to which humans are dependent on nature for their health and wellbeing. 
44
 Care should also be taken to consult directly with communities, especially those considered overburdened, to consider their perspectives and acknowledge their needs and contributions on conservation projects, policies, and decisions affecting ecosystem services. In project development, implementation, and reporting, note the scope of community consultation. 
45
 "The Millennium Ecosystem Assessment set out to assess the consequences of ecosystem change for human well-being and to establish the scientific basis for actions needed to enhance the conservation and sustainable use of ecosystems and their contributions to human well-being. Biological diversity plays a critical role in underpinning ecosystem services."
Considering ecosystem services early in conservation project planning helps identify which activities may provide direct and meaningful benefits to communities and engage people in the work.

## Plant, fish, and wildlife diseases

Diseases can be very impactful to wildlife populations and vegetation communities, affect biodiversity and ecosystem stability, and can move in many directions across wild species, humans, and domestic livestock. A real-world example is the ongoing H5N1 highly pathogenic avian influenza virus outbreak in wildlife not only here in Washington, but nationally and internationally where avian influenza has spread from wild birds to wild mammals and to poultry, cattle, and humans. 
48
 Wherever or however wildlife diseases are initiated -through wild, domestic, or human vectors -we must invest proactively in monitoring and responding to wildlife diseases and coordinate efforts between wildlife / biodiversity conservation, human health and domestic animal health sectors. In concert with environmental stressors such as drought, fire, flooding, extreme temperatures, habitat disconnection, and increased 
46
 "The Millennium Ecosystem Assessment assesses the consequences of ecosystem change for human well-being and the scientific basis for action needed to enhance the conservation and sustainable use of those systems and their contribution to human well-being (such as clean water, food, forest products, flood control, and natural resources) and the options to restore, conserve or enhance the sustainable use of ecosystems." millenniumassessment.org/en/About.html 47 Chaplin-Kramer et al. 2025 global mobility of people and animals and animal products, diseases are likely to increase in prevalence posing new and unknow threats to Washington's wildlife, humans, and ecosystems.
White-nose syndrome in bats; avian influenza or 'bird flu'; chronic wasting disease of deer, elk, or moose; elk hoof disease; snake fungal disease; eelgrass wasting disease; and wild fish diseases are just a few examples of species and system sustainability-disrupting diseases known in 
Washington. 49
 With new and emerging diseases on our horizon, conservation includes tracking disease pathways and movement outside of our borders, monitoring to detect disease emergence in Washington quickly, preparedness in the event we have a detection, scoping research and being ready to respond, and including public action in our responses. It is increasingly important to call attention to disease horizon issues that could likely threaten ecosystems in the future.

## Non-native invasive species

Non-native species are defined as invasive when they cause harm to the environment, economy, or human health. They generally require management by people to be prepared and prevent, detect, monitor, eradicate, or manage the invasive species. These species often compete with or disrupt native species and can also alter a habitat or ecosystem and hinder its ability to recover from disturbance. 
50
 This can also cause disproportionate harm to other members of an ecological community. A good example of this is the pervasive introduced cheat grass in eastern Washington which not only can replace native grasses in disturbed areas but can also amplify ignition and effects of wildfire, burning hotter and more destructively.
Like fish and wildlife diseases, conservation actions include tracking and surveillance, detection and monitoring, preparedness planning, and response that include public action. Actions that preserve habitat function and support habitat protection and recovery are a first line of defense in uninvaded systems. When faced with invasive species, we may find that bolstering the underlying ecosystem is not sufficient for species and habitat protection, so one-time or ongoing well-monitored intervention could be needed to support a system's ability to recover. For example, noxious weed control with mechanical or chemical means may be an important near-term or ongoing intervention with appropriately timed seed and rootstock restoration and reintroductions while native plant and animal populations recover.
Early detection and response are key to avoiding, minimizing, or containing an ongoing managementdependent effort.

## Facilitate effective voluntary conservation action

The SWAP is not regulatory. It can be informative for management, guidance, policies, and regulations, but the plan relies on people taking the information and recommendations from the SWAP and choosing to apply them. The actions in the SWAP will point to vetted resources and information to support the conservation decisions of formal working groups, agencies, conservation partners, 
49
 WDFW 2025b, USGS 2025 government jurisdictions including Tribes, interested communities, and inspired landowners and land trusts. The SWAP provides ideas for engagement and outreach, partnerships, and private lands stewardship to influence and contribute to habitat connectivity, climate resilience, species and habitat health, sustainability and recovery. Voluntary action is powerful because conservation solutions are often more durable when people are committed to the desired outcomes, and in some circumstances incorporating conservation action into requirements and regulations can help ensure we avoid unintended consequences and tradeoffs for species and their habitats.
To support this, the SWAP makes connection with WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species 51 which provides accepted science and tools for land use planning and proactive conservation considerations in project development. Additionally, non-governmental organizations in Washington state are wellsupported in their service territories by folks who care about conservation in their communities. They are informed and motivated to engage private landowners and community partners in strategic land and water conservation. Over 140 non-governmental conservation organizations led and supported by people who live and work in Washington state were invited to participate in the SWAP revision (Engagement section). They bring crucial local and regional knowledge and scalable networking to conservation in Washington state, and the SWAP can highlight and deliver information for these networks through conservation actions in specific ecoregions.

## Document and report progress as an important part of success

All conservation actions are constrained. Human capacity, funding, technical knowledge, social or cultural understanding or support, tools or practices, and time are a few examples of limiting resources that require project teams to pause and/or adapt. The complex solutions we need for long-term conservation durability span years (e.g., time representing several generations of the target species or established and stable habitats) and results do not often come in available or consecutive funding cycles. Governments include a public service duty and non-government/community partners are accountable to their supporters to prudently use those resources. Documenting and communicating progress, effectiveness, failure, and change are important steps in that responsibility to make good choices that address constraints and demonstrate commitment to a successful result. Another important example of cross-jurisdiction conservation and management is the Columbia River.

## Value inter-governmental conservation opportunities at all scales

Fed by watersheds originating in Nevada, Wyoming, Idaho, Montana, Oregon, Washington, British Columbia and Alberta (Canada), the Columbia River (Figure 
3
) is of state, Tribal, national, and international commercial, recreational, and natural importance as the largest river flowing into the Pacific Ocean from North America. 
54
 The Columbia River Gorge is the largest national scenic area in the United States. 55 Washington stewards important habitats for migratory species -birds, whales, fish -that travel to and from central and south America, along western states' coasts, Canada, and Alaska. By nature of our location, relationships, and the species that rely on Washington for part of their lifecycle, Washington state is a contributor or party to many international fish and wildlife conservation agreements, formal partnerships, treaty responsibilities, and organized conservation efforts, including, but not limited to:
• US Department of State bilateral cooperation on fisheries 

## Monitor, Learn, Adapt, and Communicate

Conservation implementation is guided by monitoring that help project teams make smart changes in light of new information, changes that are beneficial to project effectiveness and progress toward a consistent stated/desired conservation outcome. Conservation progress takes time; while keeping an eye on the goal, progress may show that adjustments are needed before seeing success.
A cycle of "plan, do, check (monitor), and adjust (learn and adapt)" outlines the need and value to purposefully track the progress and results of actions and adjust assumptions and actions based on this learning. The SWAP promotes using an effectiveness framework, either the one developed specifically for SWAPs 56 or a system-specific one that documents progress and provides clear information a project team can use. Additionally, projects that implement SWAP conservation actions need to include reporting, outreach, and data-sharing with communities of practice (communicate). This enables partners to consider and adapt to new and documented information, and the success of our collective actions over time, contributing to the first Compass Point: "increase the pace and scale of biodiversity conservation."
See also Reporting Progress section.

## Summary: SWAP Changes 2015 -2025

SWAP 2015 remains a very good resource for species and vegetation community information and is referenced in this revision. Documenting changes from SWAP 2015 is a requirement for revised plan approval by USFWS. 
57
 This section is an overview of the changes to the Eight Required Elements content and other changes. Only required Element changes are tabulated for the USFWS RRT in Appendix A: RRT Roadmap, using the USFWS-provided template.

## Changes beyond the Elements

These changes are not directly related to the Eight Required Elements so are not included in Appendix A.

## Organizing and presenting information

These SWAP 2025 changes are responsive to many of the concerns expressed in a pre-revision survey 58 to make the plan easier to use by more people interested in Washington's biodiversity:
• SWAP 2025 is supported by vetted technical information, uses less technical language in the plan for readability, and points to more technical information for readers who want a deeper dive. A Glossary is provided for more technical terms.
• SWAP 2025 content for SGCN and HGCN is sorted by the nine ecoregions of Washington state (Ecoregions Maps and Tables section). SWAP 2015 (Table 
4
-4 in this section of that document) used ecoregions as a description feature for Ecological Systems of Concern and some, not all, of the SGCN distributions (species fact sheets, Appendix A in SWAP 2015); ecoregions were not an organizing structure.
• SWAP 2025 has a Statewide and Transboundary section that relates large-scale issues to prioritized conservation recommendations that are either in progress or need to start in the foreseeable future. SWAP 2015 also provided a state overview however "conservation problems" and "conservation strategies" were separated and more generalized.
• The body of SWAP 2025 elevates the HGCN and ecosystem information related to SGCN to provide more habitat conservation recommendations.
Elements of SWAP 2025 that USFWS defines as a "revision," we point to information that may evolve and stay current for project development and prioritization. This way, the SWAP retains more relevancy between revisions.
Large and detailed tables are provided as Excel spreadsheets online and linked in this document so that users have the ability to download, search, and filter information a little easier than the PDF tables in SWAP 2015. Following USFWS approval of the SWAP, more information will also be available on the WDFW SWAP website to assist Plan users with data visualization and access.

## SWAP database

WDFW created a SWAP 2025 data intake and stewardship structure for SGCN information: status, range, habitat association, threats, and actions. This structure is useful to
• collect consistent information on all SGCN,
• sort and organize that information for the plan, and • create easier workflows for status and plan updates.
The structure is based on an ArcGIS 'experience' platform with a dashboard display. Subject Matter Experts (SMEs) used the database and 'experience' to provide Element-required SGCN information: selection reasons and status information, distribution/range, habitat associations, and threats and actions. These will all be discussed in this document: SWAP Species: SGCN, SGCN Distribution, SGCN Habitat Associations, and Conservation Issues and Actions. WDFW intends to deliver SWAP information in a public-facing portal or web-service for searchable SWAP information, and the database provides structure for that. Because the database fields align with many other SWAPs nationally, the Washington SWAP database can more easily feed external public-facing web-based SWAP dashboards and databases that are promoted for all SWAPs. 
59
 In creating and using the database, we identified several issues to improve the data structures, stewardship, and delivery before the next major revision (SWAP Planning Recommendations section).

 Two new SWAP species categories were added: Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN), to signal significant data gaps in necessary species information; and vulnerable species concentrations including but not limited to some that are identified as WDFW Priority Species vulnerable concentrations.

## Conservation Targets

SWAP conservation targets are the species and habitats that benefit from recommended conservation actions in this plan to address issues that affect their persistence, recovery, and sustainability. Affected by habitat loss and lack of connectivity, system to site level degradation, disease and invasive species, and other specific issues, these are the focal species and habitats that need our attention the most in the foreseeable future.
"If everything is a priority, then nothing is a priority." -said pretty much everyone

## Overview

This document asks the conservation community, planners, agencies, and private land managers to apply their resources and implement recommended conservation actions in this plan. To trust that those efforts would be worth their engagement, it is important that the conservation targets are based on vetted data in trusted and institutional databases; published and peer-reviewed literature, assessments, synthesized information, and reports; and accessible materials so that the trail of breadcrumbs to these recommendations is more transparent and defensible.
This section provides
• criteria for the SWAP species and habitats;
• processes and resources used to select/identify those targets;
• links to detailed tables resulting from the processes; and
• relationship to the conservation actions and recommendations.
SWAP Conservation Target processes, resources, and frameworks were developed by several internal WDFW cross-Program (Wildlife, Fish, Habitat) technical teams; applied by SMEs in WDFW and NHP into the SWAP database; and, informed by data, citable information and literature, expert and peer networks, and additional supported information that was provided during engagement with Tribes, agencies, and the public.
The teams working at all stages of SWAP development valued transparency, repeatability, citable information, and institutional capture for consistent and durable information that could be referenced, queried, and updated more easily when new information is available.
During scoping, participants expressed that the difference between SWAP SGCN and HGCN and WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species was somewhat confusing. And, the addition of state listed plants and ecosystems to this SWAP may add complexity since plants are not PHS Priority Species but many of the listed ecosystems have a close relationship to PHS Priority Habitats.
With this revision, the agency teams coordinated to reduce confusion as much as possible in the time we had and in line with the purposes of each product:
• SWAP highlights species, habitat, and system conservation recommendations and partnerships, including data and planning needs; • PHS guides land use decisions, and many local jurisdictions adopt PHS whole or in part into comprehensive plans and regulations including Critical Area Ordinances, focused on existing / known species and habitat information to guide avoidance, minimization and mitigation during zoning, infrastructure planning, and other development; and, • NHP identifies rare and imperiled plants and ecosystems as state sensitive, threatened or endangered for consideration in habitat restoration and protection strategies to ensure persistence and representation in our state.
All three are based on data and assessed status information and are useful to communicate which species and habitats need our conservation attention.

## WDFW Priority Habitats and Species Program

The 
67
 There is overlap among SGCN and vulnerable concentrations. An example is Bocaccio rockfish which is a PHS Priority Species and an SGCN, and is contained within the Marine -Rockfish Concentrations. Both the species and the concentration are SWAP conservation targets in marine environments.

## Washington Natural Heritage Program

A key state agency vetted and institutional source of information for this SWAP is Washington's Natural Heritage Program (NHP), managed and supported in the Department of Natural Resources. The NHP leads our state in rare plant, ecosystem, and state designated natural area conservation, and maintains occurrence and distribution data for SGCN plants and the state's ecosystems in its Biotics database. This version of the SWAP includes imperiled plants as SGCN for the first time. The NHP provided significant technical support and knowledge to help WDFW frame HGCN and the habitat associations data framework for SGCN. Plants are partially included in the WDFW SWAP database for their SGCN assignment; however NHP is the authoritative source for any updated plant occurrence and status information that could be more current than that found in the SWAP database.
The SWAP includes all Washington threatened and endangered plant species tracked by NHP as SGCN and all of the ecosystems that NHP has identified as threatened or endangered as HGCN. Many of these overlap with PHS Priority Habitats and that relationship is discussed in the SWAP Habitats: HGCN section. PHS does not include listed plants as Priority Species.
More information about how PHS and NHP products are used in the SWAP is distributed throughout the plan.

## Regulatory Note

PHS products influence local and county regulation by providing best available science for entities to use in Growth Management Act and Shoreline Management Act implementation, but SWAP lists and recommendations, and NHP lists are not regulatory. There are no laws or regulations stating that project development, implementation, or maintenance must consider these sources of information, although these sources are often consulted and considered -voluntarily -in management processes and decisions. Including a species or habitat in the SWAP does not automatically influence, change, or add to PHS, and the SWAP does not change PHS criteria. PHS Priority Species and Habitats have a defined set of criteria and any additions would require a full PHS vetting process as they influence land use decisions. SWAP is not a listing or status determination process for species. The intention in this SWAP is to better align with the PHS and NHP vetted and supported lists, while also communicating conservation that could help prevent the need to list species that are not yet imperiled (an original purpose of SWAPs across the nation is to "keep common species common").

## SWAP Species

Required Element 1. Information on the distribution and abundance of species of wildlife, including low and declining populations as the State fish and wildlife agency deems appropriate, that are indicative of the diversity and health of the State's wildlife.
Meeting this Element in this section: defining Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) criteria and processes, including finer details in SGCN section; and linked 

## First Filters

Washington state is home to thousands of species, from the tiniest mites to the mightiest whales.
A WDFW technical team convened to review the 2015 SGCN process, SWAP best practices since 2015, and available information sources that could be used to refine the universe of Washington species into a reasonable list of SWAP species for the 2025 revision.
Some broad first filters emerged:
• plants should be included for a more complete picture of the state's conservation needs (i.e., rare plants are identified by NHP and some documented invertebrates' and pollinators' declines are related to declining plant populations); • only fully described plant and animal species and subspecies native to Washington are eligible to be SWAP species (without a reliable taxonomic understanding, it's difficult to assess status and the species' life history needs, so it is also difficult to define meaningful conservation actions); • non-native species, peripheral species for which management actions in Washington would not likely affect the conservation status, or species extirpated in Washington without a current recovery community of practice or potential would not be considered; • species that do not live in Washington state year-around (e.g., migratory birds, Monarch butterflies) and depend on Washington state for some portion of their lifecycle should be eligible; • species that are extirpated in Washington with recovery potential 68 could be considered; • hunted, fished, or foraged species being stressed by a defined issue (such as disease or invasive species) could be considered separate and apart from any harvest opportunity or management decision-process 69 ; and, • well-supported reasons should be transparent for adding a species to the SWAP. These broad considerations were applied to a very large universe of possible species including those that were SGCN in the 2015 SWAP, using existing data sources. Information from the filter process and additional applied criteria (next section: Three SWAP Species Categories) and captured in a new SWAP species database that will be stewarded by WDFW.
68 Woodland Caribou is an example of a Washington extirpated species that is eligible. Although documented extirpated in Washington state, an international and multi-state recovery effort, mostly focused in Canada, is active. WDFW continues to participate in those conversations to track the recovery progress and collaborate to support the effort if or when relevant for Washington. 69 WDFW Commission presentation December 14, 2023 wdfw.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2023-12/wildlifecommittee-sgcn-briefing-paper.pdf Hunted, Fished, and Foraged Species may be SGCN.

## Plant SWAP Species

NHP was consulted for the plants to include in the SWAP. The state's well-vetted threatened and endangered plants list 
70
 is supported by the robust NatureServe conservation status assessment and ranking system, fits the other broad considerations, and SGCN-required Element information (distribution, habitat associations, threats and actions) could be pulled out of existing resources. Plant state threatened and endangered labels (status) are based on the global and state conservation status ranks.

## Animal SWAP Species

For animals (vertebrates and invertebrates), it was more complex. WDFW started with the agency's authoritative taxonomy and status database ("Taxo") of nearly 2,750 species and subspecies. Taxo provides WDFW a way to institutionally track, deliver and manage a rule-based, standardized, internal, authoritative source for valid common and scientific names, classification, and various state and federal statuses resulting from vetted processes (e.g., federal candidate, threatened, or endangered; state sensitive, threatened, or endangered; PHS Priority Species; and NatureServe conservation status ranks for animals that are also maintained by NHP -see Note on NatureServe Conservation Status).
Taxo is a database that identifies each species and subspecies as a record with a unique identifying number (TaxoID). The TaxoID field in that database is also used in other WDFW institutional databases to build relationships among survey, monitoring, models, and other information.
Example. Cascade torrent salamander TaxoID is 5086. This number stays the same, even if common or scientific names, other classification, or status for this species changes. This enables historical and current observation and status information to be related to the TaxoID and tracked over time, even if those other things change.
To view, sort, consider, and capture animal species-related SWAP information for this revision, WDFW Wildlife Science created an ArcGIS-based data entry tool (an 'experience') based on Taxo with four units to support meeting the SGCN-specific Elements: status, distribution/range, habitat associations, threats and conservation actions.
The TaxoID for each species is the shared field across all units of the tool and resulting database. This will be useful beyond this revision for future updates. The ArcGIS tool provided SMEs the ability to
• choose a species to review, see current status, and view some institutionally available aggregated observation data and information; • select a SWAP species label based on criteria (Element 1: Identify SGCN);
• pick watersheds to document the species range of conservation opportunity (Element 1: SGCN distribution); • pick habitat associations for each species (Element 2: SGCN habitats); and
• identify important contemporary threats and actions (Element 3: Threats and Element 4: Actions).
Units provided fields for SMEs to document citable reasons for their selections.
The benefit of the tool is that we now have a SWAP database that
• is tied to the required Elements for SGCN and other definitions for SWAP species considerations • relates to Taxo for current nomenclature and status as those changes are made (e.g., updated scientific names, new species, federal or state listing status, NHP NatureServe conservation status ranks) • holds the history of reasons between revisions • uses consistent language across years (whether updating every ten years as required or more frequently) • can enable future online display • can be related to other national SWAP queries and database displays, and • will allow for an easier defensible SWAP update next time.
Deploying this new tool identified some necessary improvements in ease and consistency of use, data sources to support the database use, and displays, which will be worth the effort (SWAP Planning Recommendations) to allow WDFW to more iteratively, transparently, and consistently capture and present SWAP species-specific information.

## Three SWAP Species Categories

Even with the first set of filters and considerations applied there was still a very large pool, hundreds of species, more than would be reasonable to consider a priority in the next ten years (plan period) or for which meaningful conservation actions could be identified.
The WDFW technical teams developed additional criteria to focus that pool into three types of SWAP Species 71 that meet the USFWS and AFWA 2017 SWAP Revision Guidance and Eight Required Elements to " … identify and be focused on the 'species in greatest need of conservation' and address the 'full array of wildlife' and wildlife-related issues":
• Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN),
• Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN), and • vulnerable concentrations. 
71
 SWG and C-SWG Eligibility for SWAP Species: SWAP-defined conservation actions for SGCN are eligible. SGIN are eligible for SWG and C-SWG funding if the defined work is to determine whether the species should be an SGCN. Vulnerable concentrations are eligible for SWG if one of the species is an SGCN and the conservation actions are related to the SGCN AND the vulnerable concentration.
The next three sections provide definitions, additional criteria, and information sources used to identify SGCN (required), Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN), and vulnerable concentrations.
Tables of species and supporting information resulting from these applied criteria are in each section.

## SGCN

SGCN are required in the SWAP. Additional criteria are outlined in this section, and the tables of selected SGCN at the end of this section meets the first part of Element 

## Criteria and Process

Species Abundance. No species has a "sum total count of individuals" in Washington state. Surveys, monitoring, research, modeling, and synthesis inform our understanding of species status (e.g., stable, declining, at-risk, extirpated, extinct). The comments, defined statuses based on vetted assessments (e.g., federal or state 'listing' processes), and the citations in the SGCN table (linked at the end of this section) represent our best understanding of abundance in a consistent way across all of the species that were considered eligible for the SWAP.
SGCN criteria were revised from 2015 to be more repeatable. Two primary criteria were defined for species that are eligible to be SGCN: future
foot_5
 due to documented threats, supported by evidence: research, monitoring, inventory, survey, modeling, assessment, synthesis, or expert observation [statement of a qualified scientific expert based on their best professional judgment, experience in the relevant scientific discipline, and a systematic vulnerability assessment] with documentation and/or citation that WDFW can reference in the plan.
For animals, several sensitivities arose as we applied the criteria:
• 'Enough' information. To be an SGCN, the additional required elements must be metinformation on distribution and abundance (some understanding of status), habitats the species relies on, and threats and actions. Some of the expert nominations did not have enough information to meet the SGCN bar so were considered for the SGIN category.
• Vulnerability and natural movement. WDFW incorporated vulnerability assessments into Chapter 5 of the 2015 SWAP considering sensitivity, exposure, and adaptive capacity across most SGCN and related habitats. 
75
 These assessments highlighted species at greatest risk due to factors such as physiological limitations, habitat specialization, and low dispersal ability. From this work, WDFW developed resilience fact sheets for many habitat types (e.g., shrubsteppe, riparian, eastside and westside forests) and species; identified resilience risks and opportunities in agency work 
76
 ; collaborated with Washington Department of Ecology on a state-level resilience strategy 77 ; and continues to grow a responsive and useful resilience body of work (e.g., culvert designs, stream suitability modeling, wildfire response and post-fire restoration information). Since 2019, WA DNR has conducted and published several landscape, ecosystem, and species vulnerability and resilience resources. 
78
 The SWAP 2015 analyses and a great deal of new information were considered by SMEs and the conservation community in the 2025 SWAP to identify SGCN, important habitats, landscape-level conservation needs, and actions for their conservation.
Some species that are native in adjacent states but not thought to be native and regularly occurring in Washington could be considered for future SGCN if their regular presence in Washington would be due to the influence of weather patterns, phenology shifts, and vegetation changes that influence habitat suitability. Species that can move in response to these pressures are likely to move and, with habitat connectivity, may move into Washington state. However, WDFW did not have time or capacity during this revision process to frame or explore how a conservation agency would define "native wildlife" in that circumstance, what priorities could be affected, or how adjacent states' fish and wildlife agencies would like to collaborate in that circumstance. This question will require future framing and the capacity to explore expert opinion, science, and current conservation practice. So, no species were identified as SGCN that meet this consideration, but with better framing on the criterion, it could be an option for future SWAP updates.
• Extirpated with Recovery Potential. A few species are officially considered extirpated in Washington, but still are conservation targets for USFWS, adjacent states, and/or other governments with active recovery efforts. Woodland caribou and Oregon silverspot butterfly are good examples of this consideration.
• PHS Priority Species without population level concerns. PHS designates some native and nonnative fish and wildlife species of recreational, commercial, and tribal ceremonial and subsistence purposes. Non-native species are not eligible to be SGCN, and several other PHS Priority Species are not included as SWAP species because there is not a population level conservation issue or emerging concern (e.g., moose, pink salmon).
• Uncommon/Peripheral. In the SWAP, this consideration means that the species population is not likely to be influenced by management in our state; the species is not detected here regularly. These are usually birds that may be on the state species lists, have records in eBird, or are detected here periodically as an accidental or vagrant. In this sense, "uncommon" does not mean rare. It just means we don't detect them in Washington very often if at all, and we would not base management decisions on a species that isn't dependent on Washington's habitats. Although these species are not considered for the SGCN label, data on them is always welcome.
It is important to document uncommon and peripheral occurrences to better understand longterm shifts and any future influences on patterns of species collapse, range contraction or shifts, historical importance in recovery planning, and genetic, evolutionary, and biodiversity considerations. 
79
 Eleven species met the PHS native species criteria and were identified as uncommonly or infrequently occurring in The interactions among these four species and salmon recovery are very complex site-and system-specific conservation and management balance considerations. These species are not considered SGCN but will be considered in vulnerable concentrations and HGCN conservation benefits.
• Hunted, Fished and Foraged SGCN. See WDFW Commission Wildlife Committee briefing paper, December 14, 2023. Commission documents on the WDFW website are available for more than ten years. Several PHS priority species that are hunted or fished were selected as SGCN. A few PHS native species that are also hunted were found to not have vulnerabilities at the population level so are not SGCN (e.g., moose, dusky grouse, sooty grouse).
• Subpopulations. Divisions smaller than populations, including subpopulation, evolutionarily significant unit (ESU), or distinct population segment (DPS) were identified by some SMEs as the focus unit for conservation. Range and habitat associations may reflect the entire population in Washington state, but subpopulations were highlighted in status, threats and actions where relevant. WDFW has identified a SWAP Planning Recommendation to improve the SWAP database: creating structure for subpopulations as a conservation unit (i.e., identify a subset of the population range) that can retain relationship to the parent record (species) and be related to other institutional data sources (e.g., those that organize survey data, range, references, and other information). This could also improve priority conservation project planning and reporting.
