83
 Including plant conservation also communicates a more complete approach to conservation of habitats and systems.
New SGCN in addition to plants, primarily invertebrates and fishes, were added due to new information from work done under the 2015 SWAP, earlier/broader engagement with peers and expert networks (extensively for invertebrates), improved understanding about impacts of extreme and changing environments on some species and their habitats, and new formal assessments leading to updated defined status.
Over 50 SGCN (species or subspecies) in 2015 were not selected as SGCN for 2025. It's difficult to be precise with that number because of taxonomic decisions (lumping, splitting, name changes). Additionally, species were not selected as SGCN because status improved (state or federally delisted), new distribution information helped determine the species was less in need of conservation in Washington, or they fit better in the new SGIN category. Some SGCN in the 2015 table were labeled as "SC" for "federal species of concern" which is not a federally recognized or defined status, so that designation is not a criterion for SWAP species in 2025.
Some SGCN are now 'grouped.' An example is bull trout and Dolly Varden -both are tagged as SGCN but WDFW manages these two species as an aggregate so from a conservation action perspective, they only 'count' as one. Also, many of the salmonid SGCN in the threats and SGCN actions (Conservation Issues and Actions section) as "Regional concern" or "Subpopulation concern."

## Prioritization within the SGCN Table

Some states prioritize within their SGCN tables. Washington does not so that the conservation community can adapt over time to new funding opportunities, find patterns in multi-species and habitat conservation to be more efficient and effective, support creative and engaged expertise that evolves over time, and feed state and regional working groups' focus areas as those develop. Conservation of SWAP species and habitats are the priority.

## NatureServe Conservation Status Ranks in SWAP

Many states have a "natural heritage program" and are part of the North American NatureServe network that stewards biodiversity data. Many states use this network as a common language for collecting, managing, and sharing species, site, and ecological system information with standard and rigorous methods across the network to define status of species and systems. This shared language has been very helpful to inform conservation at the site to landscape scale.
Washington's Natural Heritage Program (NHP) is managed and supported in the Department of Natural Resources and leads our state in rare plant, ecosystem, and state-designated natural area conservation. Washington Department of Fish and Wildlife leads data stewardship for fish and wildlife, and periodically contributes that information to the NHP database; however, that is not a regularly scheduled sync. Because of this, NatureServe conservation status ranks in Washington state are not the best indicator of status for fish and wildlife species.
NatureServe ranks are a SWAP species selection criterion because plants can be SGCN in this SWAP and, in cases where fish and wildlife species ranks reflect the best information, then the assessment behind the rank may be the best indicator of status. Fish and wildlife SMEs did not go through the NatureServe ranking for all SGCN or rely on those ranks first for fish and wildlife. They used the NatureServe rank assessment method if time allowed and they needed that supporting documentation.
The NatureServe rank SGCN criterion may include species that rank low globally (secure) and high for state (imperiled) (e.g., G5S2). In that case, SGCN list reviewers may flag these records for additional review.
Using the conservation status rank methodology, NatureServe assigns global conservation status ranks to each of Washington's plants. State conservation status ranks are developed assigned by WA DNR NHP using the same conservation status rank methodology. Together, the global and state conservation status ranks determine which species are considered State Endangered or State Threatened. These two categories of plants were included in the SWAP.
A SWAP Planning Recommendation is to improve the connectivity between WDFW animal status assessment and documentation and the NHP Biotics database to ensure that NatureServe animal information for Washington is more current and useful among NatureServe users. The only conservation action for SGIN is "8.1. Research and Monitoring: Basic Research and Status Monitoring" using the conservation action framework of the SWAP (Conservation Issues and Actions section). SGIN in the SWAP can guide external researchers and conservation organizations to generate data and information for conservation partners' and WDFW institutional systems that biologists and ecologists can trust and use. Distribution (range), habitat associations, and other threats and actions (the species-related SWAP Elements) are not provided for SGIN as most (if not all) of these species have extremely limited available information about their occurrences or locations.

## SGIN

SGIN can be considered for State Wildlife Grant funding to determine if they should be an SGCN in the future. If SGIN are surveyed or monitored and information is curated in a WDFW data system, the species could be considered using the SGCN criteria. For SWAP inclusion, the species-related Elements need to be defined.

## Vulnerable Concentrations

Many wildlife species concentrate or gather in high densities in relatively small areas for breeding, rearing young, or other purposes. Direct threats to individuals (e.g., disturbance) where wildlife concentrate can have a disproportionate impact on wildlife populations because a higher proportion of a species' population is represented in the group; such impacts are especially important to breeding and rearing concentrations which are essential to sustaining populations. Similarly, habitat loss or degradation in relatively small spatial areas where wildlife concentrate would have disproportionate impacts on affected species.
WDFW has long recognized the need to designate and protect vulnerable concentrations as critical Fish and Wildlife Habitat Conservation Areas through the PHS program and are now including them for the first time in the 2025 SWAP. In the SWAP, "vulnerable concentrations" refers to large groups of individuals, from one or more species, gathered in high densities (i.e., concentrate). This definition is consistent with the PHS Program's use of "vulnerable aggregation" and WDFW considers these terms interchangeable. Vulnerable concentrations may be identified by the group (e.g., shorebird concentrations) or the area in which they concentrate (e.g., breeding waterfowl concentration areas). Examples of vulnerable concentrations include heron rookeries, seabird colonies, marine mammal haulouts, shellfish beds, and fish spawning and rearing areas.
SWAP vulnerable concentrations include both single-species and multiple species concentrations designated by PHS, and one additional concentration identified during SWAP development (Table 
3
). Several vulnerable concentrations include SGCN, have a strong connection to one or more HGCN and are included as values for HGCN conservation (SWAP Habitats: HGCN section), and all vulnerable concentrations warrant conservation wherever they occur. The conservation of many single-SGCN concentrations (e.g., cliff swallow nesting colonies, western toad or toadlet breeding and dispersal concentrations, striped whipsnake winter concentrations) are not included in this list and are addressed by species-specific threats and actions.
Freshwater mussels was the group identified during SWAP development that is not a PHS vulnerable concentration. This group is one of the most imperiled species groups 85 with many of the individual species in significant decline and identified as SGCN. They play a crucial role in maintaining water quality, supporting food webs, and many have lifecycle relationships to fish and other aquatic species. Water availability, water quality, riparian health, and temperature refugia in aquatic habitats are important issues for mussel conservation. Xerces Society has identified key opportunities for freshwater mussel conservation in the Pacific Northwest. 
86
 Xerces provides a freshwater mussel atlas for western North America at the HUC 8 scale (one size larger than HUC10) which is useful albeit limited by data shared with Xerces; this is a good overview and could be paired with SGCN freshwater mussel-specific range HUC10s that were identified for this SWAP from WDFW occurrence data, literature, and other information for finer scales for conservation of this vulnerable concentration. Freshwater mussel beds conservation is included in HGCN Headwater Streams and Instream conservation, as well as these Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs: Riparian, Floodplains, and Wetlands; Indigenous First Foods Sustainability; and Water Quality and Quantity for Fish and Wildlife. Distribution for vulnerable concentrations that are designated by the PHS Program is available by county online as a Microsoft Excel file and through the PHS on the Web tool. Habitat associations vary widely as the concentrations use many types of habitats in their congregating periods or seasons.
Conservation actions for vulnerable concentrations are identified in related HGCN sections where that connection can be made, and many vulnerable concentrations are also mentioned in the Statewide and Transboundary Needs conservation recommendations.
For the SWAP database, we have identified a future need for a tag or field for species that fit in a vulnerable concentration so that information can be more easily searched and considered (SWAP Planning Recommendation). 

## Information, Processes, and Limitations

Watersheds are used in SWAP 2025 to define SGCN animal distribution as a coarse range. U.S. Geologic Survey (USGS) defines a national scale of nested water resources basins or watersheds called Hydrologic Unit Codes (HUCs, pronounced "hucks") from the National Watershed Boundary Dataset. The bigger HUC number means a smaller watershed (USGS standard). There are four levels: regions, sub-regions, accounting units, and cataloging units. HUC 2 is the largest scale -the entire state of Washington sits in the HUC2 Pacific Northwest Region (Figure 
4
). Nested within each HUC 2 are the progressively smaller HUC 8, HUC 10, and HUC12. The HUC 12 is the smallest unit of nested HUC sizes. 
87
 Washington's HUC 10 watersheds (Figure 
5
) were selected for SWAP 2025 SGCN distribution/range as a good scale to use with other SWAP data sets, compatible with other conservation efforts. These subdivisions are used in Washington in other conservation efforts: Salmon recovery is organized around watersheds using HUC 12s and other data to create watershed boundaries; 88 Water Resource Inventory Areas are fairly closely aligned with the larger HUC8 scale; and SWAP 2015 used both the HUC 10 and HUC 12 scale. 88 Lower Columbia River Subbasin boundaries for Salmon Recovery. This is an explanation how watersheds are used in a salmon recovery unit arcgis.com/home/item.html?id=d99611fd1afe4318b56530afbfcf5f1c. This SWAP presents all SGCN population level distributions as ranges consistently defined by HUC10 watersheds, a geography of conservation potential for each species. For each SGCN, this is the broadest geography within Washington where a species may/could be found. Actual occurrence is limited by accessible and suitable habitat in that selected watershed for any part of the life cycle. The selected watersheds represent species occurrence data and/or cited references that may include recovery or reintroduction plans. HUCs selected for a species indicate that the species may occur within that HUC, not that the species occurs throughout the entire HUC.
Through institutionally curated data, surveys, monitoring, synthesis, literature, research, and modeling, WDFW, WA DNR, and a broad network of conservation professionals and researchers have a good understanding where species are known. That said, not all areas are accessible for survey and resources are limited; and some species are surveyed more often than others due to limits in funding, capacity, expertise, or access. Each species has varying degrees of information and species may occur in places that have not been surveyed (or are not accessible to survey).
SGCN distribution is the HUC 10 range of a species based on what biologists, researchers, and other conservation practitioners can reference and their understanding of the species' habitat needs and distribution, representing a geography of conservation opportunity. This is related to the distribution of ecosystems that we used for SGCN habitat (Element 2), HGCN, statewide and transboundary conservation recommendations, and species occurrence data to inform conservation project planning. The conservation usefulness of HUC10s is limited for marine SGCN (see Marine, Island, and Shoreline Species in this section) but helpful for the terrestrial and freshwater aquatic SGCN to 'clip' their distribution by the nine ecoregions of Washington state (Ecoregions section) and larger watershed scales for other conservation efforts. This is a repeatable, consistent framework, not that different from SWAP 2015 which used HUC 10 and HUC 12 scales, well-supported by information, and meets the intention of Element 1.
SMEs reviewed available species occurrence information and other citable sources, and engaged professional networks and specialists.
Plant SMEs at WNHP reviewed plant occurrences and other resources:
• Online Data Explorer that provides known locations of rare plants • Species of Concern List that provides county, ecoregion, and managed areas distribution
foot_9
 , and • Online Field Guide to the Rare Plants of Washington | Department of Natural Resources which also provides range comments, a coarse scale map, and additional distribution information.
Washington NHP provides the best resource for plant SGCN by ecoregion and county (maps, descriptions) in the Online Field Guide to Rare Plants of Washington, based on the occurrence and distribution data for plants and ecosystems of Washington in its Biotics database. WNHP provided a list of HUC10s for each plant SGCN (SGCN Distribution Results section).
Animal SMEs used the SWAP ArcGIS 'experience (described in SWAP Database) to select or highlight HUC10 watersheds that could frame the broadest geography within Washington where a species is known to occur or could occur based on defensible information: recovery plans, reintroduction plans, habitat descriptions, literature, research data and models, occurrence or survey data in an institutional database from 1978 to present, and verifiable data in another trusted source, such as research grade iNaturalist or validated eBird data.
A selected HUC does not indicate that suitable habitat is widely or evenly distributed throughout the HUC. The area could contain lands and waters where species do not or cannot live, including 'matrix' through which the species travels in seasonal migration or dispersal.

## HUC selection criteria included

• species found in HUC, observation data available but not yet in an institutional database (species data may have been available from recent or current years, but not yet processed into the agency database) • species found in HUC, observation data is in an institutional source and/or a trusted source (e.g., verified research grade iNaturalist)
• species found in HUC, information from a trusted source (not necessarily observation data) is available in literature or by WDFW personal communication with an expert in the field (data has not been published, but the source is reputable and acknowledged as an expert in that species) • species may be found in HUC, proximal to known occurrences (many of these are similar or the same as those HUCs turned on as 'proximal' in SWAP 2015) • species may be found in HUC if documented recovery, reintroduction, or restoration actions may occur in the future
foot_10
 • species may be found in HUC (circumstances noted in comments; upon review and validation of the supporting information, such as suitable habitat known to occur, many of these may be more appropriately attributed to other reasons).
'Selected' or 'turned ON' HUCs represent:
• focus areas for identifying threats and actions;
• documented observations and information that the SME 'trusts' and which can be citable in the plan; • any part of the species life cycle important to species conservation; and • 'conservation potential' -species may occur through conservation action in the foreseeable future (e.g., a recovery plan includes action in this area, restoration is possible for the species and its habitats, landscape connectivity could enable species persistence or restoration) and feasible geography.
An example of the HUC10 watersheds selected for an SGCN in Figure 
6
 shows the results of SME work in the ArcGIS 'experience,' a WDFW internal tool for collecting consistent information for SGCN. These inputs are then reviewed and stored in a SWAP species database and shared in an external SWAP species data explorer on the SWAP website, and results are available in HUC 10s were not selected for historical range where there is no foreseeable potential to restore a functional population of the species. Recall that species considered extirpated in Washington with an active recovery community of practice could be selected as SGCN. A good example is woodland caribou (Figure 
7
) for which recovery efforts continue in adjacent Canada with potential to re-engage Washington, so HUCs are selected for this species in Washington. SME, regional biologists, and/or other experts may have conferred and determined that suitable habitat or species were no longer present for reasons that either permanently or long-term impacted the species potential to occur there (e.g., development, invasive species, catastrophic wildfire). Figure 
8
 shows an example screenshot from the 'experience' where a SME reviewed a HUC with observations, did not select the HUC, and provided a citation supporting that decision.

## Figure 8. Example HUC not selected with reason

Why select HUCs without current occurrence data in an institutional system? All selected HUCs are supported by information that can be referenced. Using only occurrence data may limit conservation potential (i.e., species may not have been surveyed in all potential places due to access, capacity, or funding limitations). For species that are already imperiled, it is important to communicate opportunities to recover the species in places where they don't currently exist and stabilize/improve populations where they currently occur. Proximal HUCs indicate that more certain information is needed in species-observation institutional systems to inform conservation opportunities, document change, and develop further actions. Project planning must consider institutional data and well-curated reference information to start with -essentially, a good project baseline from which to measure effectiveness of the conservation action.
Marine, Island, and Shoreline Species. The HUC10 dataset provides some large-scale marine-specific units (e.g., Port San Juan -Strait of Juan De Fuca HUC 1711002112 or Makah Bay -Pacific Ocean HUC 1710010109). And, the dataset identifies many marine-terrestrial shoreline HUCs such as Pysht River-Frontal Strait of Juan De Fuca HUC 1711002107 which are closely related contributing watersheds to marine waters. But, there were challenges using this dataset for all marine and species. The Washington HUC10 dataset was an imperfect option for some marine, island, and shoreline (e.g., shorebirds, clams) species:
• several islands may be grouped into one HUC (e.g., Lummi Island -Guemes Island HUC 1711000204) which doesn't speak well to island endemism; • an island may show as part of a mainland HUC which doesn't speak to the ecological barriers or lack of connectivity; and
• marine areas such as estuaries, bays, or shorelines may be delineated somewhat coarsely including some of the terrestrial watersheds which can represent marine species in terrestrial ecoregions.
All Washington terrestrial and marine watersheds were related to the nine ecoregions so that the information for SGCN could be sorted into ecoregions. There is admittedly fault in this approach for marine, shorebird, and some lowland species because some of the HUC10 watersheds touch marine shorelines and waters and may extend far inland and upland, placing a marine-dependent species in a mountainous ecoregion. For example, a shark is attributed to a marine-touching or marine-focused HUC10; the automatic data sorting may put that shark in any ecoregion touching that HUC, even a mountainous ecoregion like the North Cascades (Figure 
9
). After sorting the data, SMEs reviewed the tables and have manually sorted marine species out of ecoregions where they clearly do not occur (e.g., no sharks in the mountains).
WDFW will work on solutions for better marine species range representation in the period between revisions (see SWAP Planning Recommendations section).

). ).

D

Here is an excerpt as an example for one species and one HUC selection. The intention of the SGCN range maps at the HUC10 scale is to guide conservation, not capture the full history of the species in Washington state or the fine-scale location information for these species. These selections are based on best available information from 1978 to 2025: mappable observations, records, and other information, most of which are captured in institutional and trusted data systems, sourced from surveys, monitoring, reports, literature, and expert sources. As previously mentioned, a selected HUC does not indicate that suitable habitat is widely or evenly distributed throughout the HUC. Known habitat use and extent of that habitat could be considered. For example, if a biologist had knowledge of habitat lost to development and subsequent loss of the species in the watershed, they may not have selected the HUC even if observation records were present in that watershed; not all biologists have that information every year for every species.

## SGCN Habitats

Required Element 2. Descriptions of locations and relative condition of key habitats and community types essential to conservation of SGCN.
Meeting this Element in this section: This section outlines the framework, data sources, and process used to identify habitat associations for SGCN, along with the results of that work. This, paired with other information in this plan, guides habitat conservation actions that benefit SGCN. In both SWAP 2015 and SWAP 2025, habitat associations for SGCN lean on the assumption that conserving ecosystems has direct benefits to species that occur within them.

## Information, Processes, and Limitations

Habitat -enough of the right kind in the right places -is the most important piece of species conservation. It is a complex set of biotic and abiotic environmental features and conditions that may change through a species life, may shift on the landscape or over time, and is necessary to support a species' particular breeding, feeding, and sheltering. This applies to plants and animals. Threats to habitat have cascading effects to a species' ability to survive, reproduce, and maintain healthy populations, making habitat conservation a critical priority.
Because ecologists and biologists can never know all the intricate ways that species needs are met at all scales, native healthy and functional habitat conservation is the best way to set a species' population up for success or recovery, preserve relationships among species, and enable species' contributions to the larger environment.
Example. Species and habitat conservation work together, and salmon are a good example. Salmon must have healthy functioning riparian systems and streams with particular conditions and structure. For salmon to recover, we need to restore many of these habitats. And, when salmon use those streams, they die in spawning events as part of their natural cycle. Their bodies and nutrients catch in stream pools and side-channels formed by big logs and rocks, often placed there by restoration efforts. Those bodies feed stream invertebrates and streamside plants, that in turn feed and shelter other species. Salmon carcasses provide food for other animals and birds that carry those nutrients far into upland habitats, which can support the restoration and persistence of other species.
Plant and animal conservation actions such as protection for natural population rebound or recovery or captive-rearing for future reintroduction are most successful when the habitat is ready to fully support those recovery actions. And, while single-species site conservation is necessary and often the most feasible place to start, site conservation needs to be paired with ecosystem conservation for a view toward sustainability -this is the context that supports the site. For some species, we may not know enough about how it uses or contributes to the site where it occurs, so conserving the broader system improves the opportunity for success. For example, Limestone Point Mountainsnail and Olympic mudminnow both have extremely limited known ranges; the fine-scale habitats where they occur in talus and outcrops, and instream, respectively depend on the inputs and function of their context; conserving just the sites where they occur will not likely ensure that they persist.
Example. If a vernal pool site that a single species of fairy shrimp relies on is conserved, but not the surrounding contributing environment, then the pool environment will change and could be much less suitable or even unsuitable for fairy shrimp. In this case, a complete conservation solution needs to also consider conserving the terrestrial surrounding environment so that structure and contour of the surrounding area allows water to seasonally flow into the pool, upland and riparian vegetation filters pollutants from the inflow, decaying vegetation falls into the pool and provides refuge when the pools dry out seasonally, and birds and other wildlife can introduce the tiny organisms that fairy shrimp eat.
For many species, a single-species and site focus may not be feasible. Many invertebrates need conservation intervention. We are aware of the threats many of these species face but are often lacking biological information that could give us a complete picture. For example, we know that Morrison's bumble bee is threatened by many factors including loss of shrubsteppe habitat. With the pace and scale of shrubsteppe loss and degradation, we don't have time to try to understand everything about Morrison's bumble bee and how it uses that habitat before the species might disappear. Instead, protecting and restoring large areas of shrubsteppe should be prioritized. This is also important in aquatic and riparian systems where all of the species-specific habitats are connected and dependent on the water quality and quantity, instream structure, and the terrestrial contributions to the systems. Conservation ability -both in human and financial resources -is very limited to create effective and timely conservation if approached species-by-species in all cases. When we also take conservation action at the system level, our species, habitat, and site investments will be more effective and durable.
The ecosystems associated with an SGCN contain and support potential suitable habitat for that species. While species respond to finer scale habitats such as vernal pools or forest stand age or condition, and that drives where a species occurs, the fine scale (e.g., a talus slope, a patch of old growth forest, an isolated wetland) cannot alone assure the long-term sustainability of the species without a functional ecosystem context. Conserving ecosystems has direct benefits to species and their habitats and it is a scale we can apply to all species in the SWAP. Identifying ecosystem relationships for SGCN is important for successful, durable conservation.

## Habitat Association Framework and Data Sources

Similar to 2015, SGCN in SWAP 2025 are associated with ecosystems to meet Element 2 with more contemporary data frameworks. WDFW and WNHP chose to use two ecosystem data sets that can continue to improve SWAP work because they are likely to be available for the foreseeable future, scientifically vetted and peer-reviewed, improved over time, and used by many other conservation planning efforts and systems at international, national, regional, state, and local scales. One of the sets is spatially available which could be useful in the future to identify focal areas and certain scales of project work.
Using habitat associations is a coarse scale and a snapshot in time. Habitat conditions on the ground change every day. So, it will be important to pair the SWAP habitat associations, which are based on remote-sensed and/or modeled datasets, with on-the-ground information during project work (e.g., planning, implementation, monitoring).
Example. Marbled Murrelets are closely associated with North Pacific Maritime Douglas-fir -Western Hemlock Rainforest in the Coast Range ecoregion; however, they have fine-scale habitat requirements within that ecosystem type that includes a set of structures, features, conditions, and habitat core-toedge ratios of mature and old growth of that forest type. Those are critical components of habitat suitability Marbled Murrelets to consider when doing conservation for that species in the larger system.
This SWAP does not fully describe all finer scale or changing ecological conditions of the ecosystems related to each SGCN. There are WDFW and other online resources that describe fine-scale habitat requirements for most SGCN (for species with WDFW species profile webpages, links are provided in Table 
A
). Finer-scale information is more appropriately evaluated and assessed with current conditions and information prior to conservation project-level decisions. That information may evolve the more we learn about a species use of its habitat, habitat shifts over time, and the effects of environmental changes.
The two ecosystem frameworks used in the 2025 SWAP are defined by
• U.S. National Vegetation Classification (NVC) 'Groups' 
93
 and • Global Ecosystem Typology (GET) 'Functional Groups' 
94
 The NVC is used by many SWAPs in the national network, is widely regarded as a standard in other agencies and organizations' vegetation community modeling and conservation planning work, and is a spatial data set that can be used for ecoregional relationships. 
95
 NVC and GET are significant in WA DNR Natural Heritage plant and vegetation community status and conservation work. 
96
 NVC Groups include vegetation and abiotic factors for both terrestrial and aquatic systems, but that classification does not cover aquatic freshwater and marine types that do not have a vegetation feature. Some SGCN do not have a vegetation relationship in their habitat needs (e.g., many marine species like whales or rockfish) so the GET Functional Groups fill that gap.
NVC and GET functional groups represent current or recent ecosystem conditions. Ecosystems in WA are changing rapidly due to climate driven changes in temperature, hydrology, disturbance, and successional processes. As a result, vegetation structure, and distribution and character of NVC/GET may also shift over time. To manage this limitation, implementation of the SWAP will rely on updated spatial datasets from NVC, GET, and other sources to assess ecosystem and habitat trends and inform 

## NVC Groups Description

Unlike the static ecological systems used in 2015 97 , the NVC Groups are a set of defined ecosystem types that are periodically updated by the Ecological Society of America's Vegetation Classification Panel and the USNVC Peer Review Board, and most types have a spatial data set. A representative from the Washington Natural Heritage Program is involved in both committees. 
98
 The NVC is a hierarchy of nested modeled and mapped (spatial) data that can be related to watersheds and ecoregions and loosely related to the species occurrence data. NVC Groups present the wide range of existing vegetation, from natural types that include old-growth forest stands and seminatural vegetation (including grazed rangelands, old agricultural lands undergoing natural succession, and stands dominated by naturalized exotics) to planted or cultivated vegetation, such as row crops, orchards, and forest plantations.
NVC Groups are a coarser scale than species observation data. For animals, SMEs used mapped aggregated species observation and NVC systems data, system descriptions, range maps, literature about species habitat use and system relationships, and firsthand knowledge to associate SGCN to NVC Groups. Ecosystems that support plant SGCN were identified by WNHP using the NVC Group classification. 
99
 Associating species to a Group does not mean that the species occurs throughout the maximum extent of that NVC type. A species may be closely associated to a particular system but limited within that system by life history and finer scale characteristics within that system (e.g., old growth) and its range.
Example. Marbled Murrelet is closely associated with many NVC Group forest systems: North Pacific Maritime Douglas-fir -Western Hemlock Rainforest, North-Central Pacific Maritime Silver Fir -Western Hemlock Rainforest, North-Central Pacific Western Hemlock -Sitka Spruce Rainforest, to name a few. By the mapped extent of those types, one might assume Marbled Murrelets would be able to inhabit much of western Washington. However, Marbled Murrelet potential range for nesting, rearing young, and dispersal is contracted in Washington because they rely on mature and old-growth forest with very specific characteristics for nesting and within a certain distance of marine environments where they forage. Those forest patches are few and far between within those larger forested systems.
NVC Group relationships in this SWAP provide system-to-species connections that can be related to the ecoregion, landscape, and watershed scales before scaling down to project planning at the site scale. NVC Groups and finer scales (Alliances and Associations) provide a consistent hierarchy to describe 97 Ecological systems used in SWAP 2015 was a static image, a map provided as a raster file for GIS work with low resolution. 
98
 NatureServe (on behalf of US NVC Partnership) 2025a; USNVC Partnership 2016 
99
 WA DNR NHP provides a good overview of the US NVC 8-level hierarchy on their website dnr.wa.gov/naturalheritage-program/ecosystems-washington/us-national-vegetation-classification and describes the relationship to NatureServe and the system that NHP uses to identify plant and vegetation community status in Washington in their updated classification document dnr.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2025-03/amp_nh_assoc_list.pdf. conservation objectives and desired future outcomes from landscape conservation planning to habitat restoration projects. When using NVC Groups to approach a conservation project, it is useful to also describe the desired future condition of finer scale habitat quality, integrity, or stage in project planning (e.g., early seral forest or old growth/mature forest). The NVC Group data products are to be used in concert with project-level planning, not in lieu of. As the NVC data layers improve, the species -system relationship data could become more useful.
In fall 2025, USNVC updated many of the Groups for Washington state. While these changes occurred after the SGCN habitat association work, WDFW worked with WA DNR WNHP to get the updated list of names, crosswalk those appropriately with the SME selections for the SWAP database, and update the SWAP tables. All of the changes that were one-to-one name changes were made; four changes required more documentation (Table 
5
). Additional work will be done during implementation to refine these associations as we learn more about the extent of these NVC Groups. Will be removed in future SWAP data updates and any SGCN habitat assignments will be appropriately reattributed to G317, G319, and/or G320 No SGCN data changes were made for this association.
Eighty-one (81) NVC Groups for Washington are potentially relatable to SGCN associations (Table 
F
). Eight of those were not selected for either close or general SGCN association; one type (Western Interior Ruderal Grassland and Shrubland) was added in September 2025 after SGCN habitat associations were complete, so it was counted in the association dataset.

## GET Functional Groups Description

The NVC does not provide a descriptive structure for fully aquatic habitats, those that are non-vegetated aquatic systems such as such as rivers and streams, lakes and reservoirs, artificial wetlands, estuaries and coastal inlets, and marine environments. Some GET Functional Group ecosystems
foot_11
 were added which are important for SGCN for all or a portion of their lifecycle. These are listed in Table F with a habitat profile link for "global ecosystems." This approach also filled some of the habitat association gaps for some SGCN that were not related to systems in 2015.
Overall, for Washington state, GET Functional Groups are not mapped. GET provides some mapping for some systems at a very coarse scale unsuitable for this effort. 
101
 Descriptions of the GET Functional Groups are available online through the IUCN GET Explorer and links are in Table 
F
.
In Washington, there are 69 possible GET Functional Groups and 41 were related to SGCN.
See also SWAP Planning Conservation Recommendations: there is a need to relate most GET for Washington state with standardized spatial data relationships for springs, streams, rivers, marine basins, and caves to enable better project planning.

## NVC and GET Benefits

These nationally and globally adopted frameworks allow biologists and ecologists to use the same vocabulary across taxa and regions to describe a relationship between systems and species. The NVC structure is used by other SWAPs across the U.S. which gives the Washington SWAP and its SGCN information more relationship to those plans regionally and nationally.
Associating SGCN to NVC Groups and GET Functional Groups creates information that can be useful to understand which SGCN rely on the same systems. Relating species to systems in ecoregions and watersheds can find opportunities and efficiencies in multi-species conservation.
Example. Both Sagebrush Sparrow and Sagebrush Lizard are closely associated with Intermountain Basins Big Sagebrush Steppe, an NVC Group. A portion of their ranges defined by HUC10 watersheds overlap. Although they have different lifestyles and finer scale habitats, biologists can look at the range for both species and the extent of the NVC Group as a starting place to explore if there are opportunities in that overlapping geography to do projects that benefit both species and contribute to the integrity of the larger system.

## Associating NVC and GET to SGCN

SWAP 2015 and SWAP 2025 use the same vocabulary
foot_13
 to associate SGCN to systems/habitats:
• Closely Associated: The species demonstrates preference for the ecological system, as indicated by greater occurrence, high densities, greater reproductive output, or other indicators of preference, than in other systems. A species that is closely associated to individual ecological systems often relies on one to a few ecological systems for a significant part or all of its life history requirements. • Generally Associated: The species occurs in, but does not prefer, the ecological system, as indicated by lesser occurrence, lower densities, or other indicators of a general relationship with the ecological system. A species that is generally associated with individual ecological systems can typically rely on numerous ecological systems to meet its life history requirements. • Unsuitable: A species demonstrates no use or only occasional use of the ecological system.
• Unknown: The species' use of the ecological system is unknown. The are questions or uncertainty about species use of an ecological system.
In this SWAP, biologists faced the same challenge as in the 2015 SWAP: using only species observation data (points and polygons where we know a species has been documented) to relate species to the systems may
• limit potential conservation opportunity -not all areas that could be suitable for a species can be surveyed (i.e., access, capacity, expertise, and other limiting factors shape where surveys can occur and how often); • miss actual systems and habitats on the ground due to the scale at which NVC is available (species observation data paired with NVC can be used as a coarse filter for landscape-and watershed-scale project planning, supported by remote-sensed vegetation where appropriate and ground-truthed conditions); and, • not account for the rapid pace of habitat change due to human influences.
Animal SGCN habitat associations using NVC Groups and GET Functional Groups were made by WDFW SMEs viewing aggregated observation data for species, where available; reviewing literature; and applying their expertise and the advice of professional networks. This work was done in the ArcGIS 'experience' described in the SWAP database section.
Plant SGCN habitat associations were identified by WA DNR WNHP using element occurrence data, habitat requirements of each plant SGCN, along with the updated information presented in the Washington Natural Heritage Plan. 
103
 All plant SGCN are considered closely associated to the NVC Group identified in the SWAP. No GET Functional Groups were identified for plant SGCN during the 2024 SME work; however, the change noted in Table 
F
 for Temperate Pacific Seagrass Bed will potentially change that in the WA DNR WNHP database.

## Challenges

We share these challenges to be transparent about the limitations of information, generate better understanding among SWAPs, and generate future conversations and solutions with conservation partners between revisions. See also SWAP Planning Recommendations section.
NVC Groups. This coarse scale is useful for seeing patterns in SGCN associations, landscape conservation opportunities that can benefit many SGCN, and ecoregional relationships. Because the spatial data for Washington is missing ruderal and human-constructed types, the NVC dataset is not mappable wall-towall (i.e., there are gaps in spatial data). So, while it is not appropriate for project-level planning at the site scale, it is still useful for relationship-building among different types of data (e.g., species range at the HUC10 watershed scale).
For the SWAP database work, NVC Groups spatial data was aggregated into a 1-mile hex grid pattern to generalize the scale and discourage inappropriate fine-scale use. This conversion from ecological systems static map to the NVC Group spatial data will have some "growing pains" as we learn more about the best use and limitations of this information. For now, it's a good set for SWAP purposes. And, as conservation practitioners use it, other spatial data can be related to these Groups to refine the sitescale planning utility and retain the ability to "roll up" to NVC Groups for reporting implementation or sorting actions, species, and habitats of interest.
Other Spatial Data Issues. Not all NVC Groups were available for Washington as a spatial data set (only 52 mapped, missing NVC ruderal and anthropogenic types) and none of the GET Functional Groups are mapped at a scale that is useful for state conservation planning. WDFW and WA DNR would have benefitted from institutional state data set relationships to many of the unmapped types to make the SME SGCN database work easier (e.g., GET M1.2 Kelp Forests related to DNR's kelp spatial data). Even though the database work was supported with aggregated species observation data, habitat associations were difficult to make. Using the observation data and all Washington potential NVC and GET ecosystem descriptions, SMEs made informed decisions.
Marine and Freshwater Species Habitat Associations. As with SWAP 2015 decisions, this was a difficult exercise. In 2015, many freshwater and marine species were associated with mapped terrestrial or contributing systems (e.g., bull trout was associated with North Pacific Maritime Douglas-fir -Western Hemlock Rainforest). That decision makes sense when we think about the influence of terrestrial actions on the aquatic environments, but it does not give us a way to highlight or report on conservation actions in the aquatic environment, and this is complicated further by unmapped GET types (all non-vegetated aquatic types are GET and unmapped). SMEs made good choices, sticking very close to the habitats that the species occur in (or could occur in, with restoration or recovery work). In some instances, riparian vegetation types may be selected for instream SGCN because of a close association or input from streamside vegetation. It's important to keep in mind that upland restoration and protection actions are very beneficial for instream habitats and aquatic species, and in most cases the conservation actions reflect this need. And, we can do better in the next SWAP revision if we associate existing authoritative spatial data sets to the GET Functional Groups (SWAP Planning Recommendations). Ruderal vegetation. "Ruderal" is a term used in NatureServe vegetation classification 104 to capture human-influenced or human-shifted systems. Different from completely converted and managed land use types that are still vegetated, like orchards or agricultural fields actively in use, ruderal types give an "in between" classification option: not quite native, not quite totally converted or gone. While these types often have a dominant invasive species presence, they also have key identifying features of the natural type.
In many landscapes, ruderal ecosystems occupy large areas--sometimes more than any other category of communities--and can provide important biodiversity functions: useful for wildlife for habitat connectivity, buffering natural systems from some types of impact (e.g., development), or -with restoration -habitat suitability.
Annual grasslands with shrublands in a native sagebrush matrix are a good example. 
105
 Example. One of the ruderal types, Great Basin-Intermountain Ruderal Dry Shrubland and Grassland, could be an important matrix and connectivity habitat type in the HGCN Shrubsteppe with site potential for more than 50 SGCN, although only 16 species were 'associated' with that ruderal type: Example. North Pacific Maritime Coastal Ruderal Dune & Bluff are shifting sand dunes and other sandy areas at sea level along the Pacific Coast dominated by non-native beachgrasses and may have mixed areas of restoration-planted native dune grass, may include introduced low shrubs and other non-native species generally limited to those that can invade and thrive in the shifting sand and salt-spray environment. In some areas, that ruderal type may be very important to retain dunes during storm surge or may provide refuge for some dune-dependent species in that shifting environment especially where native dune habitats no longer exist. Ruderal ecosystems are generally not conservation priorities; ecologists don't manage to a ruderal type. But, these types may be important interim habitats during rare species recovery (e.g., westside prairie butterflies that can use ruderal types and non-native plant host species in those types for a portion of 104 NatureServe Biotics 5 Online Help topics index: "Ruderal." help.natureserve.org/biotics/#Record_Management/Element_Files/Element_Tracking/ETRACK_Ruderal.htm#kanc hor269 This definition page has a good explanation, examples, and references. 105 Davies et al. 2021 their lifecycle) or function as important landscape connectors or matrices in reserves. In landcover or GAP mapping, these ruderal types are important to map because of their large extent. The question for SWAP was whether associating these types to SGCN and HGCN would help drive good conservation decisions. The answer was "it depends" and we didn't really have the time to consider a full critique or set of circumstances where it would be appropriate to say "yes" or "no." During SWAP SGCN and HGCN work, the focus was on native systems, although some ruderal types were associated. Because this was an unanticipated question, the SME work wasn't guided on that topic to value or not value these types. With more discussion on ruderal values and considerations, the SMEs may have placed a higher or lower value on ruderal types. For now, in certain habitat types, the value of ruderal is in the matrix context and restoration potential, which could be very important in certain systems. One criticism is that the spatial data is too coarse to define where restoration of a ruderal type would be beneficial at the site scale, so we should focus only on associating native habitat systems since that would be the restoration objective. We have more work to do here to clearly define how to handle ruderal associations in the SWAP.
Anthropogenic or Artificial Habitats. Sometimes, plants, fish, and wildlife can't be that choosy to only live in natural (or mostly natural) habitats and still persist. In some places, the human-modified environment may be the only place the species is retaining a toehold while ecologists, biologists, and restoration specialists figure out the best approach. Wetland shrub horticultural crops such as cranberries, managed monotypical forest plantations, grain crops, artificial shorelines, and large reservoirs are examples of these types which can still serve limited natural habitat function and provide connectivity better than suburban or urban areas, although there are often human-wildlife or humanplant conflicts. Some examples include elk eating the cranberry crop, sandhill cranes nesting when it's time to harvest the wheat, marine mammals drawn to artificial haulouts and being territorial, and reservoir management that may conflict with downstream aquatic life needs. SMEs considered whether human-influenced or human-constructed habitats are key for SGCN and HGCN; in some cases that last toehold is what's giving us the opportunity to make good decisions, find funding, create partnerships, and resolve potential conflicts to improve the success and durability of conservation investments of social capital, expertise, time, and money.

## Habitats of Greatest Conservation Need (HGCN)

This section is related to, but not required by, Element 2. The habitats presented as HGCN 
106
 are key habitats and community types essential to conservation of SWAP species, not just SGCN. This section identifies supporting concepts, ecosystems, and special features that are conservation targets, prioritized in Washington. Habitat conservation is the core of successful species conservation.
foot_15
 "Ultimately, the future viability of every species depends on the extent, configuration, and quality of its habitat" - 
Noss et al. 2021 108
 SWAPs require species-based information, and habitat integrity and ecosystem conservation are foundations for the success of most direct species conservation actions. Conservation actions for habitat sustainability such as protection, restoration, or enhancement are the best actions we can take to prevent the need to list species, support recovery actions, and improve resiliency for systems. In scoping SWAP 2025, many conservation practitioners emphasized habitat conservation as a priority at statewide, ecoregion, and site scales, and we received comments asking to clarify the relationship between HGCN and WDFW's PHS Priority Habitats.

## WDFW PHS Priority Habitats and WA DNR WNHP rare ecosystems are SWAP HGCN.

HGCN are priority areas, ecosystems, and features that support SGCN and other species that are indicative of the diversity of the state's wildlife. Many HGCN support the ecosystem functions that also influence people's well-being and livelihoods. This section's information can build conservation durability when paired with the SGCN habitat associations and conservation actions by considering species' needs and their ecosystem context.
HGCN occur at many scales -big to small, regional to sites. In conservation project planning, one scale may be the focus (such as restoring habitat for a species-specific site) and the relationship to other scales in the framework should be considered (e.g., the riparian relationship to an instream habitat restoration) (Compass Point: Start 'Upstream').
The purpose of including HGCN in the SWAP is to guide habitat and system conservation, research, and management in ecoregions to support fish, wildlife, plants, and human well-being.

## The HGCN framework

• shows relationships among WDFW's Priority Habitats, Washington Natural Heritage Plan, important resiliency and habitat connectivity values, and SGCN; • includes conservation contributions in human spaces -parklands, urban riparian corridors, wildland-urban interfaces, agricultural and ranching lands; • uses science-based resources and supporting documentation; and,
• communicates the importance of habitat conservation to a wide variety of audiences.
Ideally, a higher priority is placed on native wild or conserved habitats and systems as the needs of fish and wildlife are more easily met in places with fewer human impacts. However, habitats in or adjacent to human spaces can be crucial to support species during restoration in native habitats, connect wildlands through urban areas, provide important 'stepping stones' of habitat for migratory species, and engage people in conservation. So, some HGCN may also have representation in human environments.

## Process

In SWAP 2015 Chapter 4, HGCN included
• ecological systems considered imperiled (identified by WNHP and called Ecological Systems of Concern) and • ecological systems which were closely associated to six or more SGCN.
The 2025 SWAP does not use a certain number of SGCN for HGCN identification. A distinction driven by the SGCN numbers alone doesn't incorporate philosophical or policy values about what makes a habitat important for biodiversity even though that can be one good indicator. See Future: Adding HGCN section.
During revision, an "Important Habitats" framework was developed by a cross-Program WDFW team and shared externally during Spring 2025 engagement for comment. The framework outlined categories for statewide and ecoregion habitat conservation strategies. Concepts that are presented in SWAP 2025 were honed by review and comments, and the name was changed to HGCN to align with SWAP 2015.
All NVC and GET ecosystems used in this SWAP are in Table 
F
, and a subset of those are HGCN based on their relationship to WDFW's Priority Habitats 109 and the WNHP Washington Natural Heritage Plan 110 , resiliency and habitat connectivity resources, and site to system conservation representation.

In SWAP 2025, HGCN are stand-alone conservation targets to address habitat and system sustainability and are related to SGCN to make that connection. HGCN are also highlighted in the Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs and ecoregion sections. HGCN recommended conservation actions are in this section.

 In many of the HGCN sections, additional resources are listed. These are not in the References section of this plan because they were not cited in the text.

6
) are in three categories:
• Habitat Support • Strategic Habitats and • Specialized Feature Habitats.
