## Habitat Support HGCN

Habitat Support HGCN support durable and resilient finer-scale habitats for species and represent ecological concepts that can be applied at any scale from site to landscape, can be supported by data and/or modeled information, are not species-specific, and are important in part or whole to protect habitat function and ecosystem services. These places can be a matrix of various habitat types (e.g., riparian, wetlands, floodplain, and river together), successional stages, and quality to achieve the value(s) of biodiversity areas, corridors, and/or climate refugia.
These places have characteristics that support many species and habitats at many scales. While typically these are landscape-scale, they can also be smaller-scale and important within urban areas that connect to urban edges and wildlands (e.g., connected riparian through an urban core). When these places are conserved, they support longer-term and broader habitat and species sustainability, can be applied at any scale from site to landscape, are supported by data and/or modeled information, and are not single SGCN-specific but are beneficial to several SGCN and the habitats on which they depend. These places may be single-type ecosystems (e.g., "shrubsteppe") or a matrix of various habitats and systems (e.g., floodplain, riparian freshwater wetlands, and a river system combined), successional stages, and quality to achieve the value(s) of biodiversity areas, corridors, and/or climate refugia.

## Biodiversity Areas (Supporting Biodiversity)

Biodiversity areas are natural areas that support diverse wildlife populations, habitats, and/or critical and unique elements of Washington State's biodiversity. These areas should be able to sustain viable populations of species, which can persist over the long term without facing immediate extinction due to demographic stochasticity, genetic issues or environmental fluctuations. Biodiversity areas often function as core areas 114 and are important for connectivity. WDFW defines biodiversity areas as: identified as biologically diverse through a scientifically based assessment conducted over a landscape scale (e.g., statewide, ecoregion, county-or city-wide, watershed) (examples include WDFW Local Habitat Assessments, Pierce County Biodiversity Network, Priority Amphibian and Reptile Conservation Areas 115 ); or within a city or an urban growth area (UGA) and contains habitat that is valuable to fish or wildlife and is mostly comprised of native vegetation. Relative to other vegetated areas in the same city or UGA, the mapped area is vertically diverse (e.g., multiple canopy layers, snags, or 
114
 Cores are usually large, internally well-connected blocks of relatively high-quality habitat where habitat function and accessibility to the species that depend on it are not impaired by human activities. "Large" and "high quality" are relative to conservation objectives and may differ by species/habitat relationships and spatial scale of the analysis or planning (statewide, regional, local). "Internally well-connected" means there are relatively few or no human infrastructure barriers or adverse influences on fish and wildlife use of the area. The edge of a core is often a gradient, not a hard line or border, and that edge is determined by influences on habitat quality (e.g., disruptions in canopy closure or loss of understory, light or noise penetration, non-native species invasion) that may affect how the core does or does not support species' needs. 
115
 Sutherland and deMaynadier 2012; see also parcplace.org/habitat/parcas/.
downed wood), horizontally diverse (e.g., contains a mosaic of native habitats), or supports a diverse community of species as identified by a qualified professional who has a degree in biology or closely related field and professional experience related to the habitats or species occurring in the biodiversity area. These areas may have more limited wildlife functions than other priority habitat areas due to the general nature and constraints of these sites in that they are often isolated or surrounded by highly urbanized lands. 
116
 Biodiversity areas meet at least one of the following criteria based on the IUCN 2016 guidelines 117 :
• supports a high diversity of species, habitats or ecosystems, especially those of high conservation priority (listed in PHS and/or as Species of Greatest Conservation Need).
• is large enough to sustain genetically diverse, viable population(s) of highly threatened or rare species, ensuring long-term adaptability through sufficient birth and survival rates that counteract mortality and maintain genetic diversity.
• holds geographically restricted (e.g., endemic) populations of species or ecosystems.
• maintains a high degree of naturalness and ecological integrity, meaning its ecological processes and functions are largely intact and relatively undisturbed by human activities.
• serves as a crucial refuge for species or ecosystems, allowing them to persist during periods of environmental stress (e.g., drought, extreme temperatures), or providing a critical refuge from human impacts.
• is irreplaceable for the persistence of biodiversity across the larger landscape (e.g., a core habitat connecting other source populations).
In addition to the table below, integration of the conservation priorities of the WA DNR WNHP Natural Heritage Plan (2025, and any updates that could influence SWAP outcomes) into WDFW PHS biodiversity area identification would improve both the biodiversity guidance provided by PHS and the implementation of the WA DNR WNHP Natural Heritage Plan. Identify, protect and provide endowed or permanently funded management for biodiversity areas, especially those that contribute to regional ecological integrity and connectivity, to offset habitat losses from urban and developing areas and natural habitat conversion.
Identify, purchase, protect, and manage for conservation values: floodplain and riparian habitats and buffers, wetland habitats and buffers; especially those that provide climate refugia and contribute beneficially to stream flow and temperature stabilization
Changes in temperature, precipitation, and hydrological cycles pose some of the most significant and complex threats to biodiversity area resilience, fundamentally altering the conditions that species rely upon. This might cause species range shifts and creation of "novel ecosystems" where existing conservation strategies may not apply. Protect refugia and movement corridors spanning elevational or landscape gradients, allowing species to move in response to changes. The pace and scale of habitat and system science and planning has greatly advanced in Washington in just two years. The Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan advances the concept of biodiversity areas with cores and connectivity values.

## Easements &

Apply the recommendations of the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan for spatial data improvements that help the conservation community scale and plan better core and corridor protection for biodiversity. 

## Action

## Refugia (Supporting Resilience)

Refugia are areas where local biophysical conditions reduce the rate or magnitude of climate exposure relative to the surrounding landscape. These locations allow for the persistence of species, populations, or ecological processes that might otherwise decline or disappear under a changing climate (Morelli et al. 2016, Keppel et al. 2015). Refugia may arise from topographic complexity, persistent hydrological inputs, vegetation structure, other site-specific features that moderate local climatic conditions, such as temperature extremes or moisture deficits (Dobrowski 2011; Ashcroft 2010).
Anthropogenic climate change is altering ecosystems across spatial and temporal scales. Observed impacts include range shifts, phenological mismatches, altered disturbance regimes, and increased physiological stress on species (Parmesan 2006; Pacifici et al. 2015). These stressors can lead to reduced reproductive success, local extirpations, and ecosystem restructuring, particularly for taxa with limited dispersal capacity such as amphibians or narrow in environmental tolerances such as American pika.
Within this context, climate refugia provide spatially discrete areas where environmental conditions remain within the physiological or ecological limits for resident biota. For plant and animal species, these areas can function as demographic anchors-supporting breeding populations, offering thermal or hydrological refuge during climate extremes, or enabling life cycle completion when broader habitat conditions become unsuitable (Morelli et al. 2020; Krawchuk et al. 2020). In forested ecosystems, for example, dense canopy cover and topographic shading can reduce understory temperatures and moisture loss, facilitating presence of moisture-sensitive species. In aquatic ecosystems, cold-water refuges maintained by groundwater or hyporheic exchange can buffer fish and amphibian populations from thermally stressful conditions during droughts or heatwaves (Issak et al. 2015).
To be effective, refugia must support ecological function and demographic persistence over relevant timeframes. Their benefits are particularly pronounced in landscapes undergoing rapid change, where refugia may serve as important sources of recolonization, genetic diversity, or phenotypic plasticity. However, refugia may change and thresholds may vary and depend on local buffering mechanisms, including shade, topography, and hydrology. Their capacity to buffer climate impacts may diminish under high greenhouse gas emission scenarios or when interacting stressors-such as land use change, invasive species, or altered fire regimes-overwhelm localized buffering mechanisms (Davis et al. 2023; Frey et al. 2016).
Recognizing the importance of these areas, recent conservation planning frameworks have integrated climate refugia into spatial prioritization and adaptive management strategies. These approaches aim to identify and protect areas that offer bioclimatic stability and ecological resilience under climate change.
Consider refugia protection in land use planning decisions, site protection and conservation incentives, water quality and quantity decisions, and landscape core and connectivity planning. Some good resources to inform this work: 

## Cores and Corridors (Supporting Connectivity)

Connectivity refers to the degree to which landscape's structure and composition facilitates animal movement. 
121
 It is defined by the needs of the organisms that live and move within it, making it speciesand context-dependent.
"… habitat fragmentation reduces biodiversity by 13 to 75% and impairs key ecosystem functions …. Effects are greatest in the smallest and most isolated fragments, and they magnify with the passage of time. (There is) an urgent need for conservation and restoration measures to improve landscape connectivity, which will reduce extinction rates and help maintain ecosystem services." -Haddad et al. 2015
A connected landscape can be larger blocks of quality habitat (cores) linked by habitat of lesser quality but permeable enough to allow movement between cores within species-specific maximum distance. Such a matrix can represent a diffuse connectivity with few barriers to impede or constrain movement, or more concentrated connectivity where habitat loss constrains or narrows movement options, forming corridors between core habitats. Depending on the level of habitat conversion these corridors can be wide or channelized routes allowing wildlife movement through otherwise heavily modified landscapes. 122 Furthermore, corridors can include small patches of quality habitat ("stepping stones"), not large enough to sustain populations but allowing animals to rest, find food and shelter on their journey between cores. The two main components of a conceptual connectivity network are therefore cores and corridors, each with a distinct role in conservation. It is important to note, however, that while these are often modeled as distinct entities, they are not empirically separate categories in the real world. A single location could be validly designated as a core or a corridor depending on the species, scale, and analytical objectives.
Cores are blocks of habitat large enough to support wildlife populations and their essential life functions. 
123
 They are characterized by high habitat quality and a low human footprint, meaning they are internally well-connected and largely uninterrupted by major barriers. Because of their size and lack of fragmentation, these areas provide a high level of intrinsic connectivity. They are also the critical 
121 Taylor et al. 1993
 122 
Schloss et al. 2021
123 Forman. 1995
 places that a connectivity network seeks to link together. Protecting these intact core areas is the foundational goal of any connectivity conservation effort. 124
Corridors are the pathways or linkages between habitat cores. For most large and highly mobile species, corridors provide habitat sufficient to facilitate movement and help sustain permanent populations. Species with limited mobility may need to reside within corridors and only achieve migration over multiple generations. In core-corridor modeling, corridors differ from cores because their habitat quality is lower or too linear to function as core habitat. 
125
 Corridors facilitate gene flow by allowing individuals to move between populations to find mates. This genetic exchange enhances the overall genetic variability of metapopulations, reducing their vulnerability to demographic stochasticity and preventing inbreeding depression, thereby making them more resilient to environmental fluctuations. 
126
 Corridors also support species seasonal migrations for breeding, foraging, and wintering, e.g., vertical migrations between high and low-elevation habitats.
Finally, corridors can act as a pathway for species to shift their geographic ranges in response to a changing climate. 
127
 Climate corridors are distinct from traditional wildlife corridors because they are explicitly designed to connect habitats along a climate gradient allowing altitudinal and latitudinal range shifts.
These are the key characteristics of wildlife corridors 128 :
• Connect core habitats: must link at least two distinct core habitat areas.
• Facilitate safe movement: enables movement of species, especially highly threatened or rare ones (listed in PHS and/or as Species of Greatest Conservation Need), between habitat patches. This includes movements for o Daily foraging and resource access.
o Seasonal migration.
o Dispersal.
o Genetic exchange for population health.
• Provide suitable internal habitat: offers adequate cover, food, and water within the corridor to support passage or even extended residency for "corridor dwellers". • Contain minimal barriers or impediments to movement such as o Human infrastructure (e.g., roads, fences, high-density development), 
124
 Fahrig. 2003 
125 Bennett et al. 2003
, Hilty et al. 2019
, Michalak et al. 2025
 126 Packer et al. 1991, Cushman 2006 127 Krosby et al. 2010 128 Baier and Noss 1998, Hilty et al. 2019 o Human activity (e.g., noise, light pollution), and o Increased predation risk from "bottleneck" effects.
• Enhance population resilience -contributes to the ability of populations to adapt to environmental stressors like climate change by allowing for range shifts.
Effective habitat corridors have some optimal physical characteristics:
o Generally, wider corridors are more effective, with optimal width determined by target species' home range size and sensitivity to edge effects and adjoining human disturbances (e.g., roadways).
o Sufficiently long to connect target habitats while accommodating species' movement and dispersal abilities. Generally, corridors can be narrower when the cores being connected are nearby and must be wider when connecting cores across a longer distance.
o Can be continuous strips of habitat or "stepping stone" patches, depending on species and landscape.
o Minimized human impacts and similar adjacent habitat types.
Example. A specific type of corridor that follows streams and rivers is a riparian corridor. The vegetation and undeveloped floodplains adjacent to streams and rivers make them natural pathways for wildlife movement through otherwise fragmented habitat. 
129
 In urban or agricultural areas, a riparian corridor may be the only intact and diverse natural habitat. 
130
 Riparian corridors can also allow species to move up in elevation in response to warming temperatures. 
131
 Protecting and restoring riparian areas supports both aquatic ecosystems and terrestrial wildlife movement. Riparian habitat is identified as a Priority Habitat by WDFW 132 and is protected by local Critical Areas Ordinances under the Growth Management Act, and is a statewide and transboundary conservation need identified in the SWAP.

## Connectivity network across Washington:

The Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan (WAHCAP) maps and defines connectivity in 
Washington. 133
 The WAHCAP spatial data on ArcGIS Online provides a set of spatial layers depicting different types of connectivity functions including structural ecosystem connectivity, network importance, landscape permeability, focal species connectivity, climate connectivity, and existing prioritized networks. The maps include ecosystem-specific cores mapped using a tiered approach to represent different thresholds of human impact sensitivity by systematically relaxing habitat condition requirements and human footprint thresholds used to define cores and corridors. The online data viewer also includes a layer illustrating how these individual cores and 
10
). The connectivity across the state varies considerably by region:
• Southwest Washington and Olympic Peninsula: This area is comprised of one extensive block of highest quality core centered on Olympic Mountains and many smaller cores embedded in a diffusely connected landscape of forestry lands in the southern part of the peninsula and Willapa Hills. Historically rural, this area is experiencing rapid residential and commercial development along I-5 and other major highways, which is resulting in smaller cores and highly constrained corridors in portions of the southern Cascades.
• Northwest Washington: Few, small, and fragmented cores remain in this region due to extensive human impact. The expansion of cities such as Bellingham and Everett, alongside smaller suburban communities like North Bend, contributes to the ongoing loss of remnant forests and landscape connectivity. Furthermore, fragmentation from major highways (I-5 and I-90) significantly restricts wildlife movement.
• Cascades: The region encompasses Washington's largest contiguous area of intact forest, forming a critical Connected Landscape of Statewide Significance across elevational and latitudinal gradients. With their several large and relatively well-connected blocks of high-quality core areas, the Cascades constitute a critical pathway for climate connectivity at a continental scale. Historically fully forested, the Cascades are now bisected by six highways, with I-90 and US 2 posing major barriers to animal movement. Increased development along these highways further impacts the region's ecological integrity. The highways also provide easier access to recreation areas, particularly within national forests, intensifying the ecological impact on existing core areas. 

## Strategic HGCN

Ecosystems are defined by their shared characteristics. SWAP Strategic HGCN 134 are specific ecosystems that are critical for biodiversity conservation in Washington, providing significant benefits to many SGCN, and/or are ecosystems that are at risk. 
135
 These are WDFW PHS Priority Habitats that are not Features 136 and WNHP-defined NVC Groups with state conservation status of endangered, threatened, or sensitive.
Ecosystems are defined by their shared characteristics and 'Strategic Habitats' are specific ecosystems that are critical for biodiversity conservation in Washington, similar to the habitats of the same name identified in the Oregon Conservation Strategy. These habitats are more easily relatable to specific vegetation or aquatic systems (e.g., shrubsteppe, wetlands) and the ecoregions where they occur. Most can be associated to National Vegetation Classification (NVC) Group(s) or Global Ecosystem Typology (GET) Functional Group(s) which makes a direct connection to the framework for SGCN habitat associations in the SWAP. Strategic HGCN include
• WDFW Priority Habitats that are not 'features' and which can be related one-to-one or one-tomany to NVC Groups (habitats defined by their vegetation) or GET Functional Groups (aquatic and marine habitats not defined by vegetation); and 

## Aspen Stands

Quaking aspen (Populus tremuloides) is the characteristic and dominant tree in this habitat (Crawford and Kagen 2001). It is the sole dominant in many stands although scattered ponderosa pine (Pinus ponderosa) or Douglas-fir (Pseudotsuga menziesii) may be present. Snowberry (Symphoricarpos oreophilus and less frequently S. albus) is the most common dominant shrub (Crawford and Kagen 2001). This habitat generally occurs on well-drained mountain slopes or canyon walls that have some moisture (Crawford and Kagen 2001, WDFW 2015). Rockfalls, talus, or stony north slopes are often typical sites. Aspen stands may occur in steppe on moist microsites, but are not associated with streams, ponds, or wetlands (Crawford and Kagen 2001, WDFW 2015). This habitat is found from 2,000 to 9,500 ft elevation primarily in the montane regions of eastern Washington (Crawford and Kagen 2001). It is found sporadically in valleys west of the Cascades in Washington. Aspen Stands is a designated priority habitat type in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species (PHS) program (WDFW 2008). In PHS, aspen stands are a priority when they are at least an acre in size.
Fire plays an important role in maintenance of this habitat (Crawford and Kagen 2001). Conifers now dominate many aspen stands and extensive stands of young aspen are uncommon partly because of fire suppression. With fire suppression and alteration of fine fuels, fire rejuvenation of aspen habitat has been greatly reduced. Heavy livestock browsing, primarily by domestic sheep and cattle, have adversely impacted aspen growth and regeneration (Crawford and Kagen 2001, WDFW 2015). 

## Fresh Deepwater

Deepwater habitats are generally pools deeper than 2 meters, permanently flooded lands lying below the deepwater boundary of wetlands where surface water is permanent so that water, rather than air, is the principal medium within which the dominant organisms live. The dominant plants are hydrophytes, meaning they must grow in water; if present, that vegetation is usually submerged because these habitats are generally too deep to support emergent vegetation (vegetation that sticks up above the water surface with roots or anchors generally below the waterline). These habitats include all underwater structures and features (e.g., woody debris, rock piles, caverns).
Hydrologically in streams, they are created through massive areas of scour, such as meanders, large wood structures, large boulders, or after any increased roughness areas. Depending on flow, these pools can vary in depth and size. General characteristics include low flow velocities, sometimes near-standing water conditions, and typically sediment particles drop off and aggregate if the scour is not sustained. Deepwater habitats provide rearing habitat, refuge from above water predators with sufficient depth, flow and velocity refuge, temperature refuge, and depending on fish life history strategies can provide areas where to spawn. During low flow or ephemeral conditions, deep pool provides refuge and oasis for fish and wildlife until stream flows return. In some instances, they can provide sufficient nutrients for fish, amphibian and other water related wildlife to maintain their livelihood until rains return to replenish the stream. They may also be created by structures -dams that impound water behind them. Wetlands are areas where water covers the soil or is present either at or near the surface of the soil all year or for varying periods of time during the year. The type is determined by some period of water saturation (hydrology) that determines how the soil develops and the types of plant and animal communities living in and on the soil. Wetlands may support both aquatic and terrestrial species. Longer-term water presence creates conditions that favor the growth of specially adapted plants (hydrophytes) and promote the development of characteristic wetland (hydric) soils. 
139
 Two general categories of wetlands
foot_20
 are recognized: coastal or tidal wetlands and inland or non-tidal wetlands. For SWAP, Freshwater Wetlands (this category) includes inland or non-tidal wetland such as those along floodplains or rivers associated with riparian habitats, isolated depressions on uplands such as "potholes," ponds, natural lakes, vernal pools, bogs, fens, freshwater marsh, swamps, and wet meadows. Tidal wetlands are part of the Nearshore environment, two other Strategic HGCNs in this section.

## SGCN

Wetlands are vital transitional ecosystems that form the interface between terrestrial and aquatic environments. Found in areas where water collects or flows, either permanently or seasonally, these ecosystems harness solar energy to cycle nutrients and support an exceptional diversity of life, including many endemic plant and animal species. Although commonly associated with standing water, wetlands are not always wet year-round. Their hydrology can vary widely depending on weather, temperature extremes, topography, and groundwater influence. Seasonal and annual variation is a key feature that contributes to their ecological complexity and productivity.
Wetlands perform a wide array of ecosystem functions that provide direct and indirect benefits to both natural systems and human communities. They are critical in purifying water by trapping sediments and filtering pollutants such as nitrogen and phosphorus through plant uptake and microbial processes. This natural filtration supports the health of downstream aquatic ecosystems and improves water quality for human use. Flood mitigation is another key function of wetlands. By temporarily storing excess rainfall and surface water, wetlands reduce the severity and frequency of floods. This capacity to hold water also contributes to groundwater recharge, helping maintain aquifer levels and base flows in rivers during dry periods. In coastal and riparian areas, wetlands play a role in stabilizing shorelines and reducing erosion by anchoring soils with dense vegetation.
Ecologically, wetlands are among the most productive habitats. Despite their importance, wetlands are highly vulnerable to land-use change, development, pollution, and climate impacts. Their loss or degradation can lead to diminished water quality, increased flood risk, and declines in wildlife populations. These stressors also threaten SGCN that depend on freshwater wetlands for breeding, foraging, and migratory stopovers, such as American avocet, black tern, American bittern, and Clark's and Western grebes. Recognizing, protecting, and restoring wetlands is essential to maintaining the health of ecosystems and the services they provide to society. Their plant communities form the foundation of complex food webs that support amphibians, reptiles, birds, mammals, and countless invertebrate species. Wetland vegetation provides essential resources such as cover, nesting habitat, and food for a wide range of wildlife. Herbivores rely on the nutrient-rich plant matter, while many species use wetlands for breeding, migration stopovers, or overwintering sites. Wetlands are found across Washington with unique regional hydrology and ecology that protect our water, support biodiversity, and benefit site and landscape resilience for both nature and people.

## SGCN

Closely Associated: American avocet, American bittern, American white pelican 

## Inland Dunes

Washington's inland dunes are composed of sparsely (<20% cover) to moderately vegetated (>60% cover), active and stabilized dunes and sand sheets, with vegetation cover related to the amount of annual rainfall, rate of evapotranspiration, and wind stress. 
145
 Described in Hallock et al. (2007), four simple dune types have been observed in Washington:
• Longitudinal dunes, long in proportion to width and symmetrical in cross section, form when there is a small to moderate supply of sand, much wind and little vegetation; • Transverse dunes form when there is a copious sand supply, little to moderate wind and little vegetation; • Parabolic or U-shaped dunes form when there is a moderate supply of sand, wind and vegetation, with vegetation encroachment being essential to their development; and • Climbing dunes, which climb the windward side of hills as sand sheets. Dune systems are considered "complex" if they have more than one type of simple dune present in the system.
Also described in Hallock et al. (2007), inland dunes contain three different functional stages: 1) open/migrating, 2) anchored, and 3) stabilized, with these stages differing primarily in the amount of sand movement on the dune surface and movement of the dune itself. The open/migrating stage has large areas of open active surface sand and the dune migrates with the effective wind direction and vegetation cover is minimal. Anchored dunes have active sands present on the dune surface, but movement / migration of the dune is inhibited by vegetation. Stabilized dunes lack active sands, as the dune surface is sealed with vegetation, cryptobiotic crusts, or volcanic ash. The anchored and stabilized stages are considered either "native" or "exotic" based on the dominant (>50% of relative cover) vegetation.
Common Sagebrush Lizards are ground dwelling lizards that are closely associated with inland sand dunes. More than 70 percent of this habitat type has been lost since the 1970s due to dam building, conversion, and invasive grass. Habitat for these lizards is degraded by invasive plants, such as cheatgrass, that grow densely between shrubs and eliminate bare ground. Shrubs are important for providing cover and refuge from hot summer temperatures. Common Sagebrush Lizards are gregarious and tend to be common where they occur, but their habitat has been lost, fragment, and degraded.
Excessive livestock grazing can also degrade habitat by removing too much vegetation and damaging the 
145 Hallock et al. 2007, Rocchio and
Crawford 2015
 lower limbs of shrubs. Without the lower limbs, shrubs do not provide retreats for Common Sagebrush Lizards. 
146
 Plants occupying these environments are often adapted to shifting, coarse-textured substrates (usually quartz sand) and form patchy or open grasslands, shrubsteppe, and occasionally woodlands. Hallock et al. (2007) described seven distinct plant community types associated with Washington's dunes (Appendix III), with species composition related to the degree of sand stabilization, vegetation cover and position on a particular dune; however, the authors also noted that several dune systems across inland Washington had unique species compositions that were not captured by those seven community types. Generally, the authors suggested that bunchgrasses and herbs typically dominate initial stages of dune stabilization. With increased stabilization shrubs appear, and pine and juniper may occur.
Sand dunes are by nature highly dynamic systems and patterns of plant species composition are closely related to the processes of sand erosion, deposition and dune migration and stabilization. These processes may occur rapidly, leaving legacies from previous vegetation types. While repeating patterns of vegetation are observed and allow the identification of community types, they are often present in a spatially complex, fine-scale mosaic, and the boundaries between community types range from distinct to highly blurred. Washington community types are, however, closely related to the successional processes of dune vegetation and stabilization and often have strong affinities to particular positions within a dune system. 
147
 Wind and continual supply of sand are necessary for dune dynamics. Formation of sand dunes requires a supply of well-sorted fine or medium grained (0.125-0.5 mm) sand and wind (eolian) transport. The wind moves the sand grains by saltation (bouncing) along the surface. Sand accumulates when wind passes from a rough surface to a smooth one (e.g., sand patch), flows over a depression and/or encounters a permeable obstacle such as a shrub. Dunes accumulate (accrete) sand during strong winds and lose sand during gentle winds until they reach a critical size. Once this size is attained, sand is trapped under all wind conditions due to aerodynamic factors that result in sand being dropped at the leeward margin of the dune instead of being carried off the dune. Long periods of increased precipitation and persistent presence of vegetation may lead to covering of the sand surface with litter and/or microphytic crust, soil formation and partial to complete stabilization of the dunes. In contrast, periods of drought will result in conditions unfavorable to vegetation and can reinitiate the mobility of the sands. 
148
 As of 2007, inland dunes in Washington occurred in 13 counties across eastern Washington with the largest extant systems occurring in Benton, Franklin, and Grant Counties. Hallock et al. (2007) estimated that dunes historically covered almost 450,000 acres in Washington. Extensive sand dune systems in Washington formed from wind acting on alluvial sediments derived from the Missoula floods, and glacial outburst flood-derived sands. Dune field activity and migration was slowed in the Columbia Basin approximately 3,900 years ago when cooler, moister conditions resulted in expansion of basin vegetation. Further, damming of the Columbia and Snake rivers impacted sand transport and deposition which historically fed dunes along those rivers.

## Kelp, Kelp Forest

Kelp forests are groups of brown macroalgae which provide structurally complex and highly productive intertidal and subtidal habitats. These keystone species support critical habitat for a variety of marine life, serving as nurseries, migration corridors, foraging grounds for fish and bird species, and refuge areas. They also provide habitat for vulnerable concentrations such as forage fish breeding habitat and concentration areas, while also delivering ecosystem services such as carbon sequestration, wave attenuation, primary production, and localized ocean acidification buffering. Kelp forests further support several marine SGCN including rhinoceros auklet and tufted puffin, which forage on prey associated with kelp beds, as well as northern sea otter and harbor porpoise which use these areas for foraging and refuge. 
152
 Washington has 22 species of kelp, which include canopy-forming kelp such as bull kelp (Nereocystis luetkeana) and giant kelp (Macrocystis pyrifera), as well as submerged and understory kelp such as sugar kelp (Saccharina latissima). Together, canopy-forming and understory kelps provide structure throughout the water column. Washington Department of Natural Resources maintains detailed mapping of kelp canopy and subtidal kelp forests; submerged kelp mapping can improve habitat clarity and management responsiveness. Kelp forests typically occur on rocky substrates in moderate to highenergy wave environments and extend from the lower intertidal zone to depths of approximately 30 meters, depending on turbidity and light penetration. They are unique in that they can grow on rocky reefs, where few other types of marine vegetation persist, due to their holdfast system. Kelp forests are broadly distributed along Washington's Pacific Coast, particularly around rocky substrates and along portions of the Strait of Juan de Fuca. In Puget Sound, kelp forests are more fragmented and less dense but occur in high-relief, exposed locations such as the San Juan Islands, and the west side of Whidbey Island. 
153
 The status of kelp forests is often driven by local conditions, and there are varying trends across Washington. For example, while floating kelp populations have demonstrated long-term stability along the northern Pacific Coast and Strait of Juan de Fuca, in the central and south Puget Sound kelp is no longer present for approximately 80% of shorelines where it was observed in the past. Some regions, such as Admiralty Inlet and North Puget Sound, lack sufficient historical data to make assessments of trends. Trend monitoring is primarily focused on canopy-forming kelp, as it can be assessed in aerial imagery, while understory kelp cannot. 
154
 The structure and species composition of kelp forests are maintained by a combination of factors including light availability, nutrient availability (particularly during periods of growth in spring and early summer), wave exposure, and hard substrate. 
155
 Kelp forests are experiencing stressors such as invasive seaweeds, warming temperatures, changes in sediment loading, grazing pressure from macrograzers like urchins, and shoreline development and modification. 
156
 While kelp canopy area has remained relatively stable along much of the Pacific Coast, loss and degradation in urban or ecologically stressed regions causes concern regarding long-term resilience in these areas. The loss of kelp habitat may reduce prey availability and structural refuge for seabirds, as well as marine mammals like the Northern sea otter that help maintain kelp ecosystem balance by controlling urchin populations. 

## Nearshore -Pacific Coast

The Washington Pacific Coast nearshore is the transitional zone between terrestrial, freshwater, and marine ecosystems along the outer coast of Washington. It spans from the mouth of the Columbia River to Cape Flattery at the entrance to the Strait of Juan de Fuca. The Pacific Coast nearshore includes the shorelines and shallow marine waters of the Pacific Ocean, and estuarine systems such as Willapa Bay, Grays Harbor, and the lower reaches of river deltas. The zone extends from the landward edge of marine influence-typically the top of coastal bluffs, foredunes, or cliffs adjacent to beaches, or the upper limit of tidal influence in estuaries and coastal rivers-seaward to include shallow waters and submerged lands to a depth of 100 meters. This depth allows for the inclusion of subtidal habitats as well as upwelling processes along the coastal shelf. The nearshore also includes estuarine bays to their maximum depth. 
158
 Habitats in the Pacific Coast nearshore are influenced by high wave energy, strong tidal exchange, freshwater discharge, and longshore sediment transport. Dynamic and seasonally variable currents drive upwelling in the summer to provide nutrient-rich cold water to nearshore areas. This zone includes a variety of marine riparian and shallow-water habitats such as gravel and sand beaches, rocky headlands and cliffs, tidal flats, salt marshes, eelgrass beds, dune-backed shorelines, corals and sponges, understory kelp beds, and canopy-forming kelp forests. Geomorphic systems include barrier spits, estuarine deltas, dune systems, rocky headlands, and marine terraces. Substrate characteristics, ranging from coarse cobble and bedrock to fine silts and sands, determine species composition and habitat suitability for spawning, foraging, and rearing for nearshore species. 
159
 Structural features such as large woody debris, wrack lines, boulder fields, and overhanging vegetation provide habitat complexity and refuge. The Pacific coast nearshore supports priority species including razor clams, Dungeness crab, surf smelt, sand lance, juvenile salmon, rockfish, and marine mammals such as sea otter and fur seals. It also hosts Priority Habitats and Species (PHS) vulnerable concentrations including forage fish breeding habitat and concentration areas; marine bivalve concentration areas; breeding concentrations of cormorants, storm-petrels, terns and alcids (Alcidae) such as tufted puffin and marbled murrelet; and nonbreeding concentrations of shorebirds. 
160
 Large estuaries within the Pacific Coast nearshore include Grays Harbor, Willapa Bay, and the mouth of the Columbia River (including waters west of the Astoria-Megler Bridge), with smaller estuaries lining the coast. Estuaries are semi-enclosed bodies of water that have free connection with the open ocean, and they provide high-productivity nursery habitat for juvenile salmonids, waterfowl, shorebirds, and shellfish. Tidal channels and habitats such as mudflats, salt marshes, and oyster reefs support diverse plant communities and offer spawning, feeding, and rearing grounds for fish, birds, and mammals. Tides play a notable role in the ecology of Pacific Coast estuaries, as at least half of the surface area in Grays Harbor and Willapa Bay is intertidal. 
161
 The Pacific Coast nearshore provides ecosystem services including wave attenuation, shoreline stabilization, sediment retention, carbon sequestration, and nutrient cycling. However, these functions are increasingly threatened by shoreline armoring, estuarine filling, invasive species, altered sediment 
158
 supply, and the impacts of sea level rise. These stressors also threaten SGCN that depend on nearshore and estuarine habitats, reducing available nesting, foraging, and nursery areas for sensitive fish, bird, and mammal populations. Shoreline development and coastal infrastructure can disrupt nearshore processes, leading to erosion, habitat loss, and reduced ecological function. 162
Shore: Also called the marine riparian zone, shore habitat extends inland from the Ordinary High Water Mark (OHWM) to that portion of the terrestrial landscape that is influenced by, or that directly influences, the aquatic ecosystem. The shore takes in feeder bluffs (i.e., eroding bluffs), as they are an important source of sediments that form and sustain beaches. Shores consisting of native vegetation (e.g., trees, shrubs, dune grasses), fine-grained sand, imbedded large woody debris, or actively eroding bluffs are of particular importance. Sandy beach coastlines are the only habitat type utilized by Western snowy plovers and coastal populations of streaked horned lark. Both species face risks from habitat loss, climate change, and human disturbance impacting this habitat. Headlands with concentrated seabird use and marine mammal haulout sites are also significant.
Intertidal: Extends from the OHWM to the extreme lower low water (ELLW). Intertidal areas consisting of rocky substrate, native vegetation (e.g., eelgrass macroalgae, emergent vegetation) or habitatforming species (e.g., goose-necked barnacles, mussel beds, oyster reefs) are of particular importance.
Intertidal areas within a river/stream delta or an area used for spawning by forage fish are also significant.
Subtidal: Extends from ELLW to -100 meters. Subtidal areas within an estuarine embayment or areas consisting of rocky substrate, native vegetation (e.g., eelgrass, macroalgae), or habitat-forming species (e.g., corals, sponges) are of particular importance. 

## Nearshore -Puget Sound/Salish Sea

The Puget Sound nearshore is the transitional zone between terrestrial, freshwater, and marine ecosystems (Cereghino et al. 2012). It encompasses areas of the Puget Sound, the Strait of Juan de Fuca, Admiralty Inlet, the San Juan Islands, and Hood Canal. The Puget Sound nearshore extends from the landward edge of marine influence-typically the top of coastal bluffs adjacent to beaches, or the upper limit of tidal influence in estuaries, rivers, and streams-seaward to the maximum depth of Puget Sound (EnviroVision et al. 2010, Schlenger et al. 2011). The Puget Sound nearshore also includes estuaries and bays extending to their full depth. These nearshore waters provide essential foraging habitats for several SGCN, including Cassin's auklet, gray whale, Caspian tern, Brandt's cormorant, and rhinoceros auklet.
Habitats in the Puget Sound nearshore are affected by tidal exchange, wave energy, freshwater input, sediment transport, and marine and terrestrial-derived nutrients (Simenstad et al. 2011). This zone includes a variety of marine riparian and shallow-water habitats, including mudflats, salt marshes, eelgrass beds, understory kelp beds, canopy-forming kelp forests, pocket estuaries, and barrier beaches. Geomorphic systems in the Puget Sound nearshore include rocky coasts, beaches, embayments, and large river deltas (Shipman 2008, Shipman 2014); substrate characteristics determine species composition and suitability of habitat for spawning, foraging and rearing for nearshore species.
Structural features such as large woody debris, wrack lines, shellfish beds, and overhanging vegetation provide habitat complexity and refuge. Puget Sound nearshore areas support priority species including juvenile salmon and forage fish during critical life stages, as well as juvenile rockfish, juvenile Pacific Cod, and many other species of groundfish during their early life history stages (Love et al. 2002, Clancy 2009). These nearshore waters provide essential foraging habitats for several bird and mammal SGCN, including Cassin's auklet, gray whale, Caspian tern, Brandt's cormorant, and rhinoceros auklet.
Pocket estuaries and tidal channels are of particular importance for juvenile salmonids, providing lowsalinity refuge and high productivity feeding grounds (Clancy 2009, EnviroVision et al. 2010). The Puget Sound nearshore also provides habitat for vulnerable concentrations, such as forage fish breeding habitat and concentration areas, western Washington breeding concentrations of cormorants, stormpetrels, terns and alcids, and nonbreeding concentrations of shorebirds.
The Puget Sound nearshore provides ecosystem services including wave attenuation and storm protection, carbon sequestration, and water quality regulation. However, shoreline modification degrades nearshore processes and services including sediment supply to beaches, accumulation of woody debris, terrestrial-marine connectivity, and migration corridors for salmon and forage fish (Cereghino et al. 2012, HSIL 2021). Approximately 29% of Puget Sound's 2,500-mile shoreline is armored (HSIL 2021). Coastal development, paired with shoreline modification, inhibits the services offered by the nearshore, particularly with the increasing impacts of sea level rise leading to coastal squeeze of nearshore habitats (Clancy 2009). The loss and modification of nearshore habitats reduce critical foraging and nesting sites for seabirds and shorebirds.
Priority habitat zones within the Puget Sound nearshore are:
Shore: Also called the marine riparian zone, shore habitat extends inland from the Ordinary High Water Mark (OHWM) to that portion of the terrestrial landscape that is influenced by, or that directly influences, the aquatic ecosystem. The shore takes in feeder bluffs (i.e., eroding bluffs), as they are an important source of sediments that form and sustain beaches. Shores consisting of native vegetation (e.g., trees, shrubs, dune grasses), fine-grained sand, imbedded large woody debris and wrack, and actively eroding bluffs are of particular importance. Headlands with concentrated seabird use and marine mammal haulout sites are also significant.
Intertidal: Extends from the OHWM to the extreme lower low water (ELLW). Intertidal areas consisting of rocky substrate, native vegetation (e.g., eelgrass, macroalgae, emergent vegetation) or habitatforming species (e.g., native oyster reefs) are of particular importance. Intertidal areas within a river/stream delta, estuarine embayment, or a pocket beach, or an area used for spawning by forage fish are also significant. Intertidal flats and beaches also provide vital foraging and roosting habitat for SGCN shorebirds including red knot and marbled godwit, which depend on these areas during migration and wintering periods.
Subtidal: Extends from ELLW to the maximum depth of Puget Sound. Subtidal areas consisting of rocky substrate, native vegetation (e.g., eelgrass, macroalgae), or habitat-forming species (e.g., sea pens, native oyster reefs) are of particular importance. Subtidal areas within an estuarine embayment or an area used for spawning by forage fish are also significant. where scattered pine or fir comprise the upper canopy. Shrubsteppe vegetation may be prominent in some stands and create a distinct tree/shrub/sparse grassland habitat. The understory of oak woodlands in eastern Washington may include dense stands of shrubs or, more often, be dominated by bunchgrasses, sedges, or forbs. In the Columbia River Gorge, Oregon white oak can be found in dense patches often associated with grassland or shrubland balds within a closed Douglas-fir overstory forest landscape. Oregon white oak woodlands on the eastside of Washington occur at elevations ranging from 1,500 to 6,300 feet. Oregon white oak woodlands in western Washington are most prevalent on gravelly outwash plains and on dry sites that historically experienced frequent pre-settlement fires. They occur as either large or small patches. The presence of Oregon white oak either as single species patches or where mixed with conifers characterizes these woodlands. Native shrubs such as snowberry (Symphoricarpos albus), Oregon grape (Mahonia aquifolium), Serviceberry (Amelanchier alnifolia) and the nonnative Scot's broom (Cytisus scoparius) are common in the understory. Many prairie grasses and forb species will also occur in the understory of oak savannas and open woodlands in western 
Washington. 166
 Oregon white oak woodlands are among North America's most imperiled vegetation types. Estimates suggest that the extent of oak woodlands account for less than 10 percent of their historic range before non-indigenous settlement. Almost all contemporary woodlands dominated by Oregon white oak occur in an early successional state due to human disturbances (e.g., cultural burning by indigenous peoples, natural fires, or prescribed fire). In the absence of fire, Oregon white oak is usually excluded or their growth becomes stunted with the presence of faster growing conifers. Since the suppression of burning, more than half of the pre-settlement oak savannas and open woodlands are now dense forests of Douglas-fir, grand fir (Abies grandis), and bigleaf maple (Acer macrophyllum). In some parts of the East Cascades, fire suppression combined with grazing has influenced fine fuel production and has led to encroachment by conifers and establishment of dense patches of small, shrubby oaks. Expansion of residential and commercial development has also replaced oak woodlands and has led to a significant decline in the quantity and quality of Oregon white oak habitat. All these impacts have since left only fragmented remnants and have dramatically reduced the extent of this once-expansive ecosystem.
foot_27

## Riparian and Floodplain

Riparian areas are transitional between terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems and are distinguished by gradients in biophysical conditions, ecological processes, and biota. They are areas through which surface and subsurface hydrology connect waterbodies with their adjacent uplands. They include those portions of terrestrial ecosystems that significantly influence exchanges of energy and matter with aquatic ecosystems (i.e., a zone of influence).
Riparian ecosystems are sometimes referred to by different names: riparian areas, riparian zones, riparian corridor, or riparian habitats. To compound confusion, the "riparian zone" is sometimes identified as a distinctive area within riparian ecosystems. 168 Some definitions include the adjacent waters whereas others do not. Unless stated otherwise, the SWAP considers riparian area, riparian habitat, riparian corridor, and riparian ecosystem to be synonyms; riparian ecosystem does not include adjacent waters (i.e., rivers or streams); and, riparian zone is a distinctive area within riparian ecosystems. Riparian contains wet or moist soils and plants adapted to growing conditions associated with periodically saturated soils.
Riparian ecosystems are HGCN in part because wildlife occurs more often and in greater variety in riparian areas than in any other habitat type: "Natural riparian corridors are the most diverse, dynamic, and complex biophysical habitats on the terrestrial portion of the earth." 
169
 Although riparian ecosystems constitute a small portion of the surface landscape, approximately 85% of Washington's wildlife species are known to use riparian areas associated with rivers and streams (Knutson and Naef 1997). Of these, 170 species including 134 mollusks, 11 amphibians, 3 reptiles, 10 birds, and 9 mammals may be riparian obligates, i.e., require riparian habitat to survive. 
170
 In addition, habitat for many upland and aquatic species is directly enhanced by the presence of adjacent riparian areas. A principal reason for high fish and wildlife diversity is that riparian ecosystems are exceptionally productive. Riparian ecosystems are characterized by the availability of water, milder microclimate, and relatively fertile 
168 Sedell et al. 1989;
Naiman et al. 1992;
Steiner et al. 1994
 169 Naiman et al. 1993 soils. These factors enhance the productivity of plant communities and support a complex food web that includes a rich variety and abundance of fish, invertebrates, amphibians, plants, bacteria, fungi, reptiles, birds, and mammals. 
171
 Riparian areas help provide a variety of ecosystem services: provisioning food and water; regulating flood flows; supporting nutrient cycling, sediment and pollutant filtering, and carbon sequestration; and providing cultural services such as recreational, spiritual, and other nonmaterial benefits. 172 This is a vital "in-between" zone where land and water meet that directly influences the water and the life within it by affecting things like shade, water temperature, structure and nutrient inputs (e.g., log falls, log jams, leaf litter, surface water flow into the aquatic system), and potentially the removal of some pollutants before they reach the water.

## Conservation Actions

Conservation actions for riparian and floodplain ecosystems are woven throughout the SWAP as they are directly connected to the success of so many other conservation actions. The Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs sections contain conservation priorities for this ecosystem:
• Habitat Connectivity 

## Seagrass, Seagrass Meadows

Seagrass meadows in Washington are composed primarily of eelgrass and surfgrass. These flowering marine plants anchor in intertidal and shallow subtidal environments, forming complex benthic habitats that stabilize sediment and improve water quality. They are highly productive nearshore habitats and support a wide array of fish, waterfowl, and invertebrate species. Seagrass meadows also support vulnerable concentrations such as forage fish breeding habitat and concentration areas; breeding concentrations of cormorants, storm-petrels, terns, and alcids; and nonbreeding concentrations of loons, grebes, cormorants, fulmar, shearwaters, storm-petrels, and alcids.
Seagrass meadows also hold significant cultural value for Tribal Nations in Washington. These habitats support first foods such as herring, shellfish, and salmon, serving as part of the ecological basis for traditional fishing practices and seasonal gathering activities. 
173
 Many Tribal communities recognize eelgrass as vital to cultural continuity, treaty-reserved rights, and long-term environmental stewardship, leading restoration efforts and replanting projects. 
174
 Surfgrass (Phyllospadix spp.) is typically found in wave-exposed, rocky intertidal and shallow subtidal areas, sometimes co-occurring with kelp forests. Surfgrass thrives in regions such as the Strait of Juan de Fuca, the San Juan Islands, and isolated coastal headlands. While not as extensive in distribution as eelgrass, surfgrasses are ecologically important for stabilizing sediments in high-energy environments and providing habitat for invertebrates and juvenile fish. 
175
 Eelgrass is the most widespread seagrass in Washington. Native eelgrass (Zostera marina) establishes in shallow, protected waters with soft sediment and can be found in areas of the Puget Sound along with Pacific Coast estuaries, including Willapa Bay and Grays Harbor. In the Puget Sound, approximately half of eelgrass grows on tidal flats, while the other half grows within small beds along the shore. Eelgrass performs a variety of ecosystem services including water quality enhancement, ocean acidification mitigation, and algal bloom control. Eelgrass meadows also serve as efficient carbon sinks due to their high primary productivity and low decomposition rates. In addition to the native Z. marina, there is a non-native species (Nanozostera japonica) of eelgrass in Washington. Non-native eelgrass generally occupies the high intertidal zone, while native eelgrass is typically in the high subtidal and low intertidal. Although they generally occupy different tidal elevations, the two species overlap in occurrence in some areas. N. japonica is commonly seen in the Central Puget Sound and Hood Canal. 
176
 There is uncertainty regarding the interaction between native and non-native eelgrass, as some studies find establishment of non-native eelgrass can inhibit the establishment of Z. marina in restoration sites, while others find that the presence of N. japonica allows Z. marina to move further up the intertidal. 
177
 The condition of seagrass meadows has direct implications for nearshore food webs that support SGCN. Declines in eelgrass extent can reduce prey availability for seabirds and marine mammals that depend on nearshore forage fish and invertebrates concentrated in these vegetated habitats.
While limitations in historic data records makes quantifying eelgrass losses challenging, monitoring efforts in the Puget Sound indicate that eelgrass area has declined over recent years, potentially due in part to marine heat waves in 2015, 2016, and 2019. Interannual variability in eelgrass also creates monitoring challenges, and while there is evidence of stability over larger time frames across the Puget Sound, there is significant variability at smaller spatial scales. Sites with declines in eelgrass in the San Juan Islands and the Strait of Juan de Fuca outnumbered sites with increases in recent (2016-2020) monitoring efforts, with eelgrass located in embayments showing higher vulnerability. The Skagit delta has also shown a significant decline in eelgrass populations with sites showing a decrease of over 200 hectares between 2004 and 2020. 
178
 Localized declines are due to several stressors including marine heat waves, nutrient runoff, habitat conversion through dredging and filling, shading from overwater structures, and shoreline armoring. Given the need to support belowground tissues, seagrasses require more light when compared to other marine vegetation. As a result, stressors such as shading and algal blooms have a greater impact on them. Sea level rise also poses the threat of coastal squeeze (i.e., the inability of eelgrass habitat to migrate landward due to barriers like modified shorelines or coastal development). Protecting and restoring seagrass meadows is therefore critical not only for maintaining ecosystem services but also for supporting those SGCN whose life histories are tightly linked to nearshore productivity. 179

## Conservation -Broadly, implement the Kelp Forest and Eelgrass Meadow Health and Conservation

Prioritization Plan and support social science-driven approaches to improve human engagement and behavior shifts to improve seagrass conservation. 

## Shrubsteppe

Shrubsteppe is an arid to semi-arid ecosystem that typically comprises a combination of perennial grasses, forbs, or shrubs; one or all of these structural types may be at a given site depending on recent disturbance, soil characteristics and climate. Although an occasional tree may be present, typically the lack of moisture and/or frequency of fire prevents trees from becoming dominant. Key grasses include, but are not limited to, Idaho fescue, bluebunch wheatgrass, Sandberg bluegrass, Thurber's needlegrass, and needle-and-thread. Big sagebrush is the most abundant shrub, but other species such as antelope bitterbrush, three-tip sagebrush, stiff sagebrush, dwarf sagebrush, and rubber rabbitbrush can be dominant or co-dominant. Undisturbed habitat typically has shrub canopy cover between 5% and 30% cover. 
180
 Shrubsteppe often supports a diverse layer of forbs (i.e., broadleaf herbaceous flora) such as balsamroot, lupine, buckwheat, yarrow, and phlox. Shrubsteppe ecosystems also tend to have biological soil crusts (cyanobacteria, mosses, and/or lichens) depending on management and disturbance history. At disturbed sites, invasive non-native species such as Dalmatian toadflax, knapweed, cheatgrass may be abundant, while other non-natives like intermediate wheatgrass, and crested wheatgrass may be prevalent after previous seedings aimed at improving grass density. Soil crusts benefits habitat by locking in soil moisture, reducing erosion, and by increasing the soil's nutrients and productivity and help prevent the establishment and spread of invasive plants such as cheatgrass. 
181
 Shrubsteppe disturbed especially by fire or grazing/browsing may lack perennial grass, forbs, or shrubs during periods of post-disturbance recovery.
The primary mechanisms maintaining the characteristics and composition of shrubsteppe are climate, soils, and historically, fire, as a natural disturbance. Precipitation in Washington's semi-arid shrubsteppe zone occurs mainly in late autumn and winter, with annual precipitation ranging from 6 inches in the lowest parts of the Columbia Plateau to 22 inches in higher elevations near the transition with forested zones; limited water in shrubsteppe constrains the growth of trees. Winters are relatively cold, and by late spring, rainfall diminishes rapidly, and summers are dry and hot, which makes the water-holding capacity of shrubsteppe soils a significant influence on native plant assemblages. The variation in this soil characteristic across the landscape contributes to the diversity of plant species and assemblages in shrubsteppe. The water holding capacity of these soils influences the native plant assemblages more than the chemical or profile characteristics of the soil. 
182
 Although lithosol soils do not retain much moisture, these soils support a diverse array of wildflowers. Sandier soils in areas with lower precipitation support dry-land grasses, such as Needle-and-thread and Indian ricegrass, while deeper soils with moderate precipitation support bluebunch wheatgrass and sagebrush. Finally, fire is a natural and important part of shrubsteppe ecosystems, historically playing a large role in creating a mosaic of stands of different sizes in various seral conditions. 
183
 Fire severity and frequency prior to European settlement varied among different plant associations and site characteristics and likely returned to sites at intervals of 10 to more than 200 years. This temporal and spatial variation helped maintain a patchy distribution of shrubs, both locally and across landscapes.
The large number (53) and broad diversity of SGCN that are closely associated with shrubsteppe vegetation communities really emphasize the habitat importance of that system and conservation need in Washington. These SGCN include those that are federal listed as endangered, like the Columbia Basin pygmy rabbit, as well as state listed and candidate species such as the greater sage-grouse, Columbian sharp-tailed grouse, burrowing owl, Washington ground squirrel, Townsend's ground squirrel, striped whipsnake, Northern leopard frog, tiger salamander, and monarch butterfly.
Shrubsteppe extends throughout many western states and into south-central British Columbia. In Washington, it occurs throughout the Columbia Plateau and into the surrounding higher elevations regions. 
184
 Once covering more than 10 million acres in Eastern Washington, a significant portion of the shrubsteppe landscape has been lost or degraded. Private land ownership by colonists displaced indigenous communities and significantly altered the landscape through introduction of livestock, development of an engineered irrigation network and then hydro-electric power production, and conversion to agricultural uses. 
185
 Quantifying estimates of shrubsteppe loss is difficult as assessments of historical presence are likely inaccurate and estimation methodologies vary. 
186
 However, even during the mid-1990s, WDFW estimated that nearly 60% of the original shrubsteppe habitat in Washington had been converted to other landcover. 
187
 More recently, Miller et al. (2011) 
190
 The transition to non-native annuals has contributed to movement further away from pre-1800 fire return intervals, even in the last 25 years.
As the climate changes, eastern Washington is facing even hotter and drier summers than were historically typical, and the state, like others, is experiencing extreme weather events, prolonged droughts and heatwaves. These stressors put more demand on moisture-limited systems and, over time influence shifts in vegetation composition and abundance. Stressors and vegetation shifts increase the likelihood that fire, which was a natural and primary driver in creating and maintaining shrubsteppe habitat variation, will have catastrophic impacts to habitat and wildlife. Current fire return intervals are roughly 10 years or less, especially in cheatgrass dominated areas and some shrubsteppe plants simply cannot persist at that frequency. For example, some slow-growing shrubs (e.g., Wyoming big sagebrush) do not easily reestablish post-fire and cannot develop critical structural characteristics important to dependent wildlife (e.g., shrub-nesting birds and big game). 
191
 The average size of fire has increased; based on fire data from the Washington Department of Natural Resources, Downes reported that the number of fires that burned more than 5,000 acres, increased in all size categories from 2000-2010 to 2011-2020; fires greater than 5,000 acres increased more than 200% in the 2011-2020 decade; and fires greater than 50,000 increased more than 650%. 
192
 Fires are recurring in the same areas, spanning 189 WDFW, WA DNR, and WSCC 2024 (WSRRI)
190 Pilliod et al. 2017 
191 Baker 2006
, Whisenant 1990
, Updike et al. 1990
, Vander Haegen et al. 2001
 shrubsteppe types. 
193
 Over the last century, there has been extensive fire suppression and prohibition of Indigenous burning practices 194 due to social and political changes that have prioritized suppressing all wildland fires as soon as they occur. This suppression has contributed to altering natural fire cycles by producing atypically dense shrub stands that can increase fire severity (e.g., extent, temperature, impact) when they burn. Atypically dense shrub cover, land-use changes, the spread of invasive grass species, and prolonged drought, have collectively contributed to uncharacteristic wildfire regimes in this landscape. Such fires damage soils and seed stocks reducing or eliminating the potential for native species recovery without human-assisted restoration.

## Streams -Headwaters and Instream

Headwater streams, typically defined as first-order channels with no upstream tributaries, are critical to the functioning and integrity of freshwater ecosystems. These small streams may exhibit perennial, intermittent, or ephemeral flow regimes, depending on local hydrologic and climatic conditions, and are often sourced from conveyances, natural drainages, or springs. Despite their size, headwater streams play a disproportionately large role in supporting downstream water quality, regulating flow regimes, and sustaining ecological productivity. These systems contribute essential nutrients to downstream waters and support diverse communities of fish, wildlife, and native vegetation. Their physical, chemical, and biological heterogeneity creates a range of microhabitats that promote species richness and ecological resilience. For aquatic organisms (particularly fish, amphibians, and invertebrates), headwater streams offer clean, cold water with distinct mineral and quality characteristics based on geology, soils, and streamside vegetation. These geographically different compositions influence species-specific imprinting that guides natal homing behavior crucial for reproduction and, in some cases, very specific water quality tolerances for organisms that live in these waters. Hydrologically, headwater streams account for the majority of stream miles in many watersheds and play an essential role in attenuating surface runoff, thereby reducing downstream flood risks. They initiate hyporheic exchange and drive nutrient cycling processes, particularly within intact riparian wetlands and floodplain complexes. These areas also contribute to climate regulation through carbon sequestration in densely vegetated zones. Alterations in water chemistry can disrupt larval development and orientation and adult fitness with cascading effects on population viability. Impaired water quality can reduce macroinvertebrate abundance, including several SGCN snails, streamflies, freshwater mussels, and dragonflies, which also limits a foundational food source for many aquatic species. Headwater stream health influences downstream instream habitats.
Natural complexity throughout a stream system provides necessary processes and structure for aquatic life, and that is unique watershed to watershed. Topography, climate, geology, hydrology and geomorphology is different throughout the state. The headwaters and instream habitats of the Skagit River are completely different from those in the Crab Creek watershed, with completely different ecosystems and flow regimes influenced by different types of log fall from riparian habitats, log jams, boulders or boulder clusters, gravel, riffles, pools, fine sediment, cover structures, alcoves, and side channels that support riverine biodiversity and ecological function. And, the successful function of these habitats within those stream systems depends on water availability to produce, maintain and sustain instream habitats and the aquatic life that depends on and influences them.
Instream habitats provide a wide range of essential biotic functions that sustain ecological integrity and biodiversity. These habitats support the full life cycle needs of aquatic organisms, including spawning, rearing, and refuge areas for fish; growth and reproduction sites for macroinvertebrate communities; and critical developmental environments for amphibians and turtles. These habitats and the species they support are essential to complex food webs that connect terrestrial and aquatic species, influencing population resilience and ecosystem health far beyond the edges of the water. Terrestrial wildlife also depends on these systems. These streams provide reliable freshwater, foraging opportunities, and seasonal refuge. Their riparian zones and adjacent floodplains offer important resources for migratory species, including habitat for pollination, grazing, browsing, and overwintering. In many cases, these areas serve as breeding and rearing sites due to the abundance and diversity of native vegetation and prey species. Functioning headwater streams and instream habitats sustain riparian and downstream food webs, integrate habitat functions across ecosystem boundaries, and contribute to the overall productivity and stability of watershed systems.
Streams are increasingly vulnerable to anthropogenic pressures. Land-use changes such as forest practices, agriculture at higher elevations, water withdrawals, and infrastructure development disrupt stream connectivity and hydrologic function. Inappropriate grazing, vegetation removal, and recreational impacts can degrade riparian conditions and reduce ecosystem resilience. Changing largerscale environmental conditions like extreme temperatures, prolonged drought, decreased snowpack, or earlier snow melt can further alter hydrologic patterns and increase stress on sensitive species. Protecting headwater streams is essential to maintaining ecological integrity, biodiversity, and water resources downstream, throughout the watershed, and across hydrologically connected systems.

## Conservation -Broadly, to safeguard headwater systems and the instream habitats that they support, conservation efforts should prioritize maintaining

• natural flow regimes • riparian systems, and • instream habitat connectivity.
Conservation actions related to headwater streams, instream habitats, and stream-dependent SGCN are featured throughout the entire SWAP, highlighted in nearly every Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Need, and in many SGCN conservation actions. They are not repeated here for brevity. County. 197
 Westside prairie is an herbaceous, non-forested plant community. It is fire-adapted and historically maintained by indigenous peoples who intentionally burned prairie to prevent encroachment from invading trees and shrubs, increase the growth of food plants such as camas, and improve habitat for game species. 
198
 In Washington, there are two types, dry prairie and wet prairie. The more common dry prairie occurs on deep, gravelly, well-drained glacial soils in bottomlands, along valley margins, and in lower foothills. In parts of Puget Lowlands, prairie can sometimes be recognized by mounded topography, such as at Mima Mounds Natural Area Preserve. Wet prairie in Puget Sound is usually found on glacial outwash soils in swales or low-gradient riparian areas, while in southwest Washington they lie mostly on clay-rich soils that are saturated to the surface during the early part of the growing season. 
199
 Hydrology plays a central role in wet prairie function, influencing plant composition, phenology, and restoration outcomes. 
200
 Native bunchgrass, sedge, and forb vegetation typify westside prairie in Washington. Dry, upland prairie vegetation consists of low growing native grasses and forbs. The most common native grasses in dry prairies are Roemer's fescue (Festuca roemeri) and California oatgrass (Danthonia californica). Prairie is also populated by a rich mix of native wildflowers such as common camas (Camassia quamash), Golden Paintbrush (Castilleja levisecta), and lupine (Lupinus spp.). Mosses, lichens, and bare ground may also be found in the spaces between the grasses and cover of wildflowers. Wet prairie is dominated by a highly diverse community of grasses and sedges and to a lesser degree by forbs or shrubs. 
201
 Native westside prairies are among the most endangered ecosystems in the United States. Present-day dry prairie in the Pacific Northwest covers less than 10 percent of its historic range, while wet prairie has been nearly extirpated from both the Willamette Valley and South Puget Sound. The majority of extant westside prairie in Historically, frequent fires prevented the establishment of shrubs and trees (Rocchio and Crawford 2015; pp 201). The lack of fires as a result of fire suppression and the cessation of cultural burning on westside prairies over the last 100 years has allowed Douglas fir trees and shrubs to encroach, except on the driest sites. This encroachment can cause prairies and grasslands to become forests and shrublands. Reintroducing fire through controlled burning can help to set back succession and limit tree and shrub encroachment. Nonnative species are prominent in westside prairie and generally increase after ground-disturbing activities (Rocchio and Crawford 2015; pp 201). Manual (hand pulling), mechanical (mowing), and herbicide treatments followed by the installation and monitoring of native plants is a strategy for reducing or eliminating nonnative plants in westside prairie (Nolan and Carver 2011; pp 51-55). Nonnative species are prominent in westside prairie and generally increase after ground-disturbing activities (Rocchio and Crawford 2015; pp 201). The reintroduction of fire (as part of a comprehensive restoration plan) can be used to support the reduction or elimination of nonnative and invasive plant species from westside prairie (Nolan and Carver 2011; pp 55)
. 7.1 Fire & Fire Suppression 1.2 Ecosystem & Natural Process (Re)Creation
Historically, frequent fires prevented the establishment of shrubs and trees (Rocchio and Crawford 2015; pp 201). The lack of fires as a result of fire suppression and the cessation of cultural burning on westside prairies over the last 100 years has allowed Douglas fir trees to encroach, except on the driest sites. This encroachment can cause prairies and grasslands to become forests. Fire suppression has also led to increases in shrub cover and the transformation of some prairies to shrublands. Reintroducing fire through controlled burning can help to set back succession and limit tree and shrub encroachment (Rocchio and Crawford 2015; pp 201)

HGCN Features Distribution: All of these features are widely distributed and important wherever they occur, regardless of larger habitat context. For example, snags are a Specialized Feature HGCN and important for several SGCN (e.g., slender-billed white-breasted nuthatch, Barrow's goldeneye). Snags occur in all ecoregions and are important for many species, beyond SGCN such as all cavity-nesting ducks, woodpeckers, tree swallows, and chickadees. HGCN Features are best identified during projectplanning by experts familiar with those species and structures.

7
). These features are important because of the functions they provide in many types of habitats.

 Similar to colonial breeding HGCN, several migratory bird stopovers habitat types are PHS (e.g., wetlands, riparian, nearshore) and also captured as non-breeding vulnerable concentrations of waterfowl or shorebirds. Migratory bird stopovers are important features for warblers, sparrows, and other songbirds which are not in PHS as a concentration. 206 WNHP 2024.
