The following sections describe the SWAP Specialized Feature HGCN and conservation recommendations. SGCN Actions tables also highlight some of these features where relevant (Conservation Issues and Actions section).

## Bat Roosts, Maternity Colonies, and Hibernacula

Bats play a critical role in Washington's ecosystems as primary nocturnal insect predators, and have a role in controlling agriculture and forestry pests. They save billions of dollars in the US on pesticide costs and crop damage. 
207
 Bat roosts, maternity colonies, and hibernacula occur in all ecoregions and in a variety of habitats throughout Washington. Bat hibernacula and maternity colonies are considered vulnerable concentrations. Bats group together in a variety of habitats for thermoregulation, safety, sociality, and to reduce energy expenditures and are often most vulnerable while in these colonies. Gathering size can vary depending on the species; however, some species do not aggregate in colonies and rather roost solitarily, most notably the silver-haired bat and hoary bat. Protecting this habitat is critical to species survivorship and population resiliency. 
208
 Many bat species in Washington hibernate during the winter to survive when there are limited foraging resources. Bats are vulnerable during this time, when disturbed, bats will burn critical fat reserves that are needed to survive through the winter, which can lead to poor health and even mortality. Bats will use a variety of habitats for hibernation selecting based on microclimate needs, distance to water and foraging habitat as well as protection from wind, weather and predators. 
209
 Examples of important habitat for hibernation in Washington include cliffs, talus, caves, snags, abandoned mines, human-made structures, and root wads in old growth-mature forests (underlined habitats are also stand-alone Feature HGCN and benefit other species).
Bats will aggregate outside of hibernation and maternity season, in transitional, swarming, and night roosts. Though reproduction and hibernation are not occurring at these roosts, they are vital to sustain healthy bat populations. Night roosts provide safe and protected places while bats rest between bouts of foraging. Without this habitat, foraging success decreases leading to poor health and possible mortality. Transitional roosts provide temporary safe and protected places as bats prepare for hibernation (fall) or pregnancy (spring), or rest during migration. 
210
 Transitional roosts improve survivorship and bats' ability to successfully reproduce. Swarming roosts are where bats mate and socialize, and these are crucial for reproduction success. 
211
 All of Washington's bat species use transitional, swarming, and night roosts in a variety of habitats such as, cliffs, caves, snags, bridges, bat boxes, abandoned mines, buildings, and suitable trees in old growth-mature forests, aspen stands, oak 
207
 Boyles et al. 2011 
208 Fenton 1997
, Kunz 1982
 209 Weller et al. 2018 210 Anthony et al. 1981, Barclay 1982 woodlands, and other forest types. Habitat type and structure will vary by bat species, life history, and season. 

## Caves

Caves are a priority habitat in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species program (WDFW 2008). They are defined in PHS as "naturally occurring cavity, recess, void, or system of interconnected passages (including associated dendritic tubes, cracks, and fissures) which occurs under the earth in soils, rock, ice, or other geological formations, and is large enough to contain a human. Mine shafts (a human-made excavation in the earth usually used to extract minerals) may mimic caves and abandoned mine shafts with actual or suspected occurrences of priority species should be treated in a manner similar to caves."
Caves generally provide a stable environment (Benedict 1979, Holsinger 1988) with an overall climate less variable than at the surface (Poulson and White 1969). Within caves are areas and passages that exhibit different conditions with regard to light and climate. These areas are termed the entrance, twilight, and dark zones. Caves generally contain little or no vegetation inside (Culver 1982). Cave vegetation is limited to the entrance and, to a lesser extent, the twilight zones (Senger and Crawford 1984). Cave system dark zones contain no primary producing plants (Vandel 1965, Culver 1982), although chemosynthetic autotrophs may be present (Barr 1967).
Bats are strongly associated with caves, and cave-dwelling bats are indeed critical components of cave ecosystems (Baker et al. 2015). Bats are also extremely important contributors to terrestrial ecosystems. All bats utilize terrestrial environments for foraging and hunting, though only a portion of bat species occupies caves (Baker et al. 2015). Another species that is also associated with caves for all or a portion of their life include American pika (Ochotona princeps).
Threats to cave systems and the animals which inhabit them are most often a result of human activities (Baker et al. 2015). This includes disturbance to caves from human intrusions into these fragile ecosystems, such as recreation, vandalism, and illegal garbage disposal. Modification of entrances (such as digging out the opening to make it broader, removing vegetation around the mouth of the cave, diverting water flow) and the ground directly above caves (such as paving or creating impervious cover, logging or clearing, fire, quarrying) can impact the function of habitat provided by cave ecosystems (Baker et al. 2015) not only physically but chemically. These create disturbances from natural and artificial light intrusion, pollutant inputs, change in water quantity and quality of flow or infiltration, and may increase human traffic. NVC Group(s) and/or GET Functional Group(s) Aerobic Caves (S1.1)
Human-created or -modified groups that can function as this habitat: Anthropogenic Subterranean Voids (S2.1)

Threats to caves and the animals which inhabit them are invariably a result of human activities caused by intrusion into caves (Baker, Taylor, Thomas, Lavoie, Olson, Barton, et al. 2015; pp 3). Strategy to address this problem should include outreach via tools such as signage and outreach to local organizations to get the word out about sensitive caves. 9.6 Light, Noise, Thermal Pollution 6.5 Site Infrastructure Threats to caves and the animals which inhabit them are invariably a result of human activities (Baker, Taylor, Thomas, Lavoie, Olson, Barton, et al. 2015; pp 3). These intrusions occur through recreation, vandalism, and illegal garbage disposal. Modification of entrances and natural and artificial light intrusion also impact cave habitat function. ## Site Infrastructure

Threats to caves and the animals which inhabit them are invariably a result of human activities (Baker, Taylor, Thomas, Lavoie, Olson, Barton, et al. 2015; pp 3). These intrusions occur through recreation, vandalism, and illegal garbage disposal. Modification of entrances and natural and artificial light intrusion also impact cave habitat function. One strategy to address this problem is to create barriers to exclude human (not wildlife) entrance to caves. 

## Issue

## Cliffs

Cliffs are barren or sparsely vegetated ecosystems found on steep rock faces. Dominant parent materials include basalt in eastern Washington (particularly in the Columbia Basin) and various igneous, sedimentary, and metamorphic bedrock types of rock in the montane regions of western Washington (Rocchio and Crawford 2015). Cliffs often occur near other sparsely vegetated rock features including unstable scree and talus slopes. Species richness of cliff plant communities appeared to be controlled by aspect, microsite size, and cliff surface roughness (Graham and Knight 2004). Diversity increases when cliff microhabitats are compressed into a small area. Unfractured cliffs with no rooting space for vascular plants is habitat for lichens which is often next to a ledge where accumulated organic matter, minerals and water support grasses, sedges or small trees (Larson et al. 2000). Cliffs, in general, support high endemism of plants and can be refugia for old trees (Larson et al. 2000). This ecosystem usually occurs in inaccessible locations and thus is protected from most disturbances resulting from human activities (Rocchio and Crawford 2015). However, there is evidence of links between rock climbing and disturbance to cliff-dwelling wildlife (Knight and Cole 1995) 

## Outreach & Communications

There is evidence of links between rock climbing and disturbance to cliff-dwelling wildlife (Knight and Cole 1995; pp 56-57). Strategy to address this problem should include outreach via tools such as signage and outreach to local organizations to get the word out about cliff habitat where sensitive species are known to occur.

## Colonial Bird Breeding Sites

Colonial breeding sites for waterbirds in Washington are found across a diverse range of habitats on isolated coastal and inland landscapes. These include offshore rocks, sea stacks, offshore rocky and vegetated islands, sandy spits, natural and artificially created islands in large bodies of water such as rivers, lakes, and coastal bays, steep coastal cliffs, and even human-made structures like rooftops, docks, or pilings. Structural features vary depending on species: cliff-nesting seabirds require vertical relief and ledges, while ground-nesting species prefer open or gently sloped areas with sparse vegetation or suitable burrowing substrates (WDFW 2015). Tree-nesting colonial species prefer particular species of trees and percentage of overall vegetative cover (WDFW 2015). Vegetation composition reflects habitat type and typically includes native coastal grasses, forbs, and low shrubs. Freshwater sites typically include emergent vegetation such as cattails, bulrushes, sedges, and willow shrubs, while saltwater marshes commonly support salt-tolerant plants (WDFW 2015). Some colonial breeding bird species nest on exposed gravel or shell substrates with little to no vegetation. Invasive species such as European and American beachgrass (Ammophila sp) and Phragmites australis have increasingly altered habitat structure in some areas, impacting habitat suitability (WDFW 2015). Critical to these sites are their isolation, which contributes to protection from predators and disturbance, correct nesting substrates, and the right balance of vegetation cover or openness tailored to each species' nesting requirements.
The primary ecological processes that maintain these habitats include minimal disturbance from predators and humans, intact natural hydrologic and geomorphic dynamics, and access to adjacent marine and freshwater prey resources. Natural hydrologic regimes-seasonal flooding, tidal inundation, and water level fluctuations-help maintain habitat structure by limiting excessive plant encroachment, cycling nutrients, and supporting dynamic vegetation mosaics (Sheldon et al. 2005, WDFW 2015). Physical forces such as wind, wave action, erosion, natural and artificial substrate deposition, and sediment transport continually shape nesting substrates and preserve the openness or structural complexity required by different species (Sheldon et al. 2005, WDFW 2015). Vegetation dynamics are influenced by salt spray, seasonal disturbances, herbivory, and nesting activities, including soil turnover by burrow-nesting birds. Predation pressure is minimized through site isolation-often on islands or other inaccessible locations-and seasonal vegetation growth that provides cover during nesting. The productivity of surrounding marine and freshwater ecosystems is critical for reproductive success, as it sustains forage fish and invertebrate prey populations. Where these natural processes have been altered or degraded, human interventions-such as invasive species removal, nest substrate restoration, and seasonal access restrictions-are often necessary to maintain or restore the ecological functions essential for successful breeding.
Colonial breeding sites are broadly distributed across Washington's marine and freshwater environments. Saltwater colonies occur along the outer Pacific Coast, including the Columbia River estuary, Puget Sound, the Strait of Juan de Fuca, and numerous coastal bays and islands (Duff et al. 2014). Notable marine colony sites include Protection Island, Smith and Minor Islands, Destruction Island, Tatoosh Island, and various rocky islets and sandy spits within the San Juan Islands and Willapa Bay (Hansen et al. 2019). Freshwater colonies are found in trees adjacent to water bodies of all sizes, on islands in large lakes and reservoirs, river systems including the Columbia, Spokane, and Skagit Rivers, and occasionally inland marsh complexes (WDFW 2015). Other important freshwater and estuarine sites include the barrier islands of the Columbia River estuary, Nisqually Delta, and Skagit Bay islands (Roby et al. 2021). Many of these sites are within state or federal protected areas, although the degree of active management and monitoring varies.
Colonial breeding sites in Washington hold exceptional conservation value due to their ecological specificity and the disproportionate number of priority species that depend on them. This HGCN type is broadly associated with wetlands, marine waters, shorelines, floodplains, sand bars, spits, and riparian systems. These habitats support some of the largest and most ecologically significant aggregations of diverse waterbird species, many of which rely on secure, undisturbed locations for successful reproduction. Vulnerable concentrations that depend on these habitats include breeding concentrations of cormorants, storm-petrels, terns, and alcids in western Washington, and grebes, cormorants, terns, as well as semicolonial stilts, phalaropes, and avocets in eastern Washington (WDFW 2008). By concentrating nesting efforts in colonies, these sites support critical social behaviors that enhance reproductive success and species survival. They also serve as key indicators of the health and integrity of Washington's aquatic ecosystems, contributing to biodiversity, nutrient cycling between land and sea, and the resilience of coastal food webs. However, the overall extent and functional quality of these sites have declined significantly from historic conditions. Several species have experienced colony abandonment or contraction due to cumulative stressors. While targeted conservation actions have improved conditions at some priority sites, most colonies remain vulnerable to ongoing and emerging threats. Given their limited distribution and high sensitivity, sustained protection, proactive management, and habitat restoration are essential to maintaining Washington's colonial bird breeding sites and achieving long-term conservation goals. Implementing best waste disposal practices and policies reduces human-sourced food subsidies that elevate predator populations near breeding sites. 9.6 Light, Noise, Thermal Pollution

## Outreach & Communications

Artificial lighting (from vessels, marinas, development) disrupts behavior, especially for burrow-nesting species and nocturnal return flights (e.g., auklets, petrels). Noise increases stress and may deter colony use. Raising awareness among local residents, businesses, and visitors encourages voluntary reduction of lighting and noise near colonies. 9.6 Light, Noise, Thermal Pollution

## Conservation Planning

Planning incorporates pollution control measures into habitat protection to minimize sensory disturbance and behavioral disruption to colonial nesting birds. 9.6 Light, Noise, Thermal Pollution

## Policies & Guidelines

Seasonal buffers for loud activities (e.g., blasting, loud construction, air craft engine noise). Establishing policies and guidelines provides a structured approach to minimizing these disturbances by setting limits on lighting, regulating noise levels during sensitive breeding seasons, and preventing thermal discharges into adjacent habitats. These guidelines help agencies, landowners, and developers implement best practices to reduce sensory stressors and maintain the conditions necessary for successful breeding at colony sites. ## Migratory Bird Stopovers

This feature habitat is directly connected to and described in the Statewide/Transboundary Conservation Need: Migratory Bird Conservation. This important feature is associated with many, many habitats on the ground; the location, accessibility, patch size (depending on the species needing to stopover), forage availability, and limited disturbance during use are key factors in determining whether a feature is useable during migration. Urban forest patches, mountain ridges, wetlands of all types, riparian corridors, old growth and mature forest, mudflats and intertidal areas along shorelines, and marine waters can all contain key migratory bird stopover features. These features are often significant for breeding or wintering birds as well, though some locations are especially critical during the migratory season for individual species. Migratory Bird Stopovers support hundreds of species of migratory birds, as diverse as the habitats on which they depend, including most of the avian SGCN species identified in this plan. Vulnerable concentrations identified in this plan are usually associated with migratory bird stopovers.
Because migratory bird stopovers have seasonal use, threats can affect migratory birds differently depending on when they occur. For instance, seabird bycatch or oil spills may have greater or lesser impacts depending on whether they coincide with migration periods.
Migratory bird conservation is discussed in the Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs, and migratory bird stopovers are a Strategic Habitat of Greatest Conservation Need in this plan. Migratory bird distribution is vast and variable, depending on the season. For this plan, the best source of vetted distribution information would be individual SGCN species' HUC10 distribution from this plan, paired with WDFW surveys and other occurrence documentation and information. BirdCast (screenshot Figure 
11
) can provide additional information about seasonal peaks and live seasonal tracking to inform conservation project timing at the county level. 

## Old Growth and Mature Forest

Old-growth and mature forests are designated priority habitat types in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species program. They play a crucial role in biodiversity, supporting many specialized plants and wildlife (e.g., rare and imperiled species like the northern spotted owl and marbled murrelet) that rely on these closed-canopy complex forest habitats for survival. Old-growth and mature forest conditions vary in detail among essentially all forest types in terms of their exact physical and biological attributes, which is why type-specific definitions are necessary to accurately characterize old-growth and mature forest for each type of forest in Washington. Old-growth and mature forest conditions also differ profoundly between moist westside forests, which are characterized by highly infrequent, stand replacement events; mature and old stands of Oregon white oak; and dry eastside forests, which historically were characterized by frequent low-severity fire events. Tree size (i.e., larger diameters at breast height) is not always a good indicator of tree age or maturity in drier, less productive soil conditions like those in eastern Washington. These forests are no less important as old growth or mature forest than westside giants. 
212
 Old-growth forests are dynamic systems distinguished by old trees and related structural attributes. Oldgrowth encompasses the later stages of stand development that typically differ from earlier stages in a variety of characteristics, which include accumulations of large dead woody material, number of canopy layers, species composition, and ecosystem function. In addition to their ecological attributes, oldgrowth forests are distinguished by their ecosystem services and social, cultural, and economic values. 
213
 Mature forests are delineated ecologically as the stage of forest development immediately before old growth. Mature forests exhibit structural characteristics that are lacking in earlier stages of forest development and may contain some but not all the structural attributes in old-growth forests. The mature stage of stand development generally begins when a forest stand moves beyond self-thinning, starts to diversify in height and structure, and/or the understory begins to reinitiate. Structural characteristics that mark the transition from an immature to mature forest are unique to each forest type; they may include but are not limited to abundance of large trees, large tree stem diameter, stem diameter diversity, horizontal canopy openings or patchiness, aboveground biomass accumulation, stand height, presence of standing and/or downed boles, vertical canopy layers, or a combination of these attributes. 
214
 Since the time of non-indigenous settlement, approximately 72% of the original old-growth forest has been lost in the Pacific Northwest, largely through logging and other developments. 
215
 The greatest present-day threats to the existence and ecological function of old-growth and mature forest come from 
212 WDFW 2008
, Franklin et al. 2005
 213 USDA Forest Service 1989, Barndt et al. 2023 214 Barndt et al. 2023 fire and fire suppression as well as from climate change and disease. There is an increasing body of research documenting the effects of prolonged fire exclusion in forest types that are characteristically maintained by frequent disturbance regimes. 
216
 Fire suppression and exclusion alters disturbance processes that eventually result in adverse outcomes, such as uncharacteristically severe fires and reduced resistance to insect pests, drought, and other environmental stressors. Climate change is a complex stressor, that can also interact with and exacerbate other stressors or threats (e.g., fires, insects). 
217
 New guidelines for identifying mature and old forests in western and eastern Washington state were recently released by WA DNR in 2025. 
218
 Recently, The Nature Conservancy published results indicating that restoration treatments are positively correlated with the development of key (westside coniferdominated) old-growth forest indicators-larger trees, more complex forest structures, reduced tree density, and increased understory plant richness. 
219
 Other findings of that study speak to the importance of a long-term and ongoing restoration approach, monitoring, and adaptive management.
Those practices may look very different for mature and old growth Oregon white oak forest and eastside conifer forest conservation. Protection may be more important for carbon storage. 
220
 And, a balance of restoration, protection, and management at very fine scales is needed to ensure restoration contributes to, not hinders, soil structure and development, moisture retention and gradients, and the diversity of habitats within stands (e.g., fallen logs, snags, cavities, underground mycelium networks).
Large structurally unique trees and the stands that support mature and old growth forest ecological functions (westside, east side, and Oregon white oak ecosystems) are essential for SGCN that rely on this habitat such as marbled murrelet, northern spotted owl, fisher, western gray squirrel, white-headed and Lewis's woodpeckers, flammulated owl, slender-billed white-breasted pygmy nuthatch, and meadow fritillary.

## Snags and Logs

Dead wood in the form of snags and downed logs contributes to biological richness as substrate, cavity and foraging sites, and shelter or cover. 
221
 These features are also significant habitat inputs for streams and rivers when they create structure and cover for fish and instream invertebrates, decay and provide food for instream invertebrates and add nutrients to aquatic systems. Tree species, the stage of decay, the distribution of other snags in the area (clumped or scattered), and the successional stage of the 
216 Hanberry et al. 2020
, Reilly et al. 2021
, Woodbridge et al. 2022
, Brodie et al. 2023
 217 USFS 2023
218 WDNR 2025 
219
 Case, Ettinger, and Pradhan 2023 
220 Birdsey et al. 2025
 plant community where the snag occurs are important factors in providing habitat function to wildlife. 
222
 The height and diameter of a snag or log are arguably the most important factors to their function as wildlife habitat. In general, the bigger the snags and logs the more function they will provide. 
223
 Snags and logs are a designated priority habitat type in WDFW's PHS program. In PHS, priority snags have a minimum diameter at breast height of 20 inches in western Washington and 12 inches in eastern Washington and are at least 6.5 feet in height. Priority logs are at least 12 inches in diameter at the largest end, and no less than 20 feet long. Priority snag and log habitat in PHS includes individual snags and/or logs, or groups of snags and/or logs of exceptional value to wildlife due to their scarcity or location in a particular landscape. Areas with abundant, well-distributed snags and logs are also considered priority snag and log habitat in PHS. 
224
 Factors that may contribute to the decline of important snags and logs include intensive forest management, fire suppression, and other factors that influence forest sustainability and age-class diversity. Intensive forest management has been shown to simplify stand and landscape structure. Only two rotations of managed Douglas-fir have been estimated to reduce the abundance of dead wood by 90%, compared to levels in natural old-growth systems. Fire suppression has also been demonstrated to increase the density of smaller trees, which in turn decreases abundance of larger diameter trees. This results in lower recruitment of large snags and downed wood. Complex linkages between temperature, carbon dioxide, plant growth, decomposition, and nutrient cycling influenced by climate change could also affect large wood recruitment by altering the size and relative distributions of carbon and nutrient pools in forest soils and vegetation. 
225
 More than 100 species of birds, mammals, reptiles, and amphibians are known to require snags and/or logs for nesting, roosting, shelter, denning, and feeding. 
226
 Examples of SGCN that rely on this habitat include silver-haired bats, long-legged myotis, fringed myotis, purple martin, flammulated owl, northern spotted owl, Lewis's woodpecker, white-headed woodpecker, Western bluebird, western screech owl, and Pacific marten. (Rose CL, Marcot BG, Mellen TK et al. 2001; pp 594). This results in lower recruitment of large snags and downed wood (Rose CL, Marcot BG, Mellen TK et al. 2001; pp 594). Forest health treatments, changes in forest harvest rotations, and leaving snags and logs as well as larger trees for recruitment as snags and logs should be implemented on managed forestlands.

## Talus

Talus in Washington is represented by homogenous areas of rock rubble ranging in average size 0.15 -2.0 m (0.5 -6.5 ft), composed of basalt, andesite, and/or sedimentary rock, including riprap slides used by marine SGCN and mine tailings used by bats. Several SGCN bats depend on talus, including Canyon bats, little brown bats, and pallid bats. The American pika uses talus slopes for shelter from extreme temperatures and to store food for winter. The Larch Mountain salamander and several terrestrial snails rely on talus for cool, moist microhabitats essential for respiration and shelter, as well as protection from predators. Terrestrial snail SGCN that rely on talus habitats include rare and specialized species like the Dalles sideband (Monadenia fidelis minor) and Columbia Oregonian (Cryptomastix hendersoni). These areas may also be associated with cliffs.
Talus slopes are a landform comprised of talus (or "scree") which accumulates over extended periods below rockwalls which were formed by glaciation or erosion (e.g., from the Missoula Floods). A talus slope's size and shape reflect the shape, size, and composition of the source material and stage of development. Commonly, the size of debris increases further downslope due to fall-sorting, which occurs when smaller debris is trapped between larger particles and coarser debris -with its greater momentum --travels further downslope. Further sorting can occur over time due to surface flow of the talus deposits (Curry 2025). Talus slopes can significantly affect groundwater flow and storage. In one small (0.5 ac) alpine meadow in the Canadian Rockies, a talus slope was the primary groundwater reservoir, suppling 75% of streamflow during storms and the winter base-flow period (McClymont et al., 2010).
Plant colonization or recolonization of talus slopes is highly dependent on temperature and moisture regimes. Despite differences in plant succession based on aspect (Daubenmire and Slipp 1943), succession typically begins with moss (especially in wetter, north faces) and lichens, pioneering herbaceous species common to the ecoregion (e.g., kinnikinnic, phlox, lupine), shrubs (e.g., thimbleberry, Oregon grape, Nootka rose), and trees (e.g., vine maple, red alder, Douglas fir, and western redcedar). Vegetation stabilizes talus slopes and provides habitat for various wildlife species.
The primary deposition mechanism of talus is rockfall, caused by freeze-thaw cycles, erosion (water and wind), biogeochemical weathering, and thermal expansion-contraction. Some talus slopes receive significant debris from avalanches and debris flows which can be triggered by earthquakes typically larger than magnitude 4.0 (Curry 2025). Secondary rockfalls can be triggered by human or animal activity. Rates of rockwall retreat, while highly variable, are highest immediately post-glaciation and diminish with time (Ballantyne, 2002). Talus deposits are often long-lasting landscape features with older features having negligible rates of current accumulation. Talus slopes are commonly preserved as inactive formations under established vegetation. Because of the dynamic nature of many talus slopes, vegetative communities are subject to disturbances (e.g., debris flows) which can locally reset plant colonization.
Talus slopes are a common landform found in every county in Washington however it is a specialized habitat, and not all talus is the same. Wherever cliffs or rockwalls exist, talus slopes are likely to exist as well. High-elevation talus slopes in the Cascades and Olympics are associated with glaciers and permafrost; such talus slopes are more susceptible to increased slope instability from climate change (Curry 2025). Given talus slopes' inherent instability and hazards, direct anthropogenic changes to talus slopes are not widespread. It is likely that the current extent and quality of talus slopes is similar to historic conditions. At a site-scale, where talus slopes have experienced degradation, it is typically due to mining or human-induced landslides associated with road construction, utility construction, or logging. In some locations talus slopes have been degraded by recreational impacts (e.g., trampled plants, increased erosion). Unseasonably warm and extreme warm temperatures will likely affect talus slopes by increasing rates of rockfall due to changes in freeze-thaw cycles, more intense rainfall events, and increased thermal expansion, and may affect the relationship talus has to groundwater resources.
Changing fire regimes may also lead to increased landslide frequency in steeper terrain.

## Future: Adding HGCN

To better understand which NVC Groups or GET Functional Groups were not included as a Strategic HGCN (PHS Priority Habitat or WNHP state-listed) and whether any of those might be nominees for future additions as HGCN, a technical team reviewed all of the Washington state Groups and Functional Groups that are not cultural types (human-created or ruderal) against the list of Strategic HGCN, and looked at ecosystems with two or more closely associated animal or plant 
SGCN. 227
 Twenty-one additional NVC Groups and GET Functional Groups that are not currently HGCN support more than one SGCN (Table 
30
), several montane and a few ocean ecosystems. 228 Ocean ecosystems are experiencing significant threats due to changing temperatures, ocean acidification, and harmful algal blooms. 
229
 The ocean is a vast, interconnected environment. The GET systems in Table 
30
 are not distinct waters or zones from the shoreline; these are pelagic layers from the surface where light penetrates the water (epipelagic) to pitch-black and freezing depths. 
230
 These are not systems that fit either PHS or WNHP criteria or processes. Ocean waters are influenced by conservation strategies for the Pacific Ocean and Salish Sea and contributing watersheds, and those strategies often must consider national, multi-national, and international scales to be effective. For these reasons, we did not add the ocean GET types to the HGCN list but acknowledge the conservation need in the Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs: Marine Conservation section and acknowledge we need more work in the SWAP to include marine conservation. In addition to considering marine ecosystems as HGCN, external engagement comments suggested that the SWAP include marine specialized feature habitats such as intramarine rocky reefs, given their importance for species such as the state-listed pinto abalone and multiple demersal rockfish species.
Montane ecosystems are unique environments with varied temperature and moisture gradients across elevations from foothills to peaks, and often have specialized habitats, high biodiversity, and endemic species. Recent and anticipated changes such as increased temperatures, shifting precipitation patterns that influence snowpack development and persistence, soil moisture and retention, and forest health and wildfire vulnerability are posing severe threats to montane biodiversity at the system and finer habitat scales. Local temperature differences can abruptly shift montane biodiversity and ecosystem services along elevation gradients. 
231
 Montane ecosystem changes need urgent and comprehensive research that can be applied to influence management and conservation strategies in the face of these rapid changes. 
232
 A recent modeled evaluation 233 found that many of the montane systems in Washington state at a coarse scale are somewhat protected in areas defined by IUCN I-VI land protection status categories. 
234
 That work also indicated the US NVC expert collaboration could be helpful to advance a finer scale and current condition evaluation to inform conservation strategies within and adjacent to these protected areas, so these systems are not added at this time, but are flagged for review. More information from Tribes specifically about these food -system relationships would be helpful in future HGCN considerations.

## Several systems in

Rather than add HGCN solely based on number of SGCN (in SWAP 2015, it was 6 SGCN) or create a complex third set of criteria outside of the PHS and WNHP vetted processes, and to promote the value of system conservation where many SGCN may benefit, SWAP 2025 recommends the following for the NVC systems in Table 
30
:
• find common ground in overlapping SGCN and their conservation actions, consider First Foods values (e.g., huckleberry), and include these systems in conservation planning efforts especially in identifying biodiversity areas, protected areas, and any matrix lands that provide connectivity between/among those areas and cores; and • use the PHS Priority Habitats and WNHP conservation ranking processes in the next five years to assess these habitats, document the findings in both WDFW and WA DNR databases, and include those findings in the next SWAP update to shape the future HGCN list. 
235
 This recommendation is also captured in SWAP Planning Conservation Recommendations section.

## Conservation Issues and Actions

This section describes the resources and process we used to identify threats and actions for SGCN and most HGCN in the SWAP, meeting
• Required Element 3 -Descriptions of problems which may adversely affect species identified in Element 1 or their habitats, and priority research and survey efforts needed to identify factors which may assist in restoration and improved conservation of these species and habitats, and • Required Element 4 -Descriptions of conservation actions proposed to conserve the identified species and habitats and priorities for implementing such actions.
Conservation actions are the most important part of the SWAP and address the issues that currently affect SGCN and HGCN persistence and sustainability.
Conservation actions are most effective when they include monitoring, document progress, and consider if or how to adapt to emerging information. What we understand about species and their needs, habitats, systems, and impacts to sustainability is constantly evolving. And, because assessing status without acting on the information we have puts us behind in the race to conserve biodiversity, data collection must go hand-in-hand with action. The conservation actions in the SWAP are at multiple scales based on decades of research and expertise and are not static. Recovery and restoration take time, sometimes generations of species, to see positive effects, so in-project monitoring and applying that information is key to effectiveness.
The SWAP is not a catalogue of all issues that have adversely impacted habitats and species from past to present. The SWAP identifies current and emerging issues that can be acted on now and in the foreseeable future, based on what we know now and can reasonably and confidently model. The SWAP makes conservation recommendations to support efforts that have positive momentum, wellstructured decision-making, and/or urgency to prevent the need to list species and to recover those already in the conservation "emergency room." Issues are identified in a way that helps conservation practitioners understand what we know, and conservation actions that directly benefit SWAP systems, habitats and species are identified to address those issues to get started on some or all of these questions: what needs to be done (first and ongoing), who can help, when is it needed (how urgent), and where (ecoregions, watersheds).
Example. "Residential and Commercial Development" is an issue often stated as a contributor to loss and degradation of species and habitats. Conservation investment of expertise, time, and money needs to be spent where opportunities exist to support ecosystem function and population sustainability.
Where existing long-standing development has removed or significantly degraded the ecosystem, it may be ineffective to try to return those areas to natural conditions or functions; in those situations, that would not be the most important threat to identify for a species or habitat because the action options are so very limited or unlikely to be successful. However, SGCN can occur in urban areas and opportunities exist to guide development away from sensitive areas or minimize impacts that may occur from new development. Where development can be specifically attributed as a direct impact to the species or its habitat and actions can be taken to improve the species, habitat, and/or ecosystem status and function, then identifying this issue is appropriate. A few development-related actions might be:
• Use urban land use planning and restoration, public lands stewardship, and effective voluntary conservation to connect targeted riparian corridors for high priority fish passage and habitat improvements, floodplain conservation, and/or migratory bird stopovers; • Provide technical guidance to land use planners to integrate PHS shrubsteppe management recommendations and WSRRI strategies into local land use decision making processes for system-level benefits; • Reduce pollutant inputs to HGCN to benefit salmon, Oregon spotted frog, freshwater mussels, or other specific aquatic species in a particular watershed or ecoregion; • Create regionally specific seed mixes for remediation, restoration, and education about westside prairies and pollinators with targeted outreach for wildland-adjacent suburban and exurban development (e.g., homeowners, landowners, infrastructure planners and construction companies) to reduce non-native species invasion into wildlands from these areas; • Support the DNR Forest Action Plan and work with landowners and utility companies in strategic wildland-urban interfaces to reduce wildfire risk to infrastructure and commercial forests and retain ecosystem benefits for targeted HGCN and SGCN.

## Issue and Action Vocabulary

Direct issues and actions for animal Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) are identified using a consistent framework 
236
 , with a few amendments for Washington state (these large tables are externally linked):
• Table 
I
 Many SWAPs in the national network use the Conservation Standards issues and actions to describe the species and habitat conservation needs in their state. Using the same framework helps us contribute to understanding national patterns of biodiversity impact and conservation actions that work. This standard vocabulary and set of definitions help conservation practitioners speak the same language to communicate about the problems they are facing and solutions they could use. The Conservation Standards threats/issues vocabulary was updated to version 4.0 in 2024-2025. 
238
 The SGCN and HGCN process was fully underway when Threats 4.0 was released, and the process could not pivot to use the new, revised information (SWAP Planning Recommendation to consider the new version between SWAP revisions). 

## Considerations

In addition to the vocabulary, the SWAP puts some sideboards on which issues and actions need to be in this plan. Some of these considerations support prioritization -actions that can do the most good with available resources, capacity, and information, in places where those actions will be durable to address the greatest risks to SGCN and HGCN sustainability. Contributors considered realistic and foreseeable direct threats that can be acted on based on what we know now, those that currently adversely affect or degrade the habitat, and are in most need of conservation attention. Conservation actions directly relate to the threat to make progress toward resolving the issue and could include priority research or questions that need to be answered
foot_34
 , partnerships, structured decision-making, monitoring to inform effectiveness, and planning. The plan does not include historic threats that are no longer actionable. The issues-to-actions relationship could be one-to-one or one-to-many. There may be more than one action per threat.
"Issues" in the vocabulary are not inherently "bad." These activities become issues when they adversely affect the status or sustainability of a species or habitat, and people need to resolve that issue through conservation action. For example, fire can be applied as a tool in restoration whereas some wildland fire can be catastrophic to ecosystems; or recreation can create engagement, volunteer stewardship opportunities, and connections that inspire beneficial behaviors whereas some recreation like creating "social trails" or driving in streambeds can be adversely impactful.
The SWAP identifies direct issues that limit population, habitat, or system sustainability, resiliency, redundancy, and/or representation. 
241
 In the explanation of the threat for SGCN or HGCN, indirect issues and stressors may be included in the explanation. 
242
 The "Three R's" can also be applied to habitats and systems: can the habitat or system adapt to changing conditions, can they rebound from stochastic disturbance without significant intervention, are there other places on the landscape a system occurs that could support habitats and species following a catastrophic event?
242 Conservation Measures Partnership 2025. 1.C. Identify Critical Threats, 1.D. Assess the Conservation Situation Direct issues. Direct issues may be considered sources of stress: human actions (e.g., large industrial solar development, pollution release, road construction, utilities operations/maintenance) that immediately affect/degrade a conservation target; natural phenomena altered by human activities (e.g., increase in water temperature caused by increasingly warming environment) or natural phenomena impact that is increased by human activities (e.g., natural landslide due to human-caused land-clearing activities that threatens the only known location of a springs salamander).
Indirect issues. A driver of or influence on direct threats that may help us understand the direct threat and where we can apply solutions. Sometimes called a root cause or underlying cause.
Example. Clear-cutting or unsustainable logging may be the direct threat to a forest habitat or forest-dependent species, whereas logging policies or rules may be the indirect threat (a different layer or further 'upstream' of the direct impact where policy solutions can be applied).
Stress. The impact of the direct issue on the conservation target; attributes of a conservation target's ecology that are impaired directly or indirectly.
Examples. Pygmy Rabbit (SGCN) -if direct threat is increased frequency, intensity, and extent of wildfire in shrubsteppe systems, then stresses to the rabbits may be significantly reduced forage and poor body condition leading to lower reproductive potential, isolated populations, reduced/limited population size, and increased predation from opportunistic native predators that take advantage of rabbit exposure.
Shrubsteppe (HGCN) -if direct threat is increased frequency, intensity, and extent of wildfire in shrubsteppe systems, then stresses to the habitat may be significantly reduced seed bank and soil moisture for natural recovery, reduced natural soil crust, and bare-soil exposure benefitting non-native cheatgrass invasion.
No issues are identified for Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN) because there is not enough information or confidence to understand the species' context which would guide conservation actions. So, only one action is available to choose for SGIN: Action 8.1 "Research and Monitoring: Basic Research and Status Monitoring" from Table 
J
.
Conservation actions are how practitioners guide intervention to support a specific desired future condition or positive-progress strategy to address an issue. Conservation actions are objectives that would be useful to guide and develop project plans, identify dependencies and relationships, pursue funding, and craft new or engage existing partnerships in the SWAP implementation.
Issues in the SWAP should be reasonably actionable in the foreseeable future, not a long 'laundry list.' Some impacts have happened long ago and the condition of the species or habitat is reflecting that in a permanent way. Conservation actions need to be forward looking -what can we work with now to improve the future for those systems, habitats, and species?
Issues and actions are supported by good information and references. Issues identified for HGCN and SGCN are supported in tables and other data products by citable information (e.g., recovery outlines, Periodic Status Reviews, state or federal listing documents, NatureServe threat analysis, working group documentation, models, synthesis). Actions have a direct connection to the issue and the species/habitats affected by that issue. Where a conservation action is clearly stated or described in another product, that product is referenced, but all of the content is not re-stated in the SWAP. In this way, the SWAP remains a nimble document that can point to evolving and relevant information in other plans that have active working groups or stakeholders or more frequent updates.
Actions are "prescriptive enough." Actions provide a clear framework to address an issue AND leave room for new information, methods, and partnerships in implementation. A good example is a conservation action that recommends species reintroduction. There is a LOT of partnership, policy-work, baseline monitoring, and effectiveness planning that is part of that action, dependent on current and desired habitat conditions, capacity and resources, and -importantly -the state of the practice at the time. Actions in the SWAP must be "prescriptive enough" to say the reintroduction is warranted, but not so prescriptive as to the specific steps to take in implementation. SWAP actions can help identify related issues, find partnerships, pursue funding, and inspire creative solutions. The SWAP does not provide project-or activity-level detail.
Existing expertise and complex problems. Conservation for many SGCN and HGCN already have communities of practice, expert networks to help shape effective strategies, and pathways to good partnerships. And, in some cases, those communities of practice encounter complex problems or issues where the action or approach is less clear right now, issues that need innovation or a broader human understanding or community. These days, certain threats and the pace/scale of change (e.g., ecosystem composition shifts, extensive biodiversity loss
foot_36
 , invasive species and/or disease spread) are creating problems that our past best practices and existing partnerships may not be sufficient to address. In novel situations, we need novel approaches; a willingness by practitioners, managers, leaders, and stakeholders to try new things and accept the possibility of failure, well-vetted and transparent decisionmaking and action without admiring the problem too long; and a commitment to monitoring and adaptation built into our actions. Structured decision-making, working group or partnership development or enhancement, effectiveness monitoring, and adaptive management are conservation actions, essential before and after work on the ground.
Comments for threats and actions in the SWAP are supported by good information and references (e.g., recovery outlines, Periodic Status Reviews, state or federal listing documents, NatureServe threat analysis, working group documentation, models, synthesis). References are most important in threats and actions if they provide different information from the status reference(s). If the most important conservation action is clearly stated in another product, we asked contributors to reference the product but not 'regurgitate' all of the pertinent information in the SWAP. In this way, the SWAP remains a nimble document that can point to evolving and relevant information in other plans that have active working groups or stakeholders or more frequent updates, and this is a good adaptive management strategy.

## SGCN Conservation Issues and Actions Results

The results of the Conservation Issues and Actions assessments are in externally linked tables for animal SGCN, the Washington Natural Heritage Plan for SGCN Plants, and HGCN and Statewide and Transboundary Need sections. Plant conservation issues and needs are defined in the conservation status rank process, in data curated by WNHP in the Biotics system (the most up-to-date resource) and the online Field Guide to Rare Plants of Washington (2011). The 2025 Washington Natural Heritage Plan outlines rare plant and ecosystem conservation priorities as biodiversity representation needs in Washington's Natural Areas, sorted by ecoregions (Table 
9
 of that document). The Heritage Plan outlines there is much conservation work to be done for rare plants, and for the ecosystems that are Strategic and Feature HGCN.
HGCN conservation issues and actions that use the same vocabulary are in the HGCN sections of this document. Other HGCN conservation actions are described in the Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs section, and related to the Washington Natural Heritage Plan ecosystem representation needs.
Monitoring (Required Element 5) is a common conservation action in Table L and in the HGCN sections. See also SWAP Planning Recommendations section.

## Conservation by Geography

Conservation targets, issues, and actions are also discussed and presented in ten geographic sections: statewide and transboundary, and nine ecoregions. 
244
 Conservation actions are at many scales to benefit conservation targets -HGCN, vulnerable concentrations, and SGCN -and the systems on which they depend.
The Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs section highlights key issues and efforts that are working or needed at that larger scale, across the state and with other governments (e.g., Tribes, Canada, adjacent states, western region, internationally).
Ecoregions section is divided into the nine ecoregions of Washington state and includes a graphic of the ecoregion and summary tables of SGCN to be used with the HGCN conservation recommendations, statewide and transboundary conservation recommendations if relevant, the animal SGCN Actions 

## Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs

The most important part of the SWAP are the actions that we recommend at many scales to benefit the conservation targets: HGCN, vulnerable concentrations, SGCN and the ecosystems on which they depend. Actions are proposed to address threats or issues that affect the sustainability of the conservation targets. This section identifies statewide and transboundary (i.e., across many ecoregions, statewide, many states, or international) actions to support the durability and effectiveness of the actions taken at finer scales. An example would be development of a riparian conservation policy for the state, which would increase riparian protection and restoration and benefit multiple riparian-dependent SGCN in the ecoregions or watersheds where they occur. The priority statewide and transboundary actions presented here may also be considerations in more localized SGCN and HGCN conservation implementation to improve the effectiveness and durability of site-and habitat-level actions.
The topics in this section:
• require statewide or transboundary policy, legislative action, budget commitment, legal framework, or formal guidelines to effectively address a high priority issue; and/or
• High priority conservation actions must be coordinated and sequenced across multiple regions to be effective (i.e., without that broader collaboration, significant gaps in or poor sequencing of the work reduces the effectiveness overall).
There is often overlap in and relationship among the issues in this section. Progress made in one arena often has positive benefits to another. For instance, conservation progress in land use planning and riparian protection and management could benefit connectivity, water quality and quantity for fish and wildlife, salmon recovery, forest health, and First Foods sustainability.
Most of these transboundary conservation needs are essential to support ecosystem function and species conservation, and the success of many of the recommendations in this section address habitat protection, restoration, and management. The effectiveness of the recommendations in this section are the direct or indirect result of human influence -where and how we choose to live, work, and play; the choices and tradeoffs we make, or can make, at various scales in natural resource economies (forestry, fishing, agriculture); how and when we use and manage natural resources; and how we respond to large environmental change like amplified risks of wildfire, drought, flooding, diseases, and invasive species.
This section provides descriptions, issues, and conservation recommendations for statewide and transboundary topics that fit the two criteria and description above. Similar, scaled-down actions may also be in relevant ecoregion sections. For instance, the statewide habitat connectivity issue and actions relate to the regional recommendations in the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan; in the Columbia Plateau ecoregion, the focus will be on the individual corridors (e.g., Lower Crab Creek), and SGCN and HGCN benefitting from these connectivity actions.

## Habitat Connectivity

Habitat connectivity is a major conservation issue at all scales and this is one of the most important conservation issues we could address. 
245
 Habitat fragmentation has long been identified as a significant threat to biodiversity, species persistence, and habitat resilience. 
246
 Habitat connectivity is the degree to which wildlife can move across the landscape as needed to find food and shelter, migrate seasonally, establish new territories, maintain healthy populations through genetic exchange, and adapt to changing climatic conditions. Connectivity also supports broader ecological functions such as seed dispersal and pollination and sustains species important to cultural traditions like hunting and gathering. As such, maintaining and enhancing habitat connectivity is a critical habitat feature essential to terrestrial, aquatic, and marine habitats.
All statewide to species-level conservation issues need connectivity considerations, and three themes emerge: terrestrial, aquatic and riparian, and migratory bird stopovers and overwintering habitats.
There is overlap in this plan among these, and across other transboundary conservation needs and recommendations (e.g., riparian and floodplain, salmon recovery, land use planning). This section calls attention to connectivity, regardless of habitat type. We are quickly losing the opportunity to keep and make connections among biodiversity areas and cores, subpopulations throughout a population, and the transitions across elevations (from sea to mountains).
Statewide, habitat connectivity is a theme at many scales. Local jurisdictions are including it in planning guidance, Comprehensive Plans, and open space grants (e.g., Pierce County Conservation Futures 247 ). Washington Department of Transportation is addressing it with wildlife crossings for terrestrial wildlife for human and wildlife safety and in culvert replacement to improve fish passage, an essential connectivity issue for salmon recovery (and also benefits terrestrial wildlife). Dam removal projects across the state are improving aquatic connectivity, not just for fish passage, but for aquatic system function, riparian restoration, and marine health (e.g., Elwha River and system restoration 248 and Glines Dam removal 249 ).
Vulnerable ungulate concentrations (elk, mule deer) rely on connectivity to move between summer and winter range in regular migratory patterns. The movement of American pika across fragmented talus patches represents a smaller scale connectivity need, where microhabitat linkages are essential for survival, dispersal, and climate resilience. Even among butterflies, connectivity is important at speciesspecific scales. For example, mardon skippers are limited by distance and barriers between habitat patches while Oregon silverspots may disperse several kilometers across fragmented landscapes. 
250
 Roadways can be connectivity barriers for many fish and wildlife, so culvert design and other roadway wildlife crossings large and small can be helpful for elk to fishers to snakes (snakes are at high risk of being killed on roads as they and their prey can be attracted at night to these heat sources 251 ).

## Terrestrial Connectivity

In 2025, WDFW and WSDOT co-led the development of the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan (WAHCAP) as a statewide terrestrial habitat connectivity prioritization framework for on-the-ground projects aiming to protect and reconnect Washington's landscapes for wildlife (Figure 
10
, Habitat Support HGCN section). The report and spatial data released in summer 2025 provide the best platform to view, understand, and act on habitat connectivity needs at this scale. Connectivity conservation actions in the plan are geared towards land use planning, voluntary conservation incentives, transportation projects, public lands, and indigenous-led stewardship. Both the report and the spatial data can be accessed here wdfw.wa.gov/species-habitats/habitat-recovery/connectivity/action-plan.
The WAHCAP data and analyses target the statewide and regional scales. Although terrestrial connectivity at finer scales can be represented using this product by stepping down the spatial data and analyses to see patterns, the WAHCAP data in such cases need to be used in conjunction with other available habitat connectivity resources specific to the area (e.g., Washington Transboundary Habitat Connectivity Scoping Report and the Okanogan -Kettle Subregion Connectivity Assessment 252 , Washington Shrubsteppe Restoration and Resiliency Long-term Strategy 253 ).
through their aquatic environments to find food, mate, and disperse during their whole life cycle. Aquatic connectivity quality -both physical and chemical -is foundational to so many conservation efforts, and we encourage plan users to consider how their actions can benefit aquatic connectivity when they apply conservation recommendations for riparian and floodplain, salmon and orca recovery, water quality and quantity for fish and wildlife, nearshore, and other freshwater-and marine-adjacent issues and habitats.

## Landscape connectivity for migratory birds

Migratory bird conservation is dependent upon connectivity that is not contiguous, rather a network of sites providing necessary habitats at a scale that requires continental scales of effort, guided by international partnerships, Joint Ventures (e.g., Pacific Birds Habitat, Intermountain West, Sea Duck and Arctic Goose), and conservation plans such as the North American Waterfowl Management Plan (1986, 2024 update), Waterbird Conservation for the Americas ( 
2002
), Partners in Flight Landbird Conservation Plan (2016), and Pacific Americas Shorebird Conservation Strategy (2016). 
254
 Washington is part of the Pacific Flyway and includes portions of the Central Americas and Mississippi Americas Flyways. These are generalized movement patterns of migratory birds from nesting areas in northern latitudes to wintering areas in southern latitudes. Through these flyways, migratory birds connect Washington to locations throughout the United States and the Western Hemisphere. 
255
 Conservation of Washington's migratory birds across their full annual cycle requires non-breeding and migratory stopover habitat conservation in addition to their breeding ground habitat. 
256
 HGCN such as Nearshore -Pacific Coast and Nearshore -Puget Sound (e.g., estuaries, bays, marine waters), Fresh Deepwater (e.g., Lakes), Instream (e.g., rivers), Freshwater Wetlands, Seagrass, kelp/kelp forest, old growth / mature forest, Oregon white oak woodlands, and other stopovers such as agricultural lands and alpine meadows provide essential resources including food, water, concealment, abiotic requirements, and resting areas needed to complete long-distance migrations. 
257
 Many species require specific habitat types during migration that offer high foraging efficiency and low disturbance, making stopover sites vital to survival and reproductive success. These habitats enable migrating birds to build energy reserves for physiologically intensive migrations to areas such as coastal Alaska. 
258
 Many of these habitats have been impacted or are impaired to such a degree, that managed systems have had to supplement the habitat functions that would have historically been found on the landscape, and lead to the creation of several significant National Wildlife Refuges and state Wildlife Areas attempting to mitigate those losses. On the Pacific and Puget Sound coasts, estuaries such as Willapa Bay, Grays Harbor, Padilla Bay, and the Skagit and Stillaguamish deltas provide expansive intertidal flats, eelgrass beds, and salt marshes that support large concentrations of shorebirds, waterfowl, and marine birds during migration and overwintering. 
259
 Inland, wetland and floodplain systems in regions such as the Columbia Basin and Yakima Valley include seasonal marshes and inundated agricultural land that serve as key migratory stopovers for waterfowl. 
260
 The importance of all wetland habitat types, their connectivity across the landscape, seasonal availability, and the related ecotones are common, crucial themes to the biodiversity and species richness in key migratory bird stopover sites.
SGCN waterfowl, sandhill cranes, shorebirds, and warblers pause to replenish themselves in Washington during fall and spring migrations, and some groups may overwinter. Importantly these requirements can occur in very different regions of the state depending on the season, reinforcing the need for annual life cycle strategies at appropriate scales.
Twelve of 13 neotropical migratory species that breed in Washington have been identified as focal migratory species across multiple groups and initiatives such as the Road to Recovery Partnership Bird Tipping Point Species, USFWS neotropical migratory bird conservation concerns, and Partners In Flight Avian Conservation Assessment database: 261
• SGCN -Black Scoter, Surf Scoter, Flammulated Owl, Long-billed Curlew, Long-tailed Duck, Red Knot, Sage Thrasher, Sagebrush Sparrow, Short-tailed Albatross; and • SGIN -Black Swift, Olive-sided Flycatcher, and Rufous Hummingbird.
Migratory birds are a vulnerable concentration in this SWAP, and many PHS migratory vulnerable concentrations also rely on differing combinations of breeding, stopover, and over-wintering habitats in Washington:
• Eastern Washington breeding phalaropes, stilts, and avocets; • breeding and non-breeding concentrations of waterfowl;
• Western Washington breeding -Cormorants, Storm-petrels, Terns, and Alcids; • Western Washington non-breeding -Loons, Grebes, Cormorants, Fulmar, Shearwaters, Stormpetrels, and Alcids; • Western Washington nonbreeding -Shorebird Concentrations. 
259
 Lovvorn and Baldwin 1996, Drut and Buchanan 2000 
260 Drut and Buchanan 2000
, Thomas et al. 2004
, WDFW 2020
 261 Road To Recovery Partnership (R2R) 2023, USFWS 2021, PIF 2025. The Avian Conservation Assessment Database (ACAD) is foundational to the R2R Tipping Point assessment. ACAD provides conservation assessment data and rankings for all North American bird species at multiple geographic scales. Note: Barn swallow (Hirundo rustica), one of the 13 focal migratory species, is not a SWAP Species for Washington state.
Certain critical habitats are important in a large landscape-scale way because they are in a sense contiguous for birds (shallow marine, coastal shoreline) and many of the habitat-focused issues are shared across political boundaries (wetland conservation 262 , oak woodland 263 , riparian forests and shrublands). Tidal and freshwater wetlands within coastal lowlands throughout the northern Pacific Flyway have been a conservation priority for more than a decade, and provide critical breeding, migration, and non-breeding habitat for millions of migratory birds each year. Habitat loss has been driven by agricultural conversion, commercial development, forestry, and urban expansion; more recently and into the future, sea level rise and increased flooding and storm surge will dramatically alter coastlines -and how people and wildlife use coastal lands and waters. The Pacific Birds Habitat Joint Venture and Intermountain West Joint Venture both highlight habitats that are also HGCN in the SWAP and important for migratory birds: estuaries, freshwater wetlands, shallow marine habitats, riparian forests, and oak woodlands, to name a few. River corridors such as the Columbia, Skagit, and Snake Rivers provide migration pathways and habitat through a combination of open water, sandbars, and riparian zones. High-elevation wetlands, subalpine lakes, and meadows in the Cascade and Olympic Mountains are also important stopovers for songbirds, shorebirds, and waterfowl migrating through mountainous flyways.
Stopover habitat availability and function for migratory birds are threatened in many ways, such as wetland loss, hydrologic alteration (groundwater depletion, changes in precipitation/drought), riparian loss and disconnection (patches far apart or too small to provide respite), pesticides and accumulated toxins like mercury, shoreline armoring and urbanization in coastal areas, recreational disturbance, and prey impacts due to water quality or habitat degradation, sea level rise, and phenological mismatches that affect the timing and availability of food resources. 
264
 Efforts to restore migratory bird breeding and stopover habitats associated with migration are primarily focused on coastal nearshore habitat protection and restoration, freshwater wetland conservation and functional improvements/restoration, invasive species management (tall emergent vegetation reduction), and reducing human disturbance during peak migration and breeding periods (e.g., dark skies / lights out campaigns, respectful recreation to allow migrating birds to replenish energy, find mates, breed/nest successfully, successfully raise broods to fledge). 
265
 Key efforts and plans that highlight these needs include Audubon Washington's advocacy in Shoreline Management Act programs, WDFW Sea Duck Management Strategies, The Migratory Connectivity Project, Salish Sea Avian Estuary Monitoring Framework, and the State of the Birds 2025. 266 

## Connectivity Conservation Recommendations

Apply the WAHCAP and local and regional connectivity resources to prioritize and retain, restore, and improve habitat connectivity that considers biodiversity areas, cores, and contributions to statewide opportunities.
Improve the WAHCAP data and information to include bird and aquatic conservation values.
• Use the bird conservation partnerships and plans mentioned in this section to improve WAHCAP inclusion of and benefits to migratory bird habitat conservation. Update the Washington Important Bird Areas for consideration in the evolution of WAHCAP habitat core data to include that value for migratory birds. Use information from the eBird "BirdCast" migration dashboard to supplement institutional data to understand the changing phenology of migration in Washington and vulnerabilities in certain habitats to better focus habitat incentives, preservation, and buffering in flight pathways (corridors). • Apply the connectivity recommendations and spatial data from the WAHCAP to enhance terrestrial connectivity in relation to a matrix of essential wetlands for migratory birds, other migratory bird stopovers, and aquatic habitats.
Implement Riparian Taskforce Recommendation 2.2.6 (riparian connectivity) and use the WDFW Riparian Data Engine to improve habitat connectivity at the landscape scale.
foot_40
Implement coastal shoreline, coastal wetland and inland wetland conservation for migratory bird habitat connectivity using strategies from the Pacific Flyway Council, Pacific Birds Habitat Joint Venture, and the Intermountain West Joint Venture. Many of these strategies would be useful considerations in Shoreline Management Programs, other land use planning, and voluntary conservation recommendations:
• PBHJV coastal wetland conservation strategies 2024 -2034 268 There are many migratory bird conservation partners engaged in Washington state. Applied conservation from the plans mentioned in this section, along with continued monitoring and regional coordination are necessary to ensure connectivity across international, national, and multi-state migratory networks to ensure that Washington has a clear view of the conservation needs in our state.
Wetland conservation: functional wetland loss/drying and continental waterbird migration bottlenecks are connected. 
269
 Use available information to better understand surface water availability and change over space and time along key migration pathways in Washington state; identify ecologically important wetland habitats and their risk of drying over decades; communicate those in water management strategy revisions and highlight those areas for conservation management and protection. Strengthen wetland/agricultural partnerships in these areas, apply the Pacific Flyway Integrated Landscape Conservation 'Water Scarcity' findings 270 to identify managed water systems in Washington that support important agricultural habitats and provide technical guidance to update infrastructure to modernize irrigation to benefit water sustainability.

## Riparian, Floodplain, and Wetland Ecosystems

Floodplains are lowlands adjacent to river systems that are subject to natural flooding. Soils, natural disturbance (flooding, rainfall runoff), hydrology (surface and groundwater), and human uses (development, agriculture) of floodplains influence the function, extent, and structure 271 (e.g., size and age classes of trees, presence or absence of a shrub or grass layer) of riparian ecosystems. Riparian refers to the land located immediately adjacent to a body of water, such as a stream, river, lake, wetland, bay, or ocean: a crucial transitional zone where terrestrial (land) and aquatic (water) ecosystems meet and interact. This zone directly influences the water and the life within it, affecting available cover, shade, water temperature, instream nutrients and structure (such as from logs, leaf litter, sediment, rockfall), and the removal of pollution from the land before it reaches the water. The amount and type of vegetation in that zone affects how well the riparian ecosystem serves those functions. A more thorough description of scientific understanding regarding Washington's riparian ecosystems and well-vetted approved recommendations are provided by WDFW's PHS riparian resources, which are considered best available science in conservation and land use planning in Washington state. 
272
 Riparian vegetation occurs throughout the state and is an HGCN. This ecosystem is life-critical for many SGCN (e.g., salmonids, amphibians, freshwater mussels and snails, aquatic insects, bog-obligate beetles, migratory birds) and provides First Foods and culturally significant plants for many Tribes (e.g., salmonberry). This system also provides critical functions like groundwater recharge, floodplain soil health and bank stability, instream and adjacent-to-stream SGCN habitat, aquatic and terrestrial habitat connectivity 
273
 , and stream and river resilience to uncertain and changing patterns of drought and 273 Although WAHCAP is terrestrially focused, the report acknowledges the critical roles of riparian corridors at all spatial scales as crucial conservation features, particularly in fragmented or human-dominated landscapes, natural pathways for wildlife movement.
flood. 
274
 Since non-indigenous settlement of Washington began in the 1800s, between 50 percent and 90 percent of riparian ecosystems have been lost or extensively modified. 275
Riparian conservation is a high priority in Washington, for WDFW, and in other western states' SWAPs. 
276
 Since 2022, Washington's Riparian Task Force, a facilitated process to identify and develop an effective suite of policy tools and funding priorities to improve riparian habitat, has developed a set of priority strategies and recommendations delivered in an amended final report in 2025. WDFW has contributed to this effort and developed best available science for GMA comprehensive plan development and revision through two-volumes of riparian science and management recommendations, as well as the 2025 release of the Riparian Data Engine. Conservation needs include protecting, restoring, and managing the vegetation communities and the ways in which ground and surface water are available to support the system. 277
Riparian policy and science that influence voluntary measures and regulation are often debated across the Pacific Northwest. 
278
 A 2023 study explored data and information comparing the current status of riparian buffers in Washinton state on public and private lands (to the extent possible with accessible information) and acknowledged complex social and cultural structures that affect riparian best practices, conservation policies, and regulation. 
279
 This work, the Washington Riparian Task Force, new Forest Practices rule to reduce warming in non-fish bearing perennial streams in Western Washington 280281 , Northwest Treaty Tribes' expressed values and concerns about the effectiveness of current conservation efforts, and regionally-specific economic sectors' concerns (e.g., commercial forest managers, agricultural producers) mean that any ecologically effective riparian conservation measures will be under great scrutiny and need to be science-supported, strategic, and context-sensitive. 
282
 There are many riparian conservation functions to support through conservation: maintain diverse and system-appropriate native vegetation (grasses, trees, and shrubs), deliver nutrients (e.g., leaf litter) to build bank soils and feed instream productivity, buffer streams from runoff pollutants, manage established invasives such as reed canary grass and Himalayan blackberry, protect soil moisture, foster cold water refugia and maintain normal stream temperatures, moderate floods and maintain stream health during drought, and provide naturally occurring mature and late-successional forest benefits such as large woody debris inputs to streams. Riparian management zones of sufficient width, native vegetation, and mixed structure are essential to achieve all of these riparian conservation outcomes. 
283
 Riparian management zones that protect the entire area where riparian ecosystem functions occur support aquatic species, enhance habitat connectivity, and allow SGCN to forage and move to meet their needs. Narrow riparian zones along streams offer some benefits to stream habitats, although they are more vulnerable to edge effects: open to predators, soil moisture loss, invasive species, and daily and seasonal temperature swings due to a higher ratio of edge-to-interior vegetation. For example, microclimate edge effects and limited substrate availability can hinder epiphyte growth and survival which are important for Marbled Murrelets, meaning wider riparian buffers (especially those considered mature and old growth or buffering such stands) provide greater benefits to wildlife like nesting marbled murrelets. 284
Wetland loss and conservation. Freshwater wetlands are an HGCN and are directly related to many SGCN across the state. Wetlands protect and improve water quality, store floodwaters and slow or reduce flooding, provide aquifer recharge, are exceptional carbon sinks 285 , and serve extremely important habitat functions for fish and wildlife. Since the late 1700s to 1980's, Washington lost about 31 percent of its wetland areas leaving approximately 938,000 acres of recognized wetlands in 
Washington. 286
 In 2024, US Fish and Wildlife Service released the decadal national "Wetlands Status and Trends" 287 report stating that wetland loss has increased in the contiguous 48 states (excluding Hawaii and Alaska) by 50% since 2009 primarily to drainage and fill impacts, and most of the loss was in vegetated freshwater and saltwater wetlands. Since 2009, this loss has accelerated despite Washington state regulation that is more protective than federal regulation. Remaining wetlands are being transformed from vegetated wetlands, like salt marsh and swamp, to non-vegetated wetlands, like ponds, mudflats, and sand bars.
Wetlands are one of the most productive and biodiverse habitats, with 40 percent of all plant and animal species living or breeding in wetlands.
