From the USFWS report: "Approximately half of all Endangered Species Act species in the United States are wetland dependent, and one key conservation action to prevent the need to list more species is to conserve wetlands. Wetlands provide stopover and wintering habitats for more than 4 billion birds from Canada as well as breeding habitats for nearly five billion migratory birds enroute to the tropics. Wetlands also provide shelter and vital nursery habitat for many species of fish and are an important source of cultural resources for communities and many Native American Tribes." And, wetland carbon sequestration is very important for moderating levels of atmospheric carbon. Wetland science and regulation are often at the forefront of land use debates as many wetlands may only be "wet" for part of the year. Even ephemeral wetlands (i.e., those that periodically dry out) have value to fish and wildlife that rely on those resources when they are seasonally or annually wet, and many species may use the damp or deeper wet areas in the soils and vegetation as cover and shelter during dry periods. Long-term adaptive conservation, management, and monitoring in wetland-related projects (losses, restoration, or creation) often do not include annual or regular wetlands change and loss data updates to spatial products in a consistent format that could better inform future project environmental review, restoration, response to changing weather patterns and refugia conservation, or ecological effectiveness of mitigation policies.
A discussion of the health and resilience of riparian ecosystems, floodplains and wetlands would not be complete without a discussion of beavers. Beavers are widely considered to be "ecosystem engineers" for their role in creating and maintaining diverse riparian, floodplain, and wetland habitats that are essential to many SGNC (including fish like salmon and steelhead, amphibians like Oregon Spotted Frog, and waterfowl like the Cinnamon teal). Beaver can have transformational and widespread effects on the type and quality of ecosystems by creating, modifying, or maintaining riparian ecosystems, floodplains, wetlands, and other habitats where they are active. Evidence shows that beaver activity can provide many ecosystem services such as water purification, reduced impacts from extreme weather, enhanced recreation, improved habitat conditions for fish and wildlife, enabling pollination by insects and birds, increasing fire resiliency of riparian habitat, and managing floodwaters in wetlands. Most of the positive effects of beavers are linked to how they modify the movement and location of water, especially where ecological functions like water retention and hydrologic complexity have been lost or impaired 
289
 . For example, dam building impounds water and forms shallow ponds and wetlands 290 , and the excavation of canals across floodplains enhances connectivity between different types of habitats along riparian corridors and improves conditions for native riparian vegetation 291 . When beaver fell, girdle, and coppice trees they also produce snags and logs 292 , which are required by many SGCN. Habitats that are modified by beavers tend to be more complex and dynamic, increasing the diversity of habitats and species along riparian corridors 293 . Together these activities lead to lush, diverse habitats that are more resilient to drought and wildfire, and can provide refuges for wildlife during natural disasters 294 . These are "intended to work together … and cannot be effectively implemented as individual strategies." 296 While primarily focused on salmon and steelhead, many SGCN and HGCN dependent on riparian habitats and the supporting functions they provide to instream habitats would benefit from implementation of the recommendations in this report. • Protect, connect, and restore floodplains. Floodplain function is essential to riparian habitat conservation. Strategic restoration, incentives and outreach (e.g., protection requirements under SMA, voluntary enhancement, conservation easements, acquisition), ground-and surface-water quantity protection, wetland protection, and regulation to deliver floodplain protection and connectivity provide essential foundations for long-term durable riparian conservation as well as protections for human health and safety in water-adjacent communities.

## Riparian, Floodplain, and Wetland Conservation Recommendations

Contribute fish and wildlife technical information, including focal areas from the Riparian Task Force and the Riparian Data Engine, for the Floodplains by Design partnership to find common ground in restoration and preservation priorities that benefit human communities, HGCN, SGCN and the habitats on which they depend.
• Use the best available information to prioritize riparian protection, connectivity, and restoration. Support data stewardship and delivery of the WDFW Riparian Data Engine (maps, spatial data, searchable information, and how to guide) and the Statewide Fish Passage Barrier Assessment (prioritize barrier removal, improve aquatic connectivity) to optimize investments that improve riparian habitat condition and function and aid in salmon recovery. Meet the nextgeneration needs for the Riparian Data Engine: secure stable funding to maintain the tool with updated data; identify a reliable, compatible data source for channel migration zones; develop a method/tool to collect user feedback to improve the tool; explore ways to provide regionally specific PHS Management Recommendations through the tool. Consider opportunities to promote synergies between the PHS Riparian Recommendations and other HGCN conservation recommendations where relevant (e.g., wetlands, old growth/mature forest, snags and logs). • Wetland conservation. Produce a contemporary/updated Washington statewide freshwater and estuarine wetland mapping inventory
foot_42
 inclusive of spatially explicit information on wetland function and carbon sequestration, carbon-rich as-yet-unmapped forested wetlands 298 , and natural ephemeral/seasonal wetlands in eastern Washington, compatible with data structures that would feed a future update to the National Wetlands Inventory Geospatial Dataset. Update and align mapped WDFW Priority Habitats and Species for HGCN (freshwater wetlands, nearshore) and Washington Department of Ecology wetland spatial products. Apply the updated information in stages as available to existing wetland protections and regulations in Washington state and use this information in wetland-impacting projects to inform avoidance, minimization, and mitigation that achieves ecological improvement. Promote wetland conservation on private lands through technical assistance and incentives (e.g., Wetlands Reserve Easements) provided by WDFW biologists, land trusts, and conservation districts, with an emphasis on those places that improve sustainability for SGCN and vulnerable concentrations. Produce wetland outreach products that showcase why seasonally or annually dry wetlands still have a conservation value. • Promote beaver co-existence and responsible relocation and reintroduction to increase beaver presence on the landscape where it is beneficial. Develop guidelines that describe best practices for assisting humans to co-exist with beavers and to relocate them responsibly and conduct outreach and technical assistance to implement these best practices. Identify areas that have potential to support additional beavers and are most likely to be compatible with surrounding human land uses, and develop partnerships with landowners and communities to increase support for beaver coexistence. Identify circumstances where additional beaver protection may be warranted as part of a purposeful effort to increase beaver populations in areas where their presence would be beneficial.

## Land Use Planning with Conservation Values

"Tremendous population growth and development, increasing land-use conflicts, and increasing lists of species and ecosystems that are of concern suggest that we are losing ground." -Washington Biodiversity Council 2007
How and where humans develop, convert, and use the natural landscape determines which species will and will not do well in Washington's future. The retention and restoration of habitat in the developing landscape also provides ecological services important to humans and communities. 
299
 Washington state has a strong legacy of laws, implementing rules, and guidance for land use planning by local jurisdictions. Through the Growth Management Act (GMA), cities and counties in Washington designate and protect five kinds of natural resources critical areas with certain principles: use the best available science, promote "no net loss" of ecosystem function and value, give special consideration to anadromous fishes (primarily salmon and steelhead), and support viable, protected populations through habitat and wildlife population connectivity. 300 Regulation to meet those requirements is supported by research, publications, and technical information provided by WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species program, universities, and other natural resources agencies and organizations. Through the Shoreline Management Act (SMA) and program administered by the Washington Department of Ecology (Ecology), local jurisdictions develop Shoreline Master Programs to protect shoreline resources. Ecology is also authorized through the National Coastal Zone Management Act (federal) to administer the Washington Coastal Zone Management Program (CZMP) to review federal agency activities and the issuance of federal permits and licenses that may affect any land use, water use, or natural resources in the coastal zone. 
301
 State-level councils also operate at project to regional scales to set and deploy environmental standards for certain kinds of project development. For example, the Energy Facility Site Evaluation Council (EFSEC) provides a process to approve industrial solar and wind power installations, battery energy storage, biofuels, green hydrogen, nuclear power, thermal power, and transmission projects that together encompass thousands of acres across Washington state. Whether land use policies and priorities consider the long-term alignment of human and wildlife benefits depends largely on the values and engagement of people and communities statewide.
All 39 Washington counties participate in the GMA by fulfilling its requirement to adopt critical areas ordinances and other development regulations. The Washington Spatial Data portal provides city boundaries and the unincorporated Urban Growth Areas (UGAs) for the 27 Washington counties that have UGAs, updated in conjunction with the annual statewide parcel layer update (last update as of this plan writing was completed April 2025). These are areas where more urban and suburban development is directed and where habitat impacts that are directly related to that type of growth are expected to occur. The current standard is 'no net loss' of habitats in defined critical areas; habitat loss is expected 299 WDFW 2009a 
300
 For additional GMA resources: mrsc.org/explore-topics/planning/gma/growth-management-act-contents 301 U.S. "coastal zone" map arcgis.com/apps/mapviewer/index.html?layers=776cb55084c549e0be8c8f6e91be24f9 mostly within areas that are not defined by a CAO where impacts do not require mitigation (see next section Enhance Voluntary Conservation for more context on rural and agricultural land effects). Washington state's population is growing rapidly, overtopping 8 million people in 2024, and is expected to grow about 1% per year based on the annual forecast, updated each November. In Washington, the largest driver (82%) of population growth is net migration -people moving in minus people moving out. 302 Over 76% of state population growth in 2024 occurred in the five largest metropolitan counties, with Whitman, King, and Spokane growing the fastest. The top 10 cities for numeric change, in descending order, are Seattle, Tacoma, Bellevue, Vancouver, Redmond, Shoreline, Spokane, Spokane Valley, Auburn and Marysville. In a summary of the last five years, the top five counties with the greatest percent population increase (Figure 
12
) are usually considered largely rural, with low population density. 303 Puget Sound Regional Council Vision 2050, a multi-government growth planning strategy, forecasts that the Puget Sound region's population will grow to 5.8 million people. 304 See also data graphic for Regional Growth Strategy: Population Growth 2017 -2050 public.tableau.com/app/profile/psrc.data/viz/RGS_dashboard/Story1 project proponents have varying levels of engagement with local jurisdictions, state councils, and resources agencies for guidance during scoping, planning, and designing development.
Existing development, even if compliant with permitting and practices at the time, can have unanticipated or cumulative negative effects as time passes and we learn more about how the environment responds to development actions. For instance, armoring shoreline habitat on Puget Sound diminishes habitat access and suitability for many marine species, from harbor seals to forage fishes; in 2018, armoring was present along approximately 27 percent of Puget Sound's shoreline. 
305
 As sea level rises and the risks to shoreline properties increase, it is likely that there will be increased pressure to build new shoreline defenses or expand or raise existing armoring. 
306
 Even with integrated "no net loss" standards in environmental, development, and land use policy, Washington has struggled to not only keep pace with, but get ahead of, ecosystem degradation and species declines as we learn more about how our built environment and land use decisions affect natural resources over time. We need to apply the knowledge we have now to the conservation outcomes we want in the future by using land use practices that consider landscape-scale system connectivity, fish and wildlife needs from potentially affected lands and waters, and ecosystem processes and functions.
Understanding trends and human dimensions in regional population growth, transportation and other infrastructure, natural resources-dependent markets (e.g., livestock, agriculture, timber) to which Washington contributes, and future technologies (e.g., offshore wind, tidal energy, irrigation) will be key to next generation land use planning and regulation. Understanding these trends allows for strategic avoidance, minimization, and compensation from these development activities, at both small and large environmental impact and project scales. There is an opportunity to increase the pace and scale of biodiversity conservation and provide benefits to people and animals by pairing the protection provided by avoidance, minimization, and compensation with additional restoration actions to recover already degraded habitats.

## Land Use Conservation Recommendations

"Strengthening the implementation of existing land use regulations is essential to maintain baseline environmental conditions. Once effective protections are in place and development impacts are fully offset, development could contribute to measurable ecological improvements." 307 PHS provides science-based recommendations stating, in general, that wildlife is best served by:
• Keeping large, connected patches of undeveloped native vegetation intact.
• Encouraging and maintaining low zoning densities within and immediately surrounding highvalue habitat areas and encouraging maintenance of native vegetation. 
305
 Habitat Strategic Initiative 2021 
306
 Sea Grant Washington 2022
• Managing road systems to minimize the number of new roads and new barriers to important animal movement corridors. • Planning open space to incorporate high-value habitat and corridors for animal movement.
• Zoning for higher densities within urban and developed landscapes to avoid sprawl.
Designate, enhance, protect, and connect Critical Areas, habitat cores, and connectivity corridors. Provide consistent and cohesive resources and technical guidance for local and regional land use planning jurisdictions (e.g., cities, counties, regional planning collaboratives) to facilitate the inclusion of these features in comprehensive plans. Technical guidance resources include Priority Habitats and Species management guidelines; Riparian Data Engine; Shoreline Planners Toolbox and resilience guidance 308 ; WAHCAP; Land Use Planning for Salmon, Steelhead, and Trout 309 ; actions that build on the progress reported in HSIL Synthesis 310 ; and HGCN conservation recommendations identified in the SWAP. Find opportunities to enhance conservation pace and scale by ensuring the avoidance, minimization, and compensation actions for critical areas, habitat cores, and connectivity corridors cumulatively reflect regional restoration and protection priorities.
Implement the 'Next Steps' identified in the 2025 Ecological Improvement Framework Proviso Report 
311
 . Improve protection of existing natural resources by improving standards and oversight of effective protection under current law and invest in the capacity and other resources of local government and state compliance, enforcement and prosecution programs. Require or reward (e.g., through extra evaluation points) ecological improvements in state grant programs that fund planning, design, and/or implementation of development projects.
For renewable energy development, particularly industrial solar and wind power and transmission, consistently inform site selection, scoping, design, avoidance, minimization and compensation for impacts to SGCN and HGCN during construction, operation, and maintenance. Encourage local jurisdictions and EFSEC to:
• adopt and promote the Department of Ecology Programmatic Environmental Impact Statements (PEIS) tools for onshore wind, solar, and green hydrogen production 312 • work with natural resources agencies' technical experts to interpret conservation-relevant data and information during site selection and project planning and ensure any compensatory mitigation contributes to watershed and regional conservation benefits.

## Pollinator conservation

Washington is home to an enormous diversity of pollinators including bees, butterflies, moths, beetles, wasps and hummingbirds. These animals play an essential role in ecosystem function and stability.
Pollinators also provide a wide range of benefits to society. 
315
 More than 80% of flowering plants rely on animal pollinators to reproduce. 
316
 These plants in turn provide fruits for birds, seeds for rodents, shelter for large and small mammals, and more. 
317
 Pollinators are also an important food source for many of our wildlife. For humans, animal pollination is necessary for food production. 
318
 Many important crops in Washington (including apples, blueberries, cherries and alfalfa) are dependent on animal pollination from both native and managed pollinators. 
319
 Human and cultural relationships with pollinators are long standing and diverse. 
320
 Encounters with pollinators such as butterflies and bumble bees are a common wildlife experience in both cities and protected areas and are often a child's earliest experience with wildlife. 
321
 Pollinator conservation is a statewide concern at all scales.
Pollinator habitat can be found statewide and ranges from prairies, shrubsteppe, oak woodlands, and old growth forests to agricultural lands, roadsides, and gardens. Pollinators rely on nectar and pollen from early spring to late summer and thus need a diverse array of flowers throughout the growing season. 
322
 Pollinators also require shelter to roost or build a nest. This shelter can include undisturbed soil, hollow stems, leaf litter, snags, and more. 
323
 In other life stages, pollinators require plants for food and safe places to overwinter. For example, the SGCN sand-verbena moth (Copablepharon fuscum) pollinates yellow sand-verbena (Abronia latifolia) but as a caterpillar it relies on consuming leaf tissue. Pollinator habitat is defined at multiple scales. Everything from pocket prairies and gardens to large, protected areas play a role in promoting landscape level pollinator diversity and abundance. Diverse habitats and connectivity are essential for the resilience of pollinator networks. 
326
 Recent research suggests that over one fifth of North American pollinators are at an elevated extinction risk. 
327
 In Washington, two pollinators are federally endangered (island marble butterfly and Taylor's checkerspot butterfly), and at least 50 other SGCN/SGIN are pollinators, some of which are highly specialized. For example, the SGCN black-fronted turret bee (Diadasia nigrifrons) is a specialized bee that has only been found collecting pollen from the federally endangered Wenatchee checker mallow (Sidalcea oregona var. calva) in 
Washington. 328
 Pollinators are facing serious overlapping threats including habitat loss and modification, introduced species, pollution, pesticides and climate change. 
329
 Much of their natural habitat has been lost to development and agriculture. In many areas pollinators are threatened with widespread pesticide use and pollution. 
330
 Pesticides can kill and harm pollinators via direct spray, through the consumption of sprayed plants, from the nectar or pollen of treated seeds, and from drift (e.g., soil contamination around a nest). 331 Furthermore, managed and invasive insects can compete for resources and introduce pathogens and parasites. 
332
 Finally, climate change is projected to worsen these impacts. 
333
 Rising temperatures and changing hydrological regimes can have lethal and sublethal impacts that affect survival and reproduction, particularly in prolonged or severely heightened periods of heat and/or drought. 
334
 These conditions may also impact the availability and quality of floral resources, both in abundance and timing. 
335
 In recent years, pollinators have begun to garner more support and recognition. 
336
 There is an increase in interest, capacity, and work focused on pollinators. In 2019, 

## Pollinator conservation recommendations

Protect, enhance and restore pollinator habitat: Protect and restore habitats that support native pollinators at all scales.
Encourage the implementation of Best Management Practices (BMPs): Support public and private land managers in the implementation of practices that create and maintain pollinator habitat while reducing harm to native pollinators. This includes planting hedgerows, decreasing mowing frequency, leaving leaves at city parks, implementing integrated pest management strategies, etc. 337
Reduce pesticide use: Collaborate with pesticide users to decrease pesticide use, especially in areas and at times where pollinators are active. When possible, aim to use pesticides that are the least lethal to pollinators and in the most targeted manner.
Research and monitoring: Lead and support work to understand basic life history, distribution, abundance, mechanisms that promote ecosystem stability and connections to human societies. Significant data gaps exist making it challenging to assess threats, status and conservation needs.
Outreach and Education: Support outreach and education that encourages pollinator stewardship and the creation of pollinator habitat. 
338
 Develop and teach curriculum regarding the role and importance of native Washington pollinators. 339

## Voluntary Conservation

Working proactively with private landowners, non-governmental conservation organizations, land trusts, Tribes, and local, state, and federal entities to implement voluntary conservation action is a critical strategy to achieving conservation outcomes. Two concepts work hand-in-hand to enhance effective voluntary conservation:
• raising awareness of conservation issues in targeted ways that inspire people to take action at home, in their own communities, and on their own lands; and • purposeful and strategic facilitation and support of voluntary conservation action that directly benefits habitats and species by addressing the causes of their vulnerability or decline Beneficial conservation on private lands occurs through local relationship-building, outreach, and conservation incentive delivery that finds shared benefits with people's values and communities. Urban residents to rural ranchers and farmers to private forest managers to land trusts contribute to biodiversity conservation. The scales and conservation effects of these contributions are different. For 
337
 The Xerces Society 2018 338 WDFW N.d.a (Habitat at Home) instance, urban and suburban residents who support pollinator gardens, natural greenspace and parks, and their own Habitat at Home can model living with wildlife, promote habitat connectivity and native habitats in their communities, and provide a bridge to audiences that might not yet connect with natural resources agencies on wildland conservation topics.
Compared to urban and suburban audiences, there are private residential areas in the interface between urban/suburban and rural, sometimes referred to as the exurban. These lands provide an opportunity to reach landowners with more acres than urban or suburban audiences (generally between 2 and 20 acres). Homeowners' Associations and communities encompassing these areas are avenues for coordinated outreach that could improve awareness and voluntary adoption of local and regional conservation recommendations, such as using less broad-application pesticides, appropriate septic system installation and maintenance to reduce failure (related to ground and surface water quality), or converting lawn to native landscaping for pollinators and other wildlife to minimize the conversion effect at the edges of working lands and natural habitats.
Working lands such as privately owned farms, ranches, and forests that produce food, timber, and fiber, support rural economies, and provide wildlife habitat, clean air and water, and recreational opportunities can be compatible with, even critical for, local and landscape level biodiversity conservation. In 2020, approximately 32% of Washington's acres were private working lands. 
340
 In 2024, Washington Department of Agriculture reported that agricultural lands (non-timber) totaled over 10 million acres. 
341
 Washington's small forest landowners (SFLO) account for 15% of our state's forest acres. 
342
 However, agricultural land is being lost in Washington state: Between 2012 and 2022 the number of farms operating in Washington state fell from 37,249 to 32,076, a decline of nearly 14%. The total land area under cultivation declined from 14.7 million acres to 13.8 million acres, a drop of 6%.
According to the most recent federal five-year report, covering 2017 to 2022, Washington lost 3,717 farms, about 14 farms every week. Over the same period the state lost more than 102,000 foodproducing acres. 
343
 Protecting these working lands and other larger blocks of private land from conversion to urban and residential development, restoring habitat and landscape linkages, and using sustainable grazing, agriculture, and forestry practices to support soil, water, air, and overall environment benefits are important conservation considerations.
There are several appraisals of natural resource conditions and conversion of non-federal land that report similar declining trends of working lands. ). Somewhere between 25,000 and 50,000 small forest landowners are likely anticipating selling all or some of their forest land by 
2030. 347
 This conversion of working lands to residential and commercial development is permanent loss in wildlife habitat potential. Those conversions also bring other issues such as lighting at night that disrupts nocturnal animal activities like bird migration, smaller parcel fragmentation that leads to greater variety of site management and requires more effort in conservation outreach, more fencing, different types and rates of application of pesticides and herbicides, feral pets that introduce different types of diseases and predation pressures, increases in managed corridors for infrastructure, and more traffic on existing roads that increase the potential for wildlife collisions. 
348
 This type of working lands conversion also impacts the success and persistence of existing and remaining working lands. While working lands can bring their own challenges to conservation, they can also provide important resources for species habitat including connectivity, cover, and forage, and can be managed to buffer and reduce impacts that come from complete conversion of wild lands and waters. Washington rangelands are a significant source of realized and potential ecosystem services through strategies that enhance water quality and quantity, reduce weeds and wildfire risk, and conserve and enhance native habitats (e.g., riparian, grasslands, meadows, and shrublands). 
349
 The potential for private lands to benefit conservation is significant and relies on willing landowners and strategically delivered incentives and technical guidance. Engaged private citizens and landowners who voluntarily support and apply good stewardship are essential to the future of conservation in Approaches" are directly related to this recommendation. Focus learning and awareness on statewide and multi-regional topics that can be related to lands and waters which people are most likely to experience or could recognize in their day-to-day living to spark curiosity and appreciation of contemporary conservation issues:
• HGCN (corridors, wetlands, riparian, migratory bird stopovers, nearshore, old growth/mature forest, shrubsteppe, instream habitats) and • vulnerable concentrations (e.g., breeding colonies of birds, forage fishes, marine mammal haul outs, roosting bats, shorebird concentrations).
Develop targeted conservation messages and materials for private landowner audiences in the following focal areas: Salish Sea and Pacific coastal; westside and eastside forests; and eastern Washington shrubsteppe to encourage site stewardship that
• reduces impervious cover and conversion to non-native vegetation and lawns;
350 Mindful Birding Ethical Birding Guidelines mindfulbirding.org/images/mindful/pdfs/complete.pdf, American Birding Association Code of Birding Ethics, and WDFW Ethical Wildlife Viewing 351 "Working lands" outreach could raise awareness of the connection between working lands and the services they provide to people (food, fiber, timber), importance of these lands for fish and wildlife even though the lands have a human footprint, and influence land use decisions about urban growth boundaries to conserve working lands.
• improves stewardship and diversity of natural habitats, including wildland fire resilience while keeping native habitat values. "Defensible space" 352 and Firewise USA © community outreach and certifications are frequently implemented in exurban areas, and adding context-sensitive wildlife conservation values to these curricula can encourage wildlife-and habitat-compatible practices while maintaining property safety); and • protects and restores riparian and wetlands, acknowledges the conservation values of HGCN, and reduces (or encourages label-prescribed awareness and use of) pesticide and herbicide application to reduce impacts on pollinators, bats, small mammals, and raptor SGCN. 

## Salmon and Southern Resident Killer Whale (SRKW, Orca) Recovery

Salmon and SRKW recovery often take center stage and much of the conservation spotlight in Washington state. Pacific salmon and steelhead are central to the culture, economy, and ecology of Washington, and SRKW are dependent on salmon for their survival. 
357
 The SWAP identifies SRKW and nearly all salmonids in Washington as SGCN, along with many of the species that depend on salmon or share their habitats. Salmon habitats -both freshwater and marine -are crucial targets for research, protection, and restoration; and the terrestrial habitats in contributing watersheds are key to habitat sustainability. Explore the watersheds related to listed salmon species (chinook, chum, coho, sockeye, steelhead, and bull trout) to get an idea about the extent of salmon recovery need in Washington state. Salmon recovery actions, including habitat restoration and protection, benefit many species and habitats. Salmon and SRKW recovery is not limited to Washington state; we share these priorities with many Tribal and First Nation governments, Canada, British Columbia, Idaho, Oregon, California, and Alaska.
Salmon exist statewide, require well-connected marine and freshwater habitats, are indicators of watershed health and contributors to terrestrial and aquatic systems, and, with their complex lifecycle and strong ties to humans' identity, they are a fascinating keystone species. Scientists estimate 138 species of wildlife, from whales to insects, depend on salmon for their food. Recovering salmon is important beyond ensuring salmon persist in Washington: healthy populations of salmon support a robust economy, feed iconic orcas, contribute nutrients to our forests and waterways, enrich a cultural heritage and way of life, and fulfill obligations to Tribes. 
358
 And yet, with all of that recognition and attention, fourteen species of salmon and steelhead in Washington State are still at risk of extinction.
We must do more of the right things in the right places to ensure they don't become extinct, which would cause devastating ecological, cultural, and economic impacts.
Legacy and ongoing land conversion for human uses (e.g., urban, industrial, agricultural, residential),, forest and agricultural practices, water diversion, municipal water demands, hatchery practices, overfishing and hydropower development have all contributed to the decline of salmon across Washington watersheds and marine waters.. 
359
 Humans have damaged their habitat quality, reduced available and suitable habitat, made migration to their home waters harder, polluted their waters, increased predation and competition (such as introduced warmwater fishes and brook trout), and undermined wild fish survival and reproductive capacity with hatchery intervention. 
360
 Invasive nonnative freshwater fishes can pose competition and predation threats to various SGCN species including salmon especially those with limited native ranges (e.g., Pygmy Whitefish).They face marine environment challenges like ocean warming and acidification, increased predation from marine mammals, altered foodwebs that limit their growth and survival, and overfishing.
For anadromous salmonid SGCN, hatchery practices have various impacts on natural populations usually related to genetics (e.g., domestication or loss of local adaptation), ecology (competition and predation), and demographics (wild fish are removed from the population for broodstock). These are significant and complex issues. Management of these risks is on-going and must continue to meet federal Endangered Species Act-related recovery goals. It must be noted that hatcheries provide essential production to support Tribal, recreational, and commercial fisheries across WA and the eastern Pacific Ocean. They also have helped ensure the persistence of imperiled salmon populations that would likely otherwise be gone. For many SGCN fish species, mortality due to fishery-related impacts (unintentional or incidental catch) is a threat that continues to need direct management and public education. While WDFW has made significant strides toward meaningful hatchery reform, there is still work to be done. 

## Indigenous First Foods Sustainability

This section was developed by a small working group out of the Northwest Indian Fish Commission Wildlife Committee and the WDFW SWAP Coordinator. We acknowledge that it is not comprehensive or representative of all Tribes' First Foods. We hope that introducing the topic in this section gives us a platform for ongoing and future learning, communication, and collaboration to amplify this conservation value through implementation and future SWAP revisions.
Indigenous conservation values are deeply rooted in cultural, spiritual, and social traditions that recognize a reciprocal relationship with natural resources, including plants, animals and the ecosystems that sustain them. Indigenous Led Conservation (ILC) recognizes that indigenous peoples have stewarded the sustainable harvest of locally available plants and animals for subsistence, medicine, ceremony, and other cultural connections since time immemorial 367 and continue to do so.
With Tribes, the state is committed to co-management of treaty protected fish and shellfish resources.
In addition to co-management responsibilities, WDFW and Tribes often collaborate and cooperate closely to manage other natural resources, and to implement SGCN and HGCN conservation activities such as restoration to benefit salmonids (in-river, wetlands, riparian; engineered log jams; brood rearing programs), dam removal and mitigation/culvert replacement for fish passage, forestry management, invasive species surveillance and response, research, and recovery actions (e.g., mountain goat, fisher, Columbian sharp-tailed grouse). Tribes contribute significant technical expertise, information, experience, and resources toward success of conservation efforts.
The Washington SWAP grapples with biodiversity loss and promotes actions that support recovery and enhance natural resource sustainability. The biodiversity conservation solution must include indigenous voices, information, and expertise as part of the strategy to connect people working to counter biodiversity loss. With this revision, the SWAP includes First Foods that meet the intention of the SWAP to start to weave indigenous knowledge and engagement into the SWAP's conservation actions and improve relationships with Tribes for future updates and revisions. WDFW and Washington state recognize the vital contributions Tribes make in support of sustainability and stewardship of these natural resources for use and enjoyment of future generations. With more time, capacity, and resources, the SWAP connection can be strengthened between revisions and during implementation.
First Foods sustainability fits into a much broader context of species that are culturally important to Tribes. Indigenous Peoples of this region are connected to the lands and waters in and around Washington; they seek to maintain a varied, wild-sourced diet that changes with the seasons, and access to other important natural resources for bodily, cultural, and spiritual health. 
368
 Those resources that 
367 Hessami et al. 2021
 are hunted, fished, foraged and collected for sustenance and subsistence are often referred to as First Foods.
A few examples include plants and their roots (sword fern shoots, alder catkins, nettle, kelp), berries (huckleberry, salmonberry, cranberry), mushrooms, eggs (birds, fish), mammals (black-tailed deer, elk, mountain goat, black bear), birds (waterfowl), fish and shellfish (salmon, rockfish, smelt, native littleneck clam). Several SGCN can be identified as First Foods. In this plan, we have not identified all SGCN and SGIN for "First Foods" species because many species and their relationships to Tribes are protected and sacred information. That said, where it was offered and appropriate, we have tried to weave that information into SGCN descriptions, habitat values, and HGCN. Species such as cedar (bark), orca, loon, salmon, eagles (feathers), and beavers are culturally significant in other ways, and may be central to some Tribes' identities. HGCN stewardship and restoration are important to conserve First Foods such as HGCN westside prairie conservation which can benefit camas and montane systems protection which can benefit huckleberry 369 (Future: Adding HGCN section).
With this section, the SWAP does not seek to identify all tribally important species, but to share that Tribes' First Foods and other cultural linkages are an important conservation value and consideration in many conservation actions.
With more time and capacity from Tribes, WDFW, and tribal consortia (e.g., Affiliated Tribes of Northwest Indians, Northwest Indian Fisheries Commission, Upper Columbia United Tribes, Columbia River Inter-Tribal Fish Commission) and their relevant committees, a more thorough consideration of species and habitats that are important to Tribes could be considered, better addressed, and incorporated throughout the plan. With this section, we bring First Foods sustainability into the SWAP, and it is our shared intention to explore it further in future revision(s). The Additional Information section could be helpful in that future work.
First Foods are affected by the same sustainability challenges as most fish, wildlife, and plants in Washington state:
• land conversion and development, and the loss of working lands • lack of terrestrial and aquatic habitat connectivity • degraded habitat where these foods are found • invasive plants and animals (green crab, quagga and zebra mussels, knotweed, knapweeds, Scotch broom); • certain types or timing of recreational activities • wildlife diseases (treponeme-associated hoof disease in elk, hair-loss syndrome in blacktailed deer, chronic wasting disease in cervid populations) • growth of poorly regulated commercial markets (huckleberries, mushrooms, floral greenery collection such as ferns and salal) • effects of shifting weather patterns and intensity • ocean acidification and sea level rise, and
• wildlife conflict
foot_44
 .
These threats have already reduced some First Food resources to the extent that they are not currently meeting the subsistence needs of Tribes (e.g., salmon and steelhead are widely acknowledged to be in this situation). Pressure from these threats is likely to increase over the foreseeable future, further reducing the availability of these natural resources to Tribes.

## Indigenous First Foods Conservation Recommendations

Identify, initiate, and/or participate in landscape-scale conservation efforts where First Foods need more consideration as conservation targets. Establish a First Foods Conservation Working Group to make conservation recommendations that enhance the viability, sustainability, and availability of First Foods to Tribes.
• Gather baseline data to identify the current status of First Foods availability compared to subsistence and cultural needs, where appropriate and at the discretion of each Tribe. • Identify subpopulations that are particularly important for subsistence and cultural needs.
• Identify areas where diminishing access threatens availability of First Foods to Tribes.
• Support and encourage sustainable harvest and collection practices.
• Identify conservation opportunities that support sustainable stewardship and use of First Foods in locations where Tribes can access these resources.
• Support stronger enforcement of existing laws related to recreation and the harvesting of natural resources, with an emphasis on commercial sale of First Foods, illegal trail creation, driving off-road in closed areas, and other damaging recreational practices.

## Water Quality and Quantity for Fish and Wildlife

Water is one of our most important natural resources and, as most western states can attest 371 , is the most contentious in conservation. Fish, wildlife, plants, habitats, and people 372 depend on water availability in the right places in the right seasons in the right amounts. While many audiences consider Washington to be "water rich," our ecology, species and habitats, and human communities are dependent on this finite resource.
Freshwater supply is limited -the Earth has a vast amount of water, yet only a small fraction is accessible and usable for human and ecological needs. Water chemistry and quality are naturally influenced by the substrates it flows over and through, and contributing watershed. Both water quantity and quality that are essential for the survival, distribution, and diversity of Washington's SGCN and HGCN are influenced by human activities: where, when, and if we catch rainfall and snowmelt, how we manage flows and withdrawals, whether we value and manage water we can't see (e.g., groundwater, ephemeral wetlands when they are dry), how we manage pollutants (e.g., stormwater runoff, industrial brine, misapplied pesticides or herbicides) and treated or untreated wastes and effluent, and how we plan for and respond to emerging issues like leaching or unstable chemical wastes discarded years ago, new regional changes in water demand (like surges in server farm (data center) development and expansion 373 ), or extreme weather patterns that influence drought, flooding, and changed timing of precipitation. These issues are tied to many of the other statewide, transboundary, and ecoregional issues in this plan.
Water quality and quantity conservation are essential to SGCN and HGCN conservation and directly linked to human needs and uses. In Washington state, the Department of Ecology (Ecology) is the primary regulator of water quality 374 , cooperatively implementing and enforcing the Clean Water Act with the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency and implementing the State Water Pollution Control Act. The Washington Department of Health has a role protecting water quality through the regulation of public water systems. Ecology also has authority over surface and ground water allocation and management, providing policies and procedures related to water rights, streamflow restoration, protection of instream flows, irrigation efficiency, and dam safety and construction.
371 While much of the contemporary water-management related writing about the western U.S. is focused on the Colorado River, California, or the southwestern states (e.g., authors Mark Reisner, Jack August, Robert Glennon), the vast Columbia River basin has seen its fair share of surface and groundwater controversy since time immemorial related to dam construction and operation, Treaty rights, irrigation, and salmon recovery -all water rights and management issues. 
372
 We often think about drinking water, water for irrigating crops, and water we enjoy in recreation or for scenic vistas. Water is also essential in other food processing, growing fiber for clothes, processing raw materials for industrial manufacturing which we rarely see Water in streams and rivers for fish and wildlife, from headwaters to marine environments. Streams, rivers, riparian habitats, springs are all HGCN dependent on the quality and quantity of water in our streams and rivers. Salmon, other aquatic fish and wildlife, and riparian and instream habitats depend on reliable water flows, especially during critical periods in their lifecycles. Inadequate flows and water depth in mainstem, pools and backwater stream and river habitats deprive developing fish eggs of oxygen, make it easier for fish predators to find their prey, make the journeys of migrating fish more difficult or impossible, and reduce essential habitat for the growth and development of juvenile fish, amphibians and aquatic invertebrates. Water in streams, like wetlands, also contributes to groundwater recharge and reliability, and the persistence and function of spring systems. Low water levels lead to warmer stream temperatures (habitats unsuitable for many cool-and cold-water dependent native fishes, freshwater mussels, and other invertebrates important to stream systems), increased algal growth that can affect the amount of dissolved oxygen available for aquatic life, and more frequent toxin-producing algae blooms in streams and estuaries. Salmon, other aquatic fish and wildlife, and riparian and instream habitats depend on reliable water flows, especially during critical periods in their lifecycles.
Instream and downstream conservation outcomes are affected by decisions such as the amount and duration of withdrawals, amount and timing of releases from dams or irrigation management, instream and riparian vegetation management that changes flows or floodplain character and function a. Additional, emerging water management issues include changing weather patterns that decrease snowpack, contribute to faster snow melt and flooding, and increase frequency or duration of hot and dry summer patterns or drought. Because of Prior Appropriation and the historical over-appropriation of water in WA, the setting of new minimum flows in rule would likely be too junior to afford meaningful ecological protection in most currently unprotected systems, so other solutions through state basin water planning, restoration, and partnership are needed.
Washington Department of Ecology recognizes the need in their 2025-2030 Strategic Plan to restore stream flows to save fish from extinction and to consider the pressures of warming temperatures and changing weather, especially drought, that increase the need to plan well for the future water needs in Washington:
• Goal 1A: Protect instream resources and senior water rights today and into the future;
• Goal 1D: Increase public understanding of water resource issues through transparency and proactive education; • Goal 2B: Improve water supply for instream and out-of-stream needs; and • Goal2C: Promote reforms to state water law to increase the resilience of our state's water resources to climate change. 
376
 Ecology is committed to meeting the challenges and working creatively and collaboratively to address current and future water needs of people, farms, and fish (which can benefit other aquatic organisms and habitats).
Several priority water quality problems affect SGCN and HGCN related to Washington's fresh, estuarine and marine waters:
• fecal coliform bacteria contamination;
• contaminated sediments particularly in areas that are highly urbanized, intensely industrially developed, or with legacy mining; • elevated water temperature, which can quickly alter or degrade an aquatic ecosystem; • increased sediment in streams which can blanket important food sources and fish spawning areas; • excess or inappropriately applied/contained nutrients and pesticides washed into lakes and streams from lawns, golf courses and working lands, which can directly poison aquatic organisms or contaminate waterways; • pollutants in nonpoint stormwater runoff;
• contamination potential from Hanford Nuclear Reservation which would affect the Columbia River system and its tributaries, and which also flow to marine waters; and
• significant shifts in ocean chemistry (acidification
foot_46
 ) and temperature (e.g., "the Blob"
foot_47
 )
Barriers to aquatic connectivity. Washington Department of Ecology reports there are 1,242 regulated existing dams in Washington state as of January 2025. 379 U.S. Army Corps of Engineers provides an online map viewer called the National Inventory of Dams of Public Service (link) which is a subset of 835 Washington dams. Dams are intended to obstruct the natural flow of streams and rivers and impound waters for human purposes (drinking water, recreation, flood control). For many aquatic species, dams and diversions have a negative and sometimes significant aquatic habitat effect on connectivity, water quality, and water quantity (especially downstream of dam operations). Through a natural ecology lens, dams have detrimental effects on streams, rivers, riparian, estuaries and marine habitats when they alter natural seasonal flow fluctuations and stream temperatures; interrupt the transport of nutrients and sediments normally deposited along floodplains, deltas, and estuaries; fragment resident aquatic wildlife populations and create barriers for some terrestrial species; and limit access between the ocean and spawning areas. Even on newer dams, spinning turbines that generate electricity often disorient, injure, or kill juvenile fish on their downstream migration to the sea. Ongoing efforts that can mitigate for the adverse impacts of past water diversions and dams include adding or improving fish ladders on hydroelectric dams, screening fish out of irrigation culverts, and requiring adequate year-round instream flows for fish and wildlife. These efforts have become more common and better-funded since the listing of numerous Northwest salmonids under the federal Endangered Species Act.

## Invasive Species Prevention, Surveillance, Detection, Response and Management

Washington's invasive species work is anchored in the statutory authorities of Washington Department of Fish and Wildlife (RCW 77.135) and the Washington Invasive Species Council (WISC) (RCW 79A.25.310 through 360), which collectively guide prevention, detection, monitoring, response, and interagency coordination. WISC has assessed more than 700 invasive species in and around Washington to understand which pose the greatest threat to the state's environment, economy, and human health. 380 Fifty (50) priority invasive species were identified in 2019 through a repeatable process, evaluating impact, risk, and the ability to take action. Some of these species are prohibited and regulated invasive species have a high risk of harming the state's environmental, economic or human resources. Prevention of introduction and establishment is the preferred approach, but when that is not possible, the state invests millions of dollars in detection, containment, and long-term management to minimize widespread and sustained impacts. For example, Spartina, a species on the WISC list, has been reduced from 9,500 solid acres at its peak to 6.7 acres over 30 years through a multi-agency and community effort.
Where invasive species have become established, complete eradiation is often neither feasible nor costeffective due to the scope of the invasion. In many cases, recovery and resilience of SGCN and HGCN rely on ongoing management to suppress and contain invasive species populations. Many of the SGCN and HGCN in the SWAP identify specific invasive species In many cases, recovery and resilience of SGCN and HGCN rely on ongoing management to suppress and contain invasive species populations.
This section takes a broader perspective on these challenges and proposes recommendations to strengthen coordination, prioritize management actions, and support long-term conservation outcomes across Washington.
In 2025, WISC developed and approved an updated five-year statewide strategy to prevent invasive plants and animals from taking hold in the state's forests, waters and agricultural lands. Evaluate the feasibility, in coordination with lead agencies, of developing an online tool or clearinghouse to document and track statewide invasive species efforts and activities, Building from the existing project pages on the Council's website would help avoid duplication and ensure agency ownership, and leverage current resources. Such a system could spatially highlight management "fronts" spatially, share best practices and management recommendations, link to existing reporting database for verified observations, enable query to track cumulative benefits, help ecologists evaluate impact (positive and negative) (limited list of example projects that could be spatially represented in one place invasivespecies.wa.gov/projects/). Any clearinghouse or data system would need to be created and maintained by lead agencies with authority and technical expertise. The Council's role would be to help define needs, convene partners, and promote a system once established. This approach would enhance transparency, coordination, and effectiveness of invasive species management statewide.

## Forest Protection, Restoration, and Health

A recent analysis shows that Additionally, forest management and other activities on state trust lands managed by WA DNR comply with the State Trust Lands HCP. This HCP covers northern spotted owls, marbled murrelets, ripariandependent species, habitat for other federal and state listed species, unlisted plants or animals that could be declining in the future, some of the PHS Priority Habitats (e.g., talus fields, caves, cliffs, oak woodlands, large snags, herbaceous balds, mineral springs, and large, structurally unique trees), old growth forests in five planning units, and unstable slopes. Many of these habitat types and features are also HGCN in the SWAP.
With the 2008 Farm Bill, Congress tasked the states and territories to assess the condition of trees and forests within their boundaries, regardless of ownership, and develop strategies to:
• conserve working forest landscapes,
• protect forests from harm, and • enhance public benefits from trees and forests.
The resulting State Forest Action Plans were completed in 2010, updated in 2015, comprehensively revised in 2020 by all 59 states and territories, and many are undergoing revision in 2025. These offer practical and comprehensive roadmaps for investing federal, state, local, and private resources where they can be most effective in achieving national conservation goals.

 State Forest Action Plans stateforesters.org/forest-action-plans/ Action Plan provides a comprehensive review of forests across all lands -public, private, rural and urban -and offers solutions to conserve, protect and enhance trees and forests for people and wildlife. Nearly annual reports are produced to inform revision approximately every five years. There is a strong relationship between the State Forest Action Plan's priority ecosystem conservation and restoration actions for wildlife and salmon recovery, the SWAP, and other conservation plans across the state for fish and wildlife resources and their habitats. Importantly, the 2020 State Forest Action Plan update takes an "all lands -all hands" approach, where governmental jurisdictions, non-government organizations, and private lands managers are encouraged to work collaboratively and cooperatively on building the health and resilience of state forested lands. WA DNR developed the 2020 Forest Health Strategic Plan -Central and Eastern Washington and is working on a 2025 update to the Forest Health Strategic Plan -Western Washington (under development, expected end of November 2025), both of which guide a comprehensive strategic approach to achieving resilience to climate and fire, building healthier landscapes for fish and wildlife, and sustaining local communities and economies with management strategies. WDFW's 2014 Forest Management Strategy analyzes the Department's over 100,000 acres of forested lands and aligns with WA DNR's approach. WDFW's Forest Management Strategy implementation progress since 2014 is updated annually.

Washington's vast and productive forests are fundamental to our natural resource economy, recreation and outdoor industry, rural livelihoods, clean water, and many SGCN (western screech owl, fisher, western toad, Olympic torrent salamander, and hoary elfin are just a few examples). Even with the importance of forests to so many of Washington's values, forest acres declined by 394,000 acres (or 2%) by 2019.

• conversion to residential uses (e.g., homes, second homes, farmettes, ranchettes) particularly in suburban and exurban areas, which signal growth direction of urban areas, and commercial development;
• internal and core connectivity disruption of larger forest patches due to conversion or harvest patterns across ownerships;

## Marine Conservation

For Washington, marine conservation includes marine waters and shores of the As one large system, these marine environments share some common conservation issues such as ocean acidification, increasing seawater temperatures, pollution inputs and harmful algal blooms, and lower oxygen levels that adversely affect sea life such as seagrasses and kelp, sharks and fishes, wide-ranging marine mammals, seabirds, and beds of shoreline clams and oysters. These two systems and the marine life within them are generally separated by the management and conservation issues that people can affect -in the outer coast, some environmental processes, contributing watersheds, and human relationships are different than those in the Salish Sea.
Washington's marine natural resources and waters shape and support Tribes' and other cultural identities, family and personal traditions, commercial and recreational natural resources economies, and history. Many of the marine environments of conservation importance in Washington are also important for international and national shipping, national security, and transportation. Puget Sound and the eastern coastlines along the Straits of the Salish Sea are developed with many large urban centers; islands and western edges of the Puget Sound are also developed, but not as urban; the northern
