404
 Wiken, Nava and Griffith 2011 While not a direct impact in the Blue Mountains ecoregion, wind energy development in adjacent areas can create impacts to migratory birds, nesting and foraging hawks, and bats that pass through or use the Blues and deep river canyons at certain flight periods in their lifecycle. Several ecological vulnerabilities in this ecoregion will also affect people, primarily related to observed and expected earlier/faster snowmelt, decreased snowpack, and higher air temperatures/warmer weather 408 :
• Earlier spring and higher volumes in headwaters and perennial streams -increased peak flows can damage roads near streams affecting access for recreation and firefighting, lower water quality for fish and aquatic invertebrates, and blowout or scour instream habitats. Floodplains and riparian areas are also vulnerable to vegetation loss from more intense flow/flooding. • Lower summer flows in perennial streams -negative effects to downstream municipal and public water use during peak demand periods for irrigation and power supply; lower instream flows, less stream connectivity, and warmer in-stream temperatures which adversely affect native coldwater fish and the invertebrates that rely on those stream conditions (may favor nonnative warm-water adapted fish); low flows also affect aquatic recreation opportunities and firefighting water resources. • Seasonal water availability, seasonal water features (e.g., ephemeral wetlands), and soil moisture across the region will decrease and, in some areas, stay dry beyond the summer season, which can adversely affect wetland dependent rare plants, migrating birds, amphibians, and aquatic invertebrates directly, and can require other wildlife to travel farther for water resources which increases body stress. Effects may be cumulative over time and persist into the future. This will shift vegetation communities toward shrub and grasslands, reducing the forestgrassland mosaic. Drought-tolerant and non-native plant species will be more successful.
Intensive dry summers and longer-term drought also affects agriculture costs, available range forage for livestock, and the need for supplemental feed. This can also produce conflicts if wildlife seek supplemental food and water where people are providing that for their crops, orchards, livestock, and outdoor pets. Ephemeral wetlands important for certain insects and amphibians and earlier, longer dry conditions will strand eggs and larvae. Low soil moisture increases forest and grassland vulnerability to intense wildfire and harmful insect damage. • Shifts in some areas to entirely snow-free mid-elevations will contribute to less spring and summer water availability overall. Vegetation, fish and wildlife may not be able to fully recover between seasons which can compound vulnerability to and effects of fire, insects, disease, and lower reproduction. • Species that depend on in-stream cool water refugia, groundwater-dependent vegetation and systems, riparian and floodplains vegetation (cottonwood, willow, aspen) and substrates, and wetlands will be the most vulnerable. Species that favor upland, dry-adapted vegetation and post-fire communities may not be as affected by these changes. 

## Coast Range Ecoregion

## Additional Ecoregion Conservation Information

The Puget Lowlands ecoregion in Washington State is home to 155 species identified as Species of Greatest Conservation Need. Among these, 17 are federally listed under the Endangered Species Act, and 31 are classified by the state as threatened, endangered or candidate species. This ecoregion is a dynamic mix of prairies, wetlands, estuaries, and nearshore marine waters that support a wide range of vulnerable species. This region is one of the last strongholds for the Oregon spotted frog, a federally threatened amphibian that depends on warm, shallow wetlands with abundant vegetation. Its populations have declined due to wetland loss, invasive species, and altered hydrology. The Mazama pocket gopher, found in the South Sound prairies, faces ongoing pressure from development and habitat fragmentation. Taylor's checkerspot butterfly, federally listed as endangered, is also tied to native prairie habitats, which have been reduced to a fraction of their historic extent. The sand verbena moth and island marble butterfly are rare invertebrates associated with coastal dunes and island habitats. The island marble butterfly, once thought extinct, was rediscovered on San Juan Island and is now federally listed as endangered. In the region's estuaries and nearshore waters, Pacific herring play a foundational role in the marine food web, serving as prey for seabirds, salmon, and marine mammals. Bluntnose sixgill and broadnose sevengill sharks, deep-water predators, inhabit Puget Sound's deeper channels. Harbor seals are common along the coast and in Puget Sound, using haul-out sites for resting and pupping. Orcas, particularly the endangered Southern Resident population, rely heavily on Chinook salmon and are threatened by prey scarcity, vessel noise, and pollution. Gray whales migrate along the coast and occasionally enter Puget Sound to feed on benthic invertebrates in shallow waters. Several species of scoters (sea ducks) winter in large numbers along Washington's coastal waters and estuaries, where they feed on mollusks and other invertebrates in shallow marine habitats. The Western high arctic brant, a migratory goose, winters in the region's estuaries and eelgrass beds. It is sensitive to habitat disturbance and climate-driven changes in food availability. Together, these species highlight the ecological richness and conservation challenges of the Puget Lowlands. Efforts here focus on restoring prairies and wetlands, protecting nearshore habitats, and improving connectivity and water quality to support both terrestrial and marine life. 

## Additional Ecoregion Conservation Information

The Willamette Valley ecoregion in Washington State is home to 78 species identified as Species of Greatest Conservation Need. Among these, 6 are federally listed under the Endangered Species Act, and 15 are classified by the state as threatened, endangered or candidate species. The Willamette Valley ecoregion hosts a mix of forest, wetland, and prairie habitats that support a diverse array of animal species, including several that are rare, endemic, or declining. The Columbian White-tailed deer is a federally threatened subspecies found along the lower Columbia River and parts of the valley. It relies on riparian woodlands and feeds on cottonwood, willow, and alder. The eulachon, a small anadromous fish, migrates from the ocean to spawn in freshwater rivers like the Willamette. Its populations have declined due to habitat degradation and changes in river flow. Similarly, the Pacific lamprey and green sturgeon are native migratory fish that depend on clean, connected river systems. Both face threats from dams, pollution, and loss of spawning habitat. Birds like the purple martin and American white pelican are seasonal residents. Purple martins nest in cavities and artificial structures, often near open fields and wetlands. Their numbers have dropped due to competition for nesting sites. American white pelicans forage in lakes and rivers and breed in colonies; they are sensitive to disturbance and water quality. The slender-billed white-breasted nuthatch, a subspecies adapted to Oregon white oak woodlands, is declining due to loss of oak habitat from development and fire suppression, as is an oakassociated beetle, Buprestis gibbsii. Among the region's lesser-known but ecologically important species are the blue-gray taildropper, a rare slug found in moist forest litter, and the Johnson's hairstreak, a butterfly that depends on mistletoe-bearing conifers. Both are indicators of healthy forest ecosystems and are vulnerable to logging and habitat fragmentation. Conservation in the Willamette Valley ecoregion focuses on restoring native prairie and oak savanna habitats, which have been heavily fragmented by agriculture and urban development. Key efforts include prescribed burning, invasive species control, and habitat restoration to support declining species.

## Reporting Progress and Update Opportunities

Engagement beyond Revision. WDFW serves the whole public and engagement is intended to foster good governance through transparency, communication, and public service; foster SWAP implementation effectiveness; and actively create and maintain innovative and enduring conservation. 
409
 As SWAP 2025 is implemented and more partners and communities learn about new available information and data, WDFW will document opportunities to ingest new relevant information and determine priority, need, and capacity to address. WDFW will be hosting engagement opportunities between revisions to promote SWAP content and use, receive feedback, and improve our next SWAP.
Tracking and reporting progress. With a full-time SWAP Coordinator, WDFW will using the SWAP database, experiences from the AFWA SWAP community of practice, WDFW institutional knowledge, and partners' suggestions to develop a way to collect information that helps the agency track and report SWAP implementation progress. There is no set timeline for this, but it fits well with the agency's efforts to track and report progress on the WDFW 25-Year Strategic Plan.
It is not enough to simply implement projects and spend the money; monitoring and documentation must show that conservation progress is being made or a course-correction is needed. Monitoring and documenting the response of targets to applied conservation actions reports conservation progress, shapes adaptive management, and demonstrates effectiveness -not just spending the money, but moving the needle toward SGCN and HGCN goals, and justifying a change in approach, if needed.
Revision. WDFW will, at a minimum, follow current USFWS Guidance (2017) for update processes and procedures (Element 6), unless and until that guidance changes for SWAP pieces that are directly connected to live data sources. Revisions require staff capacity, financial resources, and time from collaborators and can be constrained by these. Review and update will occur at least every ten years as is required, and the next revision will be due October 1, 2035.
SWAP 2025 is using a framework and supporting data structures that will enable more frequent data updates for existing SGCN, SGIN, and HGCN. This will help WDFW web-enable SWAP content, connect web delivery to data and other resources that are regularly updated, and keep our SWAP products more relevant for users. Any changes that might add or subtract SGCN, threats and actions for those species, or significantly change the conservation approach for the SWAP need to be coordinated through the USFWS minor or major revision process laid out in the Guidance prior to making the change. WDFW will be tracking requested changes for potential revisions between the ten-year intervals. Opportunities to provide citation-supported suggestions and recommendations will be posted periodically on the WDFW SWAP webpage for consideration and inclusion in the SWAP database.
• NatureServe's Conservation Data Portal for the Western United States (including but not limited to Areas of Biodiversity Importance and At-Risk freshwater systems and species concentrations)
foot_53
 • WDFW PHS Priority Habitats and Species online tools, • SWAP database or a paired and similar DNR database to include plant threats and actions using the Conservation Standards vocabulary; and • species-habitat ecoregional (finer scale) modeling of the most important biodiversity areas (e.g., map of biodiversity importance based on richness of imperiled species, range-size rarity, protection rarity, and unprotected areas with imperiled species richness).
Improve NVC + GET system spatial data for Washington state and define appropriate uses. Both NVC Groups and GET Functional Groups have limitations in fine-scale conservation project planning (NVC is available spatially but is a coarse filter; GET is presented at inconsistent spatial scales by type). To be able to use the consistent vocabulary benefits AND better relate big-picture, system-level thinking to fine-scale actions needed on the ground:
• use NVC to prioritize at coarse scale, document how NVC is used in project planning, and add finer scale system mapping to projects where feasible to share that information with WA DNR to improve NVC utility for all Washington conservation users; • select, document, and assign state-available current data sets to GET Functional Groups to create a spatial data set useful for springs, caves, freshwater, estuarine, and marine conservation in the SWAP data set.
Species distribution models. Develop and document non-salmonid species distribution model methods that can include/be related to WDFW and WA DNR institutional data sets, HUC10 watersheds, NVC Groups, and spatial data representation of GET Functional Groups for Washington state (including appropriate marine systems). For the next revision, where applicable, update species range and distribution.
Subpopulation ranges. Develop and maintain spatial data to identify where SGCN and SGIN species subpopulations exist. Initially these subpopulations will be bounded by the same HUC10 unit used in SWAP. These subpopulation locations help to refine where threats and subsequent management actions can be most impactful.
Vulnerable concentrations. For the SWAP database, create a tag or field for animal species that are part of a designated PHS or SWAP vulnerable concentration so that information can be more easily searched and considered in conservation plans.
Marine conservation organization for range and habitats. Identify the most relevant basin data set for Washington state conservation, relate the marine SGCN to marine basins, and provide a crosswalk to the HUC10s selected in the SWAP (this may be a separate set of data and relatable to the GET Functional Group related data recommendation).
Review the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan (WAHCAP) recommendations for data and product improvement and seek ways to integrate the spatial WAHCAP data with relevant SWAP data.
Review montane and ocean systems identified in Strategic HGCN (Future: Adding HGCN section) using PHS and WNHP processes in the next five years, document the findings, and include those findings in the next SWAP update to shape the future HGCN list. 

## Coordination with Tribes

Twenty-nine (29) federally recognized Tribes reside within Washington state of which 21 are treaty Tribes and eight are recognized by another federal pathway. There are several out-of-state Tribes that have traditional and ceded lands in Washington, and at least eight tribal groups that do not have federal recognition within the boundaries of Washington State. The Northwest Tribes and WDFW have an ongoing commitment of respect, unity, and alliance to collaboratively conserve and manage aquatic and terrestrial resources statewide and practice sound science to guide management decisions. Those relationships are documented by the Centennial Accord and re-affirmed with the Millenium Agreement to implement Treaties and achieve mutual goals. Conservation planning and action -such as the SWAPbenefit from the expertise and leadership that all Tribes bring to this work.
Coordination with the Tribes through webinar briefings was intended to learn more about their interests and capacity to participate in plan development and improve the State Wildlife Action Plan with information and values from Tribes. In the spring 2025 briefing, we heard that the SWAP should include First Foods as a conservation value. The Northwest Indian Fish Commission Wildlife Committee reached out to WDFW's SWAP Coordinator and worked together to develop the First Foods Sustainability section of the SWAP. In mid-September 2025, the small working group and SWAP Coordinator finished the draft content for First Foods Sustainability section in the Statewide and Transboundary section; identified black-tailed deer as an SGIN (need: better understand the effects of land use and land use planning on small localized herds that Tribes rely on for subsistence); and highlighted a conservation need for huckleberry in HGCN. In doing this work, we identified several other conservation recommendations that could improve coordination on this topic.
Asking for feedback in briefings and following release of draft pieces did not generate much content. This is not a slight to Tribes, but a fact of limited capacity to respond to the mountain of requests that they receive to participate in natural resources related decision-making and planning. Additionally, surveys are not a good method to request information as many Tribes work across their communities to discuss concerns and provide feedback, so a survey that goes out widely is not likely to represent a Tribes' shared and official perspective. We also heard from Tribes that we could do much better in our SWAP coordination, if we all had greater capacity to collaborate on content development together and had the effort at regional levels. These constraints were real barriers to being able to workshop Plan content together in a regional way to better understand conservation questions and needs. WDFW is going to explore how we could create better ecoregion-oriented collaboration and communication with Tribes throughout the Plan's ten-year period, to be more relevant, responsive, and inclusive in midstream updates.

## Agencies and Organizations Briefings

We've held several informational briefings and invited more than 200 local, state, and federal agencies and conservation and community organizations. In our last briefing, we shared draft pieces of the SWAP and asked for comments. Additionally, we have benefitted from the engagement of the WDFW Wildlife Diversity Advisory Council review of concepts and content; many of the members are directly associated with organizations that actively do conservation, research, and/or influence the conservation community. To try to get broad engagement, we shared these opportunities with the WDFW liaison network, a set of contacts that work closely with external organizations and agencies to share WDFW updates. WDFW SMEs reached out to their communities of practice, conservation partners, and experts from other agencies and organizations to help develop content for the SWAP. For feedback that resulted from each briefing in the meetings and afterward, we encouraged conservation professionals, community and conservation organizations, and others to connect with contacts that they already have in conservation with WDFW so that the SWAP Coordinator wasn't a bottleneck or route-around those good connections. We also provided a SWAP inbox and received quite a lot of feedback through that avenue from plans and data to species-focused interest, and more generalized "please keep me informed" requests.

## Public Engagement

Beyond just the plan review, we asked the public to engage with us to scope during a kickoff briefing, share their connections to ecoregions and resources during the fall 2024 survey, and provide feedback in review of materials shared in spring 2025 and the final draft plan in fall 2025. And, because WDFW Commission committee and full meetings are open to the public, we sought that audience to boost the engagement opportunities. All public webinars were announced on the WDFW Events calendar wdfw.wa.gov/events and the PublicInput project page. Additionally, several individuals who signed up to be notified were sent an email announcing the spring public meeting opportunity.

## Overview of the 2024 Survey

In mid-September 2024 through mid-November 2024 following the summer briefings, WDFW held a survey open for two months to help with scoping. We could have been more focused in our questions, and the great response from participants was a broad cross-section of the state's geography and the public's interest in conservation of natural resources. Several agencies and organizations provided additional plans and information to consider. We were surprised by the amount of response (Figure 
29
) as most WDFW surveys do not receive that much attention.
The number of participants ( 
592
) is a bit misleading; the SWAP Coordinator neglected to clear internal survey testing responses before opening it to the public. Actual number of respondents was 581, which is still a good outcome for a WDFW survey. Approximately 82% of the responses were from the public. 473 respondents filled out most or all of the survey and provided a lot of good information to consider. There was nearly even distribution by counties across Washington (both east and west participated). This is a breakdown of the respondent categories:
• 48 Washington communities or towns were specifically represented • 9 out-of-state responses • 12 different private businesses and industry organizations • 7 federal agencies, 2 state agencies, 4 local governments (1 from Canada) • 31 non-government conservation/community organizations • 5 universities.
The way respondents self-identified told us there are many values and interests that all represent conservation engagement in our state (Figure 
30
). Respondents made a clear connection between where they live, work, and play, and the importance of the outdoors and conservation when asked about the ecoregions they are most familiar with and know the best. While this survey represents only a fraction of the people and communities that WDFW serves all across Washington, we had good east -west representation and the agency greatly values the participation and gained good information from the comments which was distributed into the plan through species and habitat content development, as well as the values we seek to represent.

## Spring 2025 Comments

When we released several pieces of the SWAP for review in Spring 2025, we received more technical comments for species and habitat inclusion, greater concern for non-native invasive species, suggested edits from the Wildlife Diversity Advisory Council and a few other reviewers (nearly all taken into the edits), citations and plans (nearly all considered and peppered into relevant SWAP draft sections), and other information. We received quite a bit of support for sharing conservation values in the Compass Points.

## Fall 2025 Comments on Draft Plan Review

The draft plan was launched for Tribes' review on October 7, 2025, and to other audiences on October 17, 2025, with comment opportunity open until November 17, 2025.
WDFW provided an inbox for Tribes to send comments, and a brief survey was made publicly available to capture responses. Several respondents also sent emails and letters directly to WDFW through channels they were familiar with.
WDFW received five letters from Tribes -Cowlitz Indian Tribe, Point No Point Treaty Council, Swinomish Indian Tribal Community, Tulalip Tribes, and Yakama Nation. Comments generally supported the inclusion of First Foods Sustainability and requested more focus on beaver conservation as a keystone species and important to many Tribes' spiritual, cultural, and ecological relationships.
A great deal of interest and concern was expressed by survey and email respondents about a lack of narrative and recommendations regarding beaver conservation in the SWAP, and WDFW received extensive comments from members of the Washington Beaver Working Group, Washington Beaver Policy Working Group, Cowlitz Tribe, Tulalip Tribe, and Yakama Nation. WDFW values the commitment and time that many of these respondents are also contributing to develop 'Beaver Habitat Best Management Practices' that can be promoted through the PHS program. Beavers are widely considered to be ecosystem engineers for their role in creating and maintaining diverse wetland and instream habitats and the conservation of many HGCN and SGCN benefit from the presence of beaver on the landscape. While beavers were not specifically mentioned in the draft plan, their value is embedded in many conservation efforts. To remedy the inadequate discussion of the role of beaver in conservation, WDFW adjusted content in the • SGIN table 'Beaver' entry to include the ecosystem importance and benefits of beaver;
• Riparian/Floodplain/Wetland Statewide and Transboundary section to include how beaver support SGCN and habitat goals, coexistence and beaver restoration are actions that support our goals in these ecosystems, and the value of developing the PHS Beaver Habitat Management Guidelines; and,
• Threats/Action vocabulary tables description of Washington-centric examples to include beaver coexistence and reintroduction.
In the Fall 2025 public survey, we received responses from 90 participants. Twenty-eight out of 90 did not provide a comment, or the comment was not SWAP related. For those that were relevant to other WDFW work, those comments were shared with other WDFW Divisions. Most of the comments received were interested in the changes from 2015, conservation targets processes and results, SWAP Planning Recommendations, and the Statewide and Transboundary Needs, with a great deal of interest in beaver conservation (40 out of 90 respondents in the survey focused on or included beaver in their comments).

## Additional comments included

• more emphasis on keystone wildlife such as badger (need to describe the concepts of keystone and ecosystem engineer species for future SWAP values) Salmon and SRKW Recovery section), including fish harvest management and regulation (harvest and regulation are outside of SWAP scope so those were shared with WDFW Salmon Policy) • acquiring lands for species and habitat conservation (land acquisition is a specific conservation tool or project to meet a SWAP-identified objective, so it is more specific than covered in this plan) • recreational impacts (added in "Value inter-governmental …" and "First Foods Sustainability" section) • valuing anthropogenic habitats (there are anthropogenic habitat types in SGCN habitat associations as well as some of these values in species actions) • emphasizing old growth conservation (see HGCN: Mature and Old Growth Forest) • adding SGCN: Olympic mudminnow and inland redband trout (both of these are SGCN, in Table 
A
 and were in the public draft SGCN table for review); cloud sponge (consider in future revision with citable resources; could be part of the ecosystem engineer considerations acknowledged in first bullet). Noted/acknowledged in Statewide/Transboundary Marine section and SWAP Planning Recommendations: SWAP needs more comprehensive and inclusive content development for marine species and habitats.
• accountability targets, specific measures, legislative actions, and commitments to action (while valuable, these are all at finer plan and strategy implementation and project scales than the SWAP presents)
Topics that were appropriate for but not addressed in the SWAP 2025 revision are captured for future discussions and considerations as WDFW intends the SWAP to be modified to respond to different circumstances, information, or conditions between major revisions that are required every ten years.

## Glossary

This glossary provides limited definitions for technical terms in the SWAP. Additional topic-specific technical glossaries are provided at the end of this section as "other glossaries that could be useful to the reader."
Biodiversity. The full range of life in all its forms, including habitats and systems. See also wdfw.wa.gov/species-habitats/biodiversity Ecosystem services. Biodiversity benefits provided to humans, such as
• provisioning (food, water, fiber, fuel)
• regulating (flood, drought, water, disease, temperature)
• cultural (spiritual, aesthetic, recreation, education) and • supporting (primary production, soil formation).
See also Appendix A in wdfw.wa.gov/sites/default/files/publications/02628/wdfw02628.pdf.
Exurban. Settlement just beyond a city boundary usually beyond its suburbs with larger lots/low housing density, some economic and commuting connectivity to a suburban or urban area, and is usually the leading edge of urban population growth in a county. Interface between urban and rural landscapes.
Typically not considered working lands. The Ohio State University Department of Agriculture, Environmental and Development Economics: Defining Exurbia aede.osu.edu/node/1534.
Listing, Listed. Formal process to designate a species as threatened or endangered, usually codified in law or regulation such as the Endangered Species Act or WAC 220-610-110.
Marsh. a type of wetland dominated by herbaceous plants like sedges and grasses, often found in lowlying areas.
Swamp. a type of wetland with standing water and woody vegetation, such as shrubs or trees.
Bog. acidic wetlands with limited drainage and sphagnum moss, often fed by precipitation.
Fen. groundwater-fed wetlands with more neutral pH and diverse plant life.
Redundancy. The ability of a species to withstand catastrophic events by spreading risk among multiple populations or across a large area.
Representation. The ability of a species to adapt to changing environmental conditions over time as characterized by genetic and environmental diversity within and among populations.
Resiliency. The ability of a species to withstand (absorb, bounce back from, persist in the face of) random episodes of disturbance; is positively related to size and recovery rate, influenced by connectivity.
Other glossaries that could be useful to the reader Riparian Data Engine Glossary Terms & Definitions | Riparian Data Engine NOAA National Marine Fisheries online glossary st.nmfs.noaa.gov/recreational-fisheries/accessdata/glossary/index.
SGCN were added and removed reflecting changed status and taxonomy, and foreseeable and current environmental threats. State-listed threatened and endangered plants are included as SGCN for the first time in the Washington SWAP. Two new SWAP species categories were added: Species of Greatest Information Need (SGIN), to signal significant data gaps in necessary species information; and vulnerable species concentrations including but not limited to some that are identified as WDFW Priority Species vulnerable concentrations.
SGCN distribution is provided by HUC10 watersheds, rather than HUC 10 and HUC12 watersheds used in 2015. Possibly new for 2025 and made explicit in the data: SWAP 2025 also includes HUC10 watersheds documented by citable recovery or restoration plans.

## ELEMENT 2

The location and relative condition of key habitats and community types essential to the conservation of each State's SGCN. HABITATS Provide a brief summary explaining how your agency identified key habitats and community types essential to the conservation of SGCN and how the location and relative conditions of those habitats were determined. SGCN habitat associations. Like SWAP 2015, WDFW and WA DNR WNHP used ecosystems as a consistent framework for SGCN habitat associations, but in this revision used two more contemporary datasets: US National Vegetation Classification (vegetation types, updated fall 2025) and Global Ecosystem Typology (for habitat associations that do not have a vegetation component, e.g., instream or lakes or caves). These data sets are likely to be available for the foreseeable future, scientifically vetted and peer-reviewed (WNHP provides a representative on the panel), improved over time, relatable to coarser-and finer-scaled systems in the same frameworks, and used by other conservation planning efforts (international, national, regional, state, and local, including other SWAPs). One of the sets is spatially available which could be useful in the future to identify focal areas and certain scales of project work. Each SGCN was closely and generally related to habitat ecosystems using these two frameworks in the SWAP database.
Additionally, Habitats of Greatest Conservation Need (HGCN) were identified and related to SGCN. These are WDFW Priority Habitats and WA DNR WNHP state sensitive, threatened, and endangered NVC Groups.

## ELEMENT 3

The problems that may adversely affect SGCN or their habitats, and priority research and surveys needed to identify factors that may assist in restoration and improved conservation of SGCN and their habitats. THREATS/STRESSORS Provide a brief summary of the threats or stressors adversely affecting your state's SGCN and/or their habitats, including an overview of the process used to identify, prioritize, or inform research and/or survey actions to address threats or stressors.
For SWAP, WDFW is moving toward using the Conservation Measures Partnership tools. In this revision, a network of SMEs identified threats for animal SGCN using the CMP Threats Vocabulary (2.0, 2016) with some modifications for Washington state. Plant SGCN conservation threats are inherently part of the NatureServe conservation status ranks captured in the WNHP Biotics system and summarized in WA DNR WNHP Online Field Guide to the Rare Plants of Washington Animal SGCN threats are captured in the SWAP database and externally linked tables, and HGCN threats are in the plan text and embedded tables. Plant SGCN threats are part of the NatureServe conservation status rank assessments performed by the WNHP, are not part of the SWAP database, and are not in the CMP format due to lack of capacity to translate the existing WNHP work into the SWAP database. Resources are provided in the SWAP to review plant SGCN threats and related ecosystem conservation information.
The HGCN and Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs sections also provide summaries of larger-scale threats (and actions) that affect SGCN and HGCN.

Following RRT review and plan approval, SGCN and their habitats will be available in a future dashboard on the WDFW website based on content captured in SWAP database and presented in plan The provisions for periodic monitoring of SGCN and their habitats, for monitoring the effectiveness of conservation actions, and for adapting conservation actions as appropriate to respond to new information or changing conditions. MONITORING Provide a brief summary of the provisions for monitoring your SGCN and their habitats.
It is not enough to simply implement projects and spend the money; monitoring and documentation must show that conservation progress is being made or a course-correction is needed. Monitoring and documenting the response of targets to applied conservation actions reports conservation progress, shapes adaptive management, and demonstrates effectiveness -not just spending the money, but moving the needle toward SGCN and HGCN goals, and justifying a change in approach, if needed. Many of the SGCN Actions (Table 
L
) are explicitly monitoring-focused (those that state "monitoring," "assessment," "applied recommendations," or "applied science." Monitoring is also inherently part of project-level planning which is not explicitly in the SWAP. And, many of the statewide and transboundary needs have highlighted specific types of monitoring: marine conservation, forest health (Forest Practices Board monitoring for rule considerations), pollinators, wetland losses or gains, salmon hatchery programs to better understand ecological benefits and impacts, riparian conservation effectiveness, migratory bird patterns and needs, and throughout the plan, ecological improvement.

## SWAP Planning Recommendations, SGCN Table L, Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs

Provide a brief summary of the provisions for monitoring the effectiveness of conservation actions.
The Compass Points state this as a key value in several places, and emphasize it in "Document and report progress …" and "Monitor, Learn, Adapt, and Communicate." And, because our SWAP is specifically mentioned as an implementation arm of the WDFW Strategic Plan, effectiveness tracking will be more important in this version. Agency leadership is determining the metrics for that monitoring in 2026, and will be reporting on that publicly in the next years.

## Compass Points, Reporting Progress

Provide a brief summary of the provisions for adapting conservation actions to respond to new information or changing conditions.
Several of the SGCN conservation actions mention Structured Decision Making as well as monitoring and adaptation; the specifics of those actions will be dependent on project-level planning, partnerships, and best practices associated with the action. The Reporting Progress and Update Opportunities and SWAP Planning Recommendations sections outline improvements that could benefit updates before the next required revision in 2035, and ongoing communication and engagement that will foster better reporting and implementation. For the first time, the Washington Department of Fish and Wildlife (WDFW) has an employee solely dedicated to maintaining the SWAP and facilitating its implementation, so we look forward to pursuing and communicating these improvements. Any update to SWAP content will follow the 2017 Guidelines (unless and until those are amended and approved by USFWS and AFWA).

## PARTNER INVOLVEMENT

Briefly summarize how Federal, State, and local agencies, Tribal Nations, and any other key partners were involved in the development of your Plan and any opportunities you may provide for their involvement in its implementation.
Prior to SWAP kickoff, WDFW contracted with Triangle Associates to survey a small cross-section of the many conservation organizations operating in Washington state to better understand if and how they used the SWAP and what they'd recommend changing in the revision. They produced the State Wildlife Action Plan Assessment Report: Key Themes and Recommendations for Plan Development and Engagement which we used to inform our approach to content development and engagement.
Following the first WDFW Commission briefing in December 2023, WDFW hosted the first Tribes' briefing in January 2024, then planned and hosted additional Tribes, agencies, organizations, and public online webinar briefings, a few in-person meetings, one survey opportunity for scoping, and several comment opportunities for SWAP components and draft review (Table 
31
) through November 2025. These episodes were beyond internal coordination and presentations to various divisions within WDFW.
Conservation organizations provided a great deal of included content sourced from plans, data, expert coordination with SMEs, and survey responses.
Washington DNR WNHP was a key part of the SWAP technical team, provided extensive content and coordination for the habitat association ecosystems and updates, HGCN and cross-walking with PHS, and plant SGCN status and conservation (a first for Washington's SWAP).
Additionally, the SWAP Coordinator worked with the Wildlife Committee of the Northwest Indian Fish Commission to develop the First Foods Sustainability section to co-author a portion of the plan with representatives of some Tribes' values and conservation needs (another first for the Washington SWAP).

). [FIGURE: Figure 13. Washington Counties with the most population increase 2020-2025Population growth is not inherently bad for conservation, although natural resources sustainability requires managing growth in ways that consider human and conservation values. The ways and extents to which humans use resources, create or expand a development footprint, and generate pollution and waste can affect habitat suitability and sustainability for plants, fish, and wildlife. More people usually means more land and water use for residential development and commercial services, roadways, power and power lines, drinking water, and waste and pollutant management. New development that converts, isolates, and degrades natural habitats and working lands that are compatible with plant, fish and wildlife conservation takes a cumulative toll and introduces other issues: isolated fish and wildlife subpopulations that can collapse, more edges and avenues for invasive species, natural disruption (e.g., lighting at night, water availability decrease, water quality issues), competition and predation (e.g., feral pets), and increased traffic (animal death due to collisions, from amphibians to elk). Development]

[FIGURE: Figure 27. Self-reported representation highlights from the SWAP fall survey (WDFW 2024) Many of the habitats that respondents mentioned are also in part or wholly WDFW Priority Habitats which are HGCN in this plan, and several habitats mentioned valuable functions (e.g., pollinator support) which are captured in the Statewide and Transboundary Conservation Needs. Nearly 200 conservation concerns were expressed, across all habitat types -shores to the mountains to the Columbia Basin -and at all scales -from mal-regulated septic systems to fragmentation of large corridors that connect habitats across political jurisdictions. Briefly, here is a summary of themes: • Species -about 110 mentioned for conservation, many reflected in draft SWAP Species tables (April 2025) and nearly all addressed in the final draft SGCN, SGIN, or vulnerable concentration work • Habitats -about 108, all scales • Conservation concerns -about 200 -Top mentions o unplanned or poorly planned development o lack of habitat connectivity o invasive species o wildfire o impacts from extreme temperature and low snowpack or rainfall. o pollution.]

[FIGURE: RRT review and plan approval, SGCN and their habitats will be available in a future dashboard on the WDFW website based on content captured in SWAP database and presented in plan Provide a brief summary of significant Element 2 changes from your previous Plan. Similar to 2015, SGCN in SWAP 2025 are associated with ecosystems to meet Element 2 but use more contemporary data frameworks. SWAP 2015 used a static raster map of ecological systems (also created by US NVC); SWAP 2025 uses a dynamic spatial data set for US NVC and will be relating spatial data sets to GET ecosystems for future use. In SWAP 2015 Chapter 4, HGCN were included based on • ecological systems considered imperiled (identified by WNHP and called Ecological Systems of Concern) and • ecological systems which were closely associated to six or more SGCN. The 2025 SWAP does not use a certain number of SGCN for HGCN identification. A distinction driven by the SGCN numbers alone doesn't incorporate philosophical or policy values about what makes a habitat important for biodiversity even though that can be one good indicator. Sections for changes from SWAP 2015 • Summary: SWAP Changes 2015 -2025 • SGCN Habitats: Comparison to SWAP 2015]

[TABLE:  Washington state is approximately 43,352,831 acres. Of that approximately 38% is publicly owned and managed (RCO maintains a Public Lands Inventory, last updated May 2025).52  Private lands with public easements and sovereign tribal lands are not part of this information.While state government in Washington provides stewardship of the SWAP through WDFW, Washington's conservation community includes a broader governmental network of Tribes, state and 51 WDFW 2008, as amended federal resources agencies, and 39 county governments and communities that span from the Pacific Ocean across the Cascades through the Columbia Basin. These entities have natural resources management and stewardship roles in some way.Since time immemorial, Indigenous People have lived in the Pacific Northwest and hunted, fished, and gathered natural resources, traditional foods, and medicinal plants to support their diverse cultures. They were the original occupants and stewards of this land that all Washingtonians enjoy today. Twentynine (29) federally recognized Tribes reside within Washington state of which 21 are treaty Tribes or recognized through another federal path, such as Executive Order Tribes, act of the U.S. Congress, or Department of the Interior. There are several additional out-of-state Tribes that have traditional and ceded lands in Washington, and there are at least eight tribal groups that do not have federal recognition within the boundaries of Washington State. Informed by painful history of colonization, abrogated treaties, infringement of civil rights, and the salmon protests of the 1960s, the Northwest Tribes and WDFW have an ongoing commitment of respect, unity, and alliance to collaboratively conserve and manage aquatic and terrestrial resources statewide and practice sound science to guide management decisions. Those relationships are documented by the Centennial Accord and re-affirmed with the Millenium Agreement to implement Treaties and achieve mutual goals. lands and waters. The RCO in its capacity as administrator of state recreation and conservation grants has also joined the effort. This is a large and complex undertaking that involves agreeing to a general scope of the effort, organizing communication and coordination among independent state agencies and Tribes, and developing the planning and technical tools necessary to objectively assess and manage recreation impacts across millions of acres of state-managed lands and waters.Local governments develop comprehensive plans and regulations to manage growth, protect critical environmental features and areas, and designate urban growth areas to achieve the 15 planning goals outlined under the Growth Management Act (RCW 36.70A.020). WDFW contributes to local land use planning through the Priority Habitats and Species Program (PHS). PHS provides resource maps, technical assistance and management recommendations, primarily to cities and counties, to implement and update land use plans and development regulations.]

[TABLE: and Transboundary Conservation Needs: discussions Issues affecting SGCN and HGCN that are identified in this SWAP use the Conservation Measures Partnership's Open Standards for Conservation standardized threats vocabulary (v. 2.0, 2016) 61 with some clarifications for Washington state (TableI). This vocabulary was not available for use in the 2015 SWAP.Understanding why species and ecosystems are vulnerable or potentially less resilient is a critical first step in developing effective conservation strategies. The 2015 SWAP included a statewide assessment of species vulnerability to changing environmental conditions, considering sensitivity and exposure across most SGCN and associated habitats.62  These analyses identified species at most risk and were considered in the 2025 SGCN threats and actions work. Due to time limitations, no new or expanded vulnerability assessment was conducted for the 2025 SWAP, and newly added SGCN were not scored. For existing vulnerability assessment results, please refer to Chapter 5 of the 2015 SWAP. of many of the major issues for each topic, addressed by conservation recommendations in each section.]

| SGIN are also 'Expert Nominations' made by WDFW and external biologists, researchers, and ecologists. |
| This category highlights the need for basic status information: new/urgent survey, inventory, research, |
| and assessment. Experts have documented threats or potential risks to these species' habitats or |
| ecosystem context; may have some population or subpopulation information that indicates decline; and |
| do not have data to fully understand species population or subpopulation(s) status or conservation |
| needs for decision-making. This category is not used for 'nice to have' information. Species in this |
| category signal that a key piece of information is necessary to craft more effective management and |
| conservation. |

[TABLE: Plateau and Blue Mountains: The Columbia Plateau is dominated by sagebrush steppe, which has experienced significant declines due to substantial land conversion for agriculture, ranching, residential and commercial developments, and renewable energy projects. The region's remaining shrubsteppe cores on the Columbia Plateau are highly fragmented, with several larger and many small, sparsely distributed cores having relatively weak connections. In contrast, the Blue Mountains predominantly feature dry forest ecosystems with several larger and well-connected cores. Several major highways (e.g., I-90, I-82, US 12, and US 97) significantly fragment cores across both the Columbia Plateau and Blue Mountains, creating substantial barriers to wildlife movement.]

* , Beller's ground beetle, Big brown bat, Black tern, Black-fronted turret bee, Boreal Whiteface, Burrowing owl, California myotis, Canyon Bat, Cascade red fox, Cascades frog, Columbia Oregonian, Columbia spotted frog, Columbian white-tailed deer, Delicate Emerald, Fisher, Fringed myotis, Gray wolf, Hatch's Click Beetle, Hoary bat, Little Brown Bat, Longbilled curlew, Long-eared myotis, Long-legged myotis, Lynx, Makah Copper, Marbled murrelet, Monarch, Mountain goat, Mountain quail, Nelson's/Juniper/Cedar Hairstreak, Northern bog lemming, Northern Forestfly, Northern Leopard Frog, Northern Rocky Mt Refugium Caddisfly, Olympic mudminnow, Oregon spotted frog, Pacific Marten, Pallid bat, Puget Oregonian, Pygmy Whitefish, Sandhill crane, Short-eared owl, Silver-bordered Herbaceous balds are most abundant in the northern Puget Trough (San Juan Islands and vicinity), the driest climatic area in western Washington, and in the Columbia River Gorge, where drying winds may be in important ecological factor. 142Herbaceous balds are notable for their high plant diversity. They support a suite of rare and sensitive species including Taylor's checkerspot, propertius duskywing, Sonora skipper, Puget Sound fritillary, valley silverspot, Mardon skipper, western bluebird, and California mountain kingsnake. Maintaining early-seral conditions through tree and shrub removal, limited prescribed fire, native plant enhancement, and invasive plant management can prevent conifer and shrub encroachment into These actions help preserve the open, low-stature vegetation structure that bald associated species require.143    The exclusion of fire from most of this system over the last 100+ years has resulted in profound changes in bald vegetation, including encroachment of Douglas-fir and other woody species, loss of native herbaceous cover, and reduced habitat for open-habitat specialists. In addition to altered disturbance regimes, anthropogenic impacts such as road building, timber harvest, and recreational use further degrade bald ecosystems. Road building, when it occurs within a bald, is clearly the most destructive of these activities, resulting in habitat degradation and the spread of non-native plants. Maintaining ecological integrity in bald systems requires a combination of disturbance-based management such as prescribed fire and protection from further physical alterations.144    The absence of fire can lead to the invasion of trees and shrubs on bald(Rocchio and Crawford 2015; pp 186). Ground disturbing activities, road building, and timber harvest activities in adjacent forest can increase the spread of nonnative species on balds. Mechanical and herbicide treatments can be used to eliminate and reduce invasive species in bald ecosystems.*

*Table 12 . Inland Dunes Conservation Recommendations Washington inland sand dune systems declined approximately 76% from the early 1970s, and as of 2007, estimates were that only 106,953 acres of inland dunes remained in eastern Washington. Washington's Inland Dunes are classified by the USNVC as the Intermountain Open Dune Scrub & Grassland Group (G775). This group is globally apparently secure (G4) but considered critically imperiled inWashington (S1). Losses in Washington are primarily due to agricultural conversion, reservoir flooding and dune stabilization. Currently, the major threats to the sand dune ecological system in Washington are stabilization by invasive species, agricultural conversion including effects from adjacent irrigation, off-road vehicle (ORVs) use, intentional sand dune stabilization, conversion to residential lots, mining activities and livestock grazing.150    Juniper savanna is a designated priority habitat type in WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species program(WDFW 2008). Western juniper (Juniperus occidentalis) is the characteristic and dominant tree in this habitat. Ponderosa pine (Pinus ponderosa) and Oregon white oak (Quercus garryana) may also be present. Big sagebrush (Artemisia tridentata) is the most common shrub within juniper ecosystems. Bunchgrasses and sedges common to shrubsteppe such as Idaho fescue (Festuca idahoensis), Sandberg bluegrass (Poa secunda), and bluebunch wheatgrass (Pseudoroegneria spicata) are common within juniper woodlands and savanna. In Washington, this important habitat is mostly restricted to eastern Klickitat, southern Benton and Franklin counties. Sites with either juniper savanna or woodlands are restricted to areas with excessively drained soils, such as sand dunes, rock outcrops or escarpments.*

Kelp and Kelp Forest Conservation Recommendations Kelp also carries vital cultural significance to Tribal and First Nations communities in the Pacific Northwest, representing a physical link between land and sea as well as interdependence between indigenous people and the sea. Kelp plays a spiritual and symbolic role among many traditional Northwest Coast cultures, as it is often represented in Coast Salish stories and oral histories.*

In western Washington, they are most common on dry, well-drained sites in South Puget Sound and in the Willamette Valley of southwest Washington. Smaller, more scattered stands also occur in the Olympic Peninsula rain shadow. In eastern Washington, drier forms of oak woodland are found in the Columbia River Gorge, extending north through Yakima and Kittitas counties. There, oak occurs primarily on foothills and low-elevation slopes, often near the foreststeppe ecotone. * estimated that 76.3% of historical sagebrush distribution in Washington had been lost.188    What little remains of our state's native shrubsteppe habitat is under threat due to increased human pressure, invasion of non-native annual grasses, a rapidly changing climate, and the unprecedented extent, frequency, and severity of wildland fire; these threats are interrelated and difficult to address independently. Most of the shrubsteppe habitat that occurred historically in deeper soils was lost to agricultural conversion in the 1900s, and Washington continues to see habitats converted to agricultural , including wheat, fruit, and wineries. Now most extant shrubsteppe encompasses areas of less productive shallow and rocky soils which influence habitat quality. Grazing practices also altered shrubsteppe throughout the American west, including Washington, and poorly managed grazing is considered a modern threat to shrubsteppe habitat.189  Further, land use and development to accommodate growing human populations in eastern Washington, gravel mining, and renewable energy development (e.g., large industrial solar facilities) are resulting in significant losses and fragmentation of remaining habitat. This fragmentation and increased human pressure and presence has contributed to the increased presence and spread of invasive plants.Non-native annual grasses such as bulbous bluegrass, medusahead, and cheatgrass are heavily detrimental to shrubsteppe habitat and shrubsteppe dependent wildlife. For example, Striped Whipsnake are threatened by dense monocultures of invasive cheatgrass, as well as other weeds, because the invasive species reduce or eliminate areas of open/bare ground important for this species to move through the habitat and catch prey. It also is a threat to their lizard prey.Cheatgrass has invaded an estimated 31.5 million acres throughout the Intermountain West. Cheatgrass germinates early giving it a competitive advantage in access to soil moisture against bunchgrasses and other native species. It also dries out early in the season, providing fine fuels to carry fires across large areas and lengthening the fire season. Cheatgrass, where present, can dominate bare soil postfire, and can provide enough fuel for another fire within two years.*

*Washington Shrubsteppe Restoration and Resiliency Initiative Long-term Strategy 2024 -2054 Table 19. Shrubsteppe Conservation Recommendations While significant renewable energy generation and storage is necessary to combat climate change and meet the state's decarbonization and electrification requirements, renewable energy development results in direct loss, degradation and fragmentation of shrubsteppe. This important habitat is currently heavily targeted for this type of energy infrastructure. In exploratory planning for renewable energy developments, developers, state and local leaders should utilize existing geographic prioritization tools to avoid impacting remaining shrubsteppe habitat. These tools include, but are not limited to, Washington State University's Least-Conflict Solar Siting mapping tool (WSU 2023), WSRRI's Spatial Priorities (WSRRI 2024), the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan(Michalak, et al. 2025). * In wet prairie, hydrologic alteration, such as ditching, drainage, or adjacent upland development, lowers the water table and disrupts the seasonal saturation essential to native sedge and forb dominated communities(Krueger et al. 2014). These impacts are exacerbated by climate change that alter hydrological function, contributing to earlier seasonal drying and increased drought frequency. Together, these threats lead to altered species composition and structure, loss of native biodiversity, and reduced ecological resilience.Although westside prairie looks much different than it did historically due to the spread of invasive plants, it still has significant wildlife habitat value. Westside prairie provides habitat for several SGCN, including many state and federally listed species like Taylor's checkerspot (Euphydryas editha taylori), Mazama pocket gopher (Thomomys mazama), Oregon vesper sparrow (Pooecetes gramineus affinis), streaked horned lark (Eremophila alpestris), and Island marble (Euchloe ausonides insulanus). Ongoing restoration, prescribed fire, and invasive species control remain essential to protecting westside prairie for these and other prairie associated species.*

WNHP Listed NVC Groups that are Strategic HGCN Specialized feature habitats (Table9) are fine-scale and difficult to map from satellite data because they are difficult to detect within larger habitat types in which they occur. They may be distributed broadly across Washington but are locally uncommon. Usually, these types have specialized function(s), environment, or conditions; may host a suite of rare or endemic species; and are very important to specific SGCN and/or animal concentrations.Limited human-created habitat surrogates for native habitats were considered for inclusion in this section. Many have a strong connection to SGCN (e.g., shrubsteppe Conservation Reserve Program lands in the Columbia Basin that are important to pygmy rabbits and greater sage-grouse) or vulnerable concentrations (e.g., large legacy constructed wetlands that may serve wintering waterfowl), so they are not in this section.*

Actions vocabulary, adapted for Washington state (use Level 2).Animal SGCN issues and actions were defined by SMEs using the standardized vocabulary and captured in the SWAP database. Plant SGCN issues are part of the NatureServe conservation status rank assessments performed by the WNHP and are not part of the SWAP database. The plant SGCN status and conservation information is best sourced directly from the WNHP in the Field Guide to Rare Plants of Washington 237 and Technical Reports (population viability analyses, status reports, population trends, conservation recommendations) that support the conservation status ranking process. SGCN plant issues and actions are not in the same format in this SWAP as the animal information due to lack of capacity to translate the existing WNHP work into the SWAP database using the Conservation Standards vocabulary. As we evolve the SWAP database and have capacity, WDFW and the WNHP can work together to align the formats for threats and actions (SWAP Conservation Planning Recommendation).]

*Build and maintain support and funding for strategic and effective riparian conservation outreach and voluntary conservation. Riparian conservation is in the spotlight. Interest, science, capacity, funding, and urgency are helping to strengthen protection and restoration policies, programs, and techniques in Washington, including recommendations in the Riparian Task Force and Ecological Improvement Framework reports. 295• Fully implement legal requirements. Fully implemented, monitored, and enforced policies and regulation under the Washington Growth Management Act, Washington Shoreline Management Act, and Forest Practices Act are essential to protect existing and functioning floodplain, riparian, and wetland habitats. State agencies support implementation with best available science and technical guidance for local governments, land use planners, and land managers, and adapt that information and guidance based on management and monitoring outcomes (i.e., adaptive management). Provide state and local governments with adequate, dedicated funding for well-staffed compliance monitoring and enforcement of protections of existing riparian habitat. • The most effective voluntary riparian conservation solutions will be watershed-based and strategically implemented with strategies that resonate with local communities and regional audiences. To build and maintain support, it will be important to track implementation and progress toward watershed-framed objectives with a defined and transparent pathway for adaptive management actions. Riparian-focused incentive funding programs need consistent and stable funding and support (e.g., SCC Riparian Grant Program, Voluntary Stewardship Program, US EPA funds delivered through the Puget Sound Riparian Systems Lead collaboration, eligible grant types through Washington Recreation and Conservation Office, Farm Service Agency Conservation Reserve Enhancement Program). *and Orca Recovery Conservation Recommendations Implement the 2021 Governor's Salmon Strategy, regional and watershed salmon recovery plans, and Southern Resident Killer Whale Task Force Recommendations. 361  ,362  And, as with most fishes, lack of data on abundance, distribution, breeding habitats and/or viability status is considered a threat for many SGCN salmonid species.In 2021, the State updated its statewide salmon recovery strategy and reconvened the Natural Resources Subcabinet to coordinate state agency implementation of the identified priorities. The Governor's Salmon Recovery Office (GSRO) (RCW 77.85.030) stewards this strategy and develops a biennial work plan that aligns and elevates state agency priorities, regional salmon recovery plans, and Tribal priorities. This biennial work plan informs state budget and policy priorities for salmon recovery. In 2018-19, the Governor's Southern Resident Killer Whale (Orca) Task Force aligned recovery goals and strategies with the Puget Sound Partnership orca vital sign target and the NMFS 2008 orca Recovery Plan in the Southern Resident Orca Task Force Final Report and Recommendations. Many of these recommendations document the strong link between salmon and orca recovery. In Washington, salmon recovery is guided by the Governor's Salmon Strategy as well as regionally specific, scientifically rigorous recovery Shared priorities and threats across these regions include habitat loss and degradation, predation and food web pressures, polluted waters, fish passage barriers, changes in hydrology and fire, hydropower, and harvest. Salmon and orca recovery are critically linked. The Southern Resident Killer Whale Task Force recommendations emphasize the need to address prey (salmon) population improvement and conservation, contaminants, climate change, funding, and urban growth. Vessel impacts and ocean noise are also a significant threat to orca recovery.*

[TABLE]
*Floodplain and Wetland Ecosystems section. Support and implement the Columbia Basin Restoration Initiative (CBRI). 363 Use available funding and policy tools to implement the CBRI developed by the "Six Sovereigns": the four lower Columbia River Treaty Tribes (Yakama, Umatilla, Warm Springs, and Nez Perce), and the states of Washington and Oregon. The CBRI is the only comprehensive vision to restore Columbia Basin salmon and other native fish while meeting the basin's economic and resilience needs related to energy, water supply, and freight transportation. The CBRI looks to ramp up habitat restoration funding, catch up with a maintenance backlog at dams and hatcheries, reduce non-natural levels of avian, pinniped, and piscine predation, and significantly reduce hydropower-related mortality. The CBRI also supports a separate effort by the Upper Columbia United Tribes (UCUT) to reintroduce salmon above Grand Coulee and Chief Joseph dams. Washington is also working separately with the UCUT Tribes to advance a phased reintroduction process. Look for opportunities to expand support for all or parts of the CBRI vision to other sovereigns around the Columbia Basin including Idaho, Montana, and other Tribes.*

[TABLE: and implement the Fish Passage Prioritization Strategy. Led by WDFW, project partners include state agencies, Fish Barrier Removal Board organizations, salmon recovery regions, and fisheries enhancement groups. WDFW is also closely coordinating with tribal governments to develop the strategy. A progress report was released in September 2025. Use, curate, and maintain the Washington State Fish Passage barrier mapping tool as a companion to the prioritization strategy implementation. Riparian conservation has a strong connection to fish passage improvement (e.g., riparian protection and restoration as part of road crossing and culvert barrier improvements can mitigate influence of toxic stormwater).363  Columbia Basin Restoration Initiative and the Resilient Columbia Basin Agreement. 2024. wdfw.wa.gov/sites/default/files/2024-06/4-presentation-columbia-basin-agreement.pdf CBRI Fact Sheet. 2024. critfc.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/02/crbi-fact-sheet.pdf]
[TABLE: Improve ecological and genetic outcomes of salmon hatchery programs in Washington State --many of which WDFW is working on or already implementing, in collaboration with tribal and federal partners.364  Actions include:• Define Hatchery Goals to ensure that programs are accountable for their own success related to recovery, harvest, or both. • Ensure Broodstock Management -align each hatchery's intended influence on wild populations and the hatchery goals relative to conservation and/or harvest and be clear about segregated or integrated practices. • Right-Size Hatchery Programs to avoid exceeding carrying capacity, align production with ecological limits, and scale down where natural populations are at risk. • Improve Rearing and Release Practices to reduce domestication selection, reduce ecological interactions, improve homing, minimize competition and predation. • Strengthen Disease Management to maximize the benefits of hatchery investments and limit risk to wild populations • Enhance Monitoring and Adaptive Management with quantifiable metrics (e.g., survival and reproductive success) and adjust operations based on monitoring results to support recovery goals for ESA-listed species, wild salmon populations, and the stated hatchery goals related to conservation and harvest. • Address Ecological Interactions to evaluate and mitigate competition and predation risks in freshwater and marine environments, protect wild populations, consider marine carrying capacity, and apply solutions that address the effects of warming stream temperatures and fluctuations in seasonal streamflows. 365]

*Continue to build common commitment and action with Canada's Pacific Salmon Strategy Initiative (PSSI). 366 PSSI was launched in 2021 as collaborative project among Canadian governments of all levels, First Nations, and stakeholders. Many salmon populations in Washington depend on Canadian ecosystems and management during their complex life cycles. Washington can seek collaboration and alignment with the 'four pillars' of the PSSI (conservation and stewardship, salmon enhancement, harvest transformation, and other integrative solutions in data management, work planning, engagement, and transparency to support long term salmon viability. WDFW's 2020 Hatchery Policy Review papers[Anderson et al. 2020; Murdoch and Marsten 2020] and input from WDFW's Hatchery Division leadership 365 Anderson, Warheit, Craig, Seamons, and Haukenes 2020 Effectiveness of State Programs on Riparian Habitat Protection and Restoration Analysis and Recommendations*

[TABLE:  With the rare exception of few riparian water rights in Washington, most water use of any sort is subject to the prior appropriation doctrine, also referred to as "first in time, first in right." The rules and planning around surface and groundwater use in Washington are complex. Washington's RCW Chapter 90.03 is known as Washington's "water code" and forms the foundation of surface water management. Chapters 90.44, closely aligned with the water code but was enacted subsequently and focuses on groundwater. Multiple chapters articulate watershed planning priorities (e.g., 90.82 and 90.94 RCW). Water Resource Inventory Areas or WRIAs are closely, but not exactly, aligned with HUC8 watershed scale and are the units of local government for water resources planning to protect existing water rights, instream flows for fish, address water quality issues, and economic considerations for people and their communities. Fifteen plans developed under RCW 90.94 are done so in collaboration with Ecology which adopts plans that meet legal sufficiency, and other agencies provide information resources to support plan development and implementation. These have implementation plans and most have been updated recently.375  Groundwater withdrawals managed by Ecology are either permitted or exempt from permitting requirements if the water use meets a set of requirements generally applicable to smaller, domestic uses.]

[TABLE]
*Sufficient quantity and reliably good quality water in streams and rivers for fish and wildlife. WDFW and DNR bring SGCN and HGCN natural resources information to the table in local lead entity WRIA planning and Ecology's strategic water resources conservation and planning efforts, stormwater pollution prevention permitting decisions, and groundwater withdrawal decisions. Other planning processes and decisions also provide opportunities to engage to benefit fish and wildlife, such as irrigation district management, dam operations, dam licensing and relicensing. To ensure early and frequent coordination that can benefit HGCN, SGCN and vulnerable concentrations in these processes, agencies needs sufficient capacity to coordinate (within, among), retrieve and deliver timely and relevant information (e.g., Columbia River Supply and Demand Forecast, Instream Flow Incremental Methodology, Weighted Usable Area curves, Physical Habitat Simulation, other water sciences studies), and maintain relationships to build engagement to improve persistent refugia and stable stream temperatures and quality for aquatic and riparian habitat integrity. Coordination recommendations include: effective fish screens on irrigation headgates to prevent fish, turtles, and amphibians from being entrained in irrigation canals o irrigation efficiencies that releases to better mimic natural stream and river flows, o vegetation management (instream and riparian, maintain connected vegetated wetlands and natural floodplain character o flexible management strategies responsive to changing weather patterns that decrease snowpack, contribute to faster snow melt and flooding, and increase frequency or duration of hot and dry summer patterns or drought o necessary to adjudicate over-appropriated WRIAs to provide clarity regarding existing water rights in an effort to more efficiently manage rights. o increase the state's capacity to monitor compliance *Species Conservation Recommendations Implement and continue to update the 2025 -2030 WISC Statewide Strategy as a central framework for invasive species management across Washington. 381  The state's economy, recreation, values, culture and native species are threatened by invasives if they establish in Washington. Globally, invasive species are implicated in approximately 60% of species extinctions, and in Washington scientists estimate that $1.2 billion annually is at risk if there is no prevention or management of invasive species. Hydroelectric power, irrigation, recreational and commercial crabbing and fishing, trees and forest health, state lands revenue, aquatic habitats, shellfish, and cultural values tied to natural resources, to name a few things, are adversely impacted by invasive species. WISC's strategy emphasizes statewide leadership, WISC's strategy emphasizes statewide leadership, crossjurisdictional coordination, and integration with watershed, species recovery, and forest resilience planning, reflecting the understanding that solutions require collaboration across local, regional, Tribal, and federal agencies, as well as the private sector and the public.The strategy also prioritizes early detection, rapid response, and containment of established invasives, while promoting research, innovation, and community engagement to enhance prevention and management outcomes. By identifying priority species and pathways, WISC supports targeted interventions that reduce ecological, economic, and human health impacts. The plan calls for ongoing monitoring, adaptive management, and coordinated communication to ensure resources are invested efficiently and effectively. Sustained attention to invasive species is essential for maintaining resilient landscapes, supporting Species and Habitats of Greatest Conservation Need, and protecting the state's natural and cultural heritage. Through collaboration, evidence-based decision-making, and proactive management, Washington aims to minimize the long-term consequences of invasive species and strengthen ecological and economic resilience statewide. This multi-partner, multi-jurisdiction strategy is the state's most effective mechanism for improving coordination, leveraging resources, and advancing best practices. Extending the Council's purpose and work at least to 2032 is critical for continuity, accountability, and adaptive management as new threats emerge. The updated strategy emphasizes integration with watershed and species recovery planning, forest health initiatives, and broader resilience efforts, while recognizing the need for species-and regionally specific actions, continuing to build explicit connections with regional structures such as WRIAs, salmon recovery regions, and other existing partnerships. Council members also play a role in carrying recommendations back into their organizations, extending impact beyond the limited Council staff's work. Lead regulatory agencies update priority species lists, identify monitoring gaps, and ensure monitoring programs are adequately resourced and empowered to detect, track, and respond to invasive species. The Council can enhance these efforts by convening partners, aligning priorities across jurisdictions, and promoting tools that improve monitoring efficiency. More community engagement in detection and reporting, such as through organized community science, can feed institutional tracking systems, with a focus on the WISC prioritization matrix, WDFW invasive species efforts, and DNR Natural Heritage Program Washington Invasive Ranking System (WIRS) for ecologically impactful invasive plants.A coordinated, cross-agency process should be established to review and validate those observations and research-grade community science (e.g., iNaturalist sponsored projects) detections for targeted invasives. Vetted, ecologically significant records sourced from professional agencies such as WDFW, and Washington Department of Agriculture should be integrated into state institutional systems on a recurring schedule. These data should inform updates to the WISC prioritization matrix, SGCN databases, and invasive species mapping at the HUC 10 watershed scale, while supporting episodic response to newly detected threats. By combining agency oversight, community-sourced data, and standardized vetting procedures, Washington can strengthen early detection, improve response efficiency, and enhance long-term conservation outcomes for native species and habitats. Effective outreach and communication are essential for preventing the introduction and spread of invasive species Targeting efforts to ecoregions and HUC10 watersheds in the state where SGCN, HGCN, and any of the 50 high-priority WISC-identified invasive species occur can focus resources where they will have the greatest impact. Mapping these locations supports local jurisdictions and public outreach, while providing valuable input for the next SWAP update, which may occur sooner than the ten-year cycle.Public education should emphasize actionable steps to prevent the introduction and establishment of invasive species, particularly those with the highest potential for ecological or economic harm. Messaging campaigns should be consistent, science-based, and widely promoted, including programs such as "Buy it Where You Burn it," "Call Before You Haul," "Safeguard Our Shellfish," "Clean, Dry, Drain," "Don't Let it Loose, Don't Pack a Pest," "Play, Clean, Go," and "Squeal on Pigs." Outreach efforts should also support professional certification programs, mandatory watercraft inspections, Aquatic Invasive Species (AIS) prevention permits for watercraft and seaplanes, vessel inspections, and management of ballast water, tank sediments, biofouling, and hull fouling. These activities must be reinforced through enforcement of relevant federal and state regulations. Evaluating the effectiveness of outreach and education efforts is critical to improving compliance, reinforcing preventable pathways, and enhancing both ecological protection and regulatory adherence over time.*
