*influence timber production, long- term forest ownership and retention of forested working lands, and potential effects on ecological values. Washington state is losing approximately 30,000 acres of forest each year and the state has lost more than one million acres of forest since the 1970s; between 2007 and 2019 Washington recorded a net decline of approximately 394,000 forested acres.382  Over half the land area of Washington is forested, east and west of the Cascade crest: coastal forests along the outer Pacificfacing foothills and slopes of the Coast Range ecoregion, lowland forests and hills in the Puget Lowland ecoregion, mountain forests of the Cascade and Olympic ranges in the West Cascades and North Cascades ecoregions (North Cascades includes the ecologically similar Olympics), and eastside forests of the East Cascades Slopes and Foothills, Northern Rockies, and Blue Mountains ecoregions.383    382 Greene Economics for WA DNR 2025 Forest management for commercial harvest or ecological conservation influences fish and wildlife habitat suitability, integrity of native forested systems, HGCN representation and function, and sustainability of commercially managed and harvested forests. A significant portion of public lands in Washington State are passively managed in reserves including millions of acres of DNR lands managed under the Habitat Conservation Plan (HCP) for Northern Spotted Owl and reserves established on USFS lands including Wilderness, Late Successional Reserves, and Riparian Reserves for which management focuses on critical habitat and associated recovery plans. Timber harvest for wood or biofuel products is a priority on private industrial lands in Washington State. Changes in forestry ownership (e.g., residential, commercial, financial asset), tax laws, human population growth and trends, timber harvest technology, markets (e.g., global, local housing), and other uses on these lands (e.g., leases, easements, or sales for hunting, recreation, mineral rights, and development) all Forest management and harvest on federal lands (e.g., US Forest Service National Forests, Bureau of Land Management forested lands) are regulated by the Northwest Forest Plan, which covers 24.5 million acres of federal forest lands in Washington, Oregon, and northern California. The NWFP is undergoing amendment 384 , a Draft Environmental Impact Statement was released in March 2025, and a Record of Decision is expected in March 2026. From 2023 until March 2025, a federal advisory committee of Tribal leaders, environmental advocates, timber representatives and state and local government officials met to create a set of recommendations for the amendment to protect threatened and endangered species and the habitats they depend on, apply ecologically appropriate forest health and sustainability measures to support First Foods sustainability (e.g., huckleberry), and address issues like wildland fire, pests and invasive species, and diseases. They delivered a first set of 192 recommendations in July 2024 which address critical issues such as fire resiliency as a key component of forest management.385    The Washington state Forest Practices Rules regulate the management and harvest of timber, salvaging standing and down wood, constructing forest roads, installing and replacing forest road stream crossings, applying forest chemicals from an aircraft, and other forest management-related activities on non-federal land in Washington Ownerships range from individuals and families who own small forest to large holdings owned and/or managed by private corporations and public agencies. Small forest landowners (SFLO) account for 15% of forest acres. SFLO forest acres declined from 2.99 million acres in 2007 to 2.88 million acres in 2019 (a 3.7% decline). Total parcel acreage owned by SFLO declined from 5.04 million acres to 4.84 million acres (a 4% decline). The number of small forest landowners increased from 201,000 in 2007 to 218,000 in 2019 (or 8.5%).386  The number of SFLO parcels increased from 256,500 to 261,800 (or 2.1%). The Forest Practices Rules are established by the Forest Practices Board and implemented by WA DNR on non-federal forestland.387  The Board has several considerations that lead to Rules:• best available science in aquatic resources and upland wildlife conservation for state and federal listed species and the habitats on which they depend' • the Forests & Fish Agreement and the Forest Practices Habitat Conservation Plan (HCP) 388 ,• integration with Fish Protection Standards (WDFW Hydraulic Code Rules and permitting), and • compliance monitoring and adaptive management requirements.*

[TABLE: Ecoregions Maps and Tables Ecoregions are places with similar physical and environmental characteristics, where ecosystems are mostly alike and are mapped at a very large scale. Ecologists who map these places consider geology, landforms, soils, vegetation, climate, land use, fish and wildlife, and hydrology.402  Ecoregion characteristics are influenced by human uses, and our emotional and cultural connections to these places where we live, work and play. These mapped boundaries are helpful to organize how the conservation community can work at big, similar landscape and ecosystem scales to improve resilience and conserve habitats and species at many scales. For instance, ecologists working across the Willamette Valley Ecoregion in Oregon and Washington can share similar resources, techniques, and expertise to conserve, restore, and connect larger areas of native prairie vegetation communities; special features like dry prairie, mounds, and wet prairie; and SGCN populations and subpopulations such as pocket gophers, butterflies, amphibians, native grasses, rare plants, and related priority communities such as oak woodlands.]

[TABLE: Blue Mountains Ecoregion Figure 17. Blue Mountains Ecoregion Intergovernmental collaboration is very important for fish and wildlife conservation. The Blue Mountains ecoregion crosses adjacent state borders. It is the largest ecoregion in Oregon and a significant ecological region slicing through the middle of Idaho (Middle Rocky Mountain Steppe -Coniferous Forest -Alpine Meadow Province).407  Additional significant conservation relationships in this ecoregion include several Tribes (e.g., Yakama, Nez Perce, Umatilla, Cayuse, Walla Walla) that hunt, fish, forage, and collect natural resources for First Foods and culturally significant purposes. These Tribes are connected to a much larger community of Sahaptin-speaking peoples and regionally known as part of the Northwest Plateau Tribes from inland areas of the Columbia and Fraser River basins of the U.S. and Canada.]

[TABLE]
*West Cascades Ecoregion Figure 24. West Cascades Ecoregion West Cascades Resource Tables Externally linked file: Ecoregion -West Cascades SGCN This file has two tabs: one for animal SGCN and the other for plant SGCN.The West Cascades ecoregion in Washington State is home to 127 species identified as Species of Greatest Conservation Need. Among these, 12 are federally listed under the Endangered Species Act, and 46 are classified by the state as threatened, endangered or candidate species. The West Cascades ecoregion is home to a range of species of concern, each adapted to habitats such as forests, alpine meadows, and wetlands. The Cascade red fox, found in high elevations around Mount Rainier, is threatened by climate change and competition from coyotes. The northwestern pond turtle survives in isolated wetlands due to habitat loss and invasive species. American pikas are vulnerable to warming temperatures that shrink their alpine habitat. The American pika population in the Columbia Gorge is notable because it exists at unusually low elevations compared to alpine populations. The Olympic mudminnow, Washington's only endemic fish, faces pollution and habitat degradation. Forest carnivores like the Pacific marten and fisher depend on mature conifer forests but are impacted by logging and fragmentation. The little brown bat is declining due to white-nose syndrome and roost disturbance. The Mazama pocket gopher, though more common in prairies, has isolated populations near the southern Cascades. Birds like the band-tailed pigeon and sandhill crane rely on intact forest edges and grasslands, but face threats from habitat conversion and human activity. This ecoregion is notable for comparatively high amphibian species endemism. Four SGCN amphibians are the Cascades torrent salamander, Larch Mountain salamander, Van Dyke's salamander and the Cascades frog. Other important inhabitants include endemic streamflies and terrestrial snails such as the northern forestfly and the shiny tightcoil. Pressing conservation challenges in the West Cascades include habitat fragmentation from logging and development, climate change impacts on alpine and wetland ecosystems, and the spread of invasive species and wildlife diseases. Coordinated efforts to preserve habitat connectivity and mitigate climaterelated stressors are essential to sustaining the region's biodiversity.*

Wildlife conflict is a complex issue. Land uses, conversion, and human interactions change wildlife's ability to use habitat. Not only related to habitat suitability and connectivity, sometimes wildlife conflict leads to lethal removal of a conflict animal when other deterrence like hazing doesn't work (e.g., elk in cranberry bogs, deer in orchards, black bear in tree farms). While such removals are considered in game population management and season-setting processes that engage Tribes and stakeholders (Game Management Plan process and status and trend reports), other conflicts are related to more localized subpopulation conservation, social tolerance, and land use decisions.
As of December 2024, Ecology adopted 15 watershed plans ecology.wa.gov/water-shorelines/watersupply/improving-streamflows/watershed-planning (accessed September 2025). See also ecology.wa.gov/watershorelines/water-supply/improving-streamflows/watershed-plan-archive.
Ocean acidification is caused by carbon dioxide (CO2) absorption in seawater and wastewater discharges high in organic pollutants. More than 30% of Puget Sound's marine species are vulnerable to ocean acidification which affects their ability to form their shells, skeletons, and other calcified body parts (WDFW 2015).
A suffocating, hot patch of marine waters called "the Blob" caused massive seabird, Pacific cod, and other animal die-offs in 2013, and caused toxic algal blooms and may have contributed to a 75 percent decline in whale mother-calf encounters; more research has been done since that event and the term "Blob" remains a cultural reference for this long-term warming pool in the Pacific Ocean sciencealert.com/scientists-find-what-s-beenfueling-the-murderous-ocean-heatwave-called-the-blob.
The wildland urban interface can be a place of conflicting values to protect human development and wildlife habitat; cooperation and communication are key.

## Blue Mountains Resource Table

Externally linked file: Ecoregion -Blue Mountains SGCN This file has two tabs: one for animal SGCN and the other for plant SGCN.

## Additional Ecoregion Conservation Information

Located in the southeastern-most corner of Washington, the Blue Mountains ecoregion has a large core of public lands, centered around the Wenaha -Tucannon Wilderness with surrounding public lands held by US Forest Service, WA DNR, WDFW (Asotin Creek, Chief Joseph, and W.T. Wooten which together have 10 units), and BLM. The Snake River and Grande Ronde Rivers and their contributing waters are in rugged incised canyons and several stretches in Washington, Oregon, and Idaho are designated Wild and/or Scenic. The ecoregion has a rugged, mostly natural feel, with diverse recreation opportunities (e.g., hiking, hunting, fishing, river rafting, birdwatching). Most of the human population is around the ecoregion edges, and most private landholdings in this ecoregion are in ranching, dryland farming, and orchards. Economic drivers are varied, and most jobs in the region are government (university, state agency), with manufacturing, health care, retail, hotel and food service, and agriculture, forestry, fishing and hunting following. Job growth is highest in utilities (236% in the last five years) with wind power development driving most of that growth. 
406
 The Washington portion of the Blue Mountains ecoregion is home to 71 species identified as Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN). Among these, four are federally listed under the Endangered Species Act, and 23 are classified by the state as threatened, endangered, or candidate species. This ecoregion offers essential habitat for a wide range of vulnerable wildlife. The Suckley cuckoo bumble bee, for example, depends on healthy populations of host bees and faces significant threats from habitat degradation and pesticide exposure. Mann's mollusk-eating ground beetle is tied to relatively moist environments, making it particularly sensitive to climate shifts. Cold, fast-moving streams support species like the Rocky Mountain tailed frog and the harlequin duck, both of which are at risk from sedimentation and disturbances caused by logging and grazing. The margined sculpin, a rare cold-water fish found only in a few southeastern Washington streams, also relies on clean, fast-flowing waters and is similarly threatened by habitat disruption. Forest-dwelling insectivores like the silver-haired bat face pressures from tree removal and wind energy development, underscoring the importance of protecting forest and stream ecosystems. Bighorn sheep inhabit the rugged landscapes of the Wenaha-Tucannon Wilderness and Asotin Creek, where they contend with disease outbreaks and fragmented habitats. Some species in the Blue Mountains are so cryptic that they often go unnoticed, such as the Limestone Point mountainsnail, which survives only on talus slopes at a single known site and is highly susceptible to disturbance due to its limited range and specific habitat needs.

## Coast Range Resource Tables

Externally linked file: Ecoregion -Coast Range SGCN This file has two tabs: one for animal SGCN and the other for plant SGCN.

## Additional Ecoregion Conservation Information

The Coast Range ecoregion in Washington State is home to 155 species identified as Species of Greatest Conservation Need. Among these, 18 are federally listed under the Endangered Species Act, and 46 are classified by the state as threatened, endangered or candidate species. Washington's Coast Range ecoregion supports a diverse array of species of concern across forest, freshwater, and marine habitats.
The northern spotted owl and marbled murrelet rely on old-growth forests and face threats from logging and habitat fragmentation. The Olympic marmot, endemic to the Olympic Peninsula, is vulnerable to climate change. Amphibians like the Olympic torrent salamander and coastal tailed frog are sensitive to water quality and streamside disturbance. Rivers and estuaries in the region are vital for salmon species. Rockfish thrive in the cold, nutrient-rich waters off Washington's rugged coastline, sheltering among rocky reefs and kelp forests that provide both food and protection. Large marine mammals including the Steller's sea lion and sea otter are found in adjacent waters of the Pacific Ocean.
The coastal habitats also support the western snowy plover, a small shorebird that nests on sparsely vegetated beaches and dunes. This species is highly sensitive to human disturbance, habitat loss, and predation, making conservation of its nesting areas a priority along Washington's outer coast. Offshore, the Coast Range supports key marine species such as tufted puffin, Cassin's auklet, Brandt's cormorant, black-footed albatross, and large whales. These species reflect the health of Washington's outer coast, where ocean productivity, prey availability, and secure nesting habitat are essential. Coordinated management of seabird colonies, coastal cliffs, and offshore foraging zones is critical to maintaining these species and the ecosystems they represent. Bogs provide essential habitat for the Beller's ground beetle and Hatch's click beetle, both of which are uniquely adapted to and found only within these wetland ecosystems. Ecoregion conservation efforts focus on restoring forest and stream habitats while integrating marine protections to support long-term biodiversity.

## Columbia Plateau Resource Tables

Externally linked file: Ecoregion -Columbia Plateau SGCN This file has two tabs: one for animal SGCN and the other for plant SGCN.

## Additional Ecoregion Conservation Information

The Columbia Plateau ecoregion in Washington State is home to 136 animal SGCN. Among these, six are federally listed under the Endangered Species Act, and 50 are classified by the state as threatened, endangered or candidate species. The Columbia Plateau supports a range of species of concern, many of which are closely tied to the region's shrinking shrubsteppe habitat. Among the most notable are the greater sage-grouse and the Columbian sharp-tailed grouse, both of which have experienced significant declines due to habitat fragmentation, invasive species, and agricultural development. The Columbia Basin pygmy rabbit, federally listed as endangered, is one of the smallest rabbit species in North America and depends on dense sagebrush for food and cover. The burrowing owl, a captivating groundnesting bird, is also in decline due to the loss of open grasslands and suitable nesting burrows, often created by animals like ground squirrels. Amphibians and reptiles such as the striped whipsnake and the northern leopard frog are also vulnerable to habitat degradation and climate change. Invertebrates of concern include a number of isolated gastropods including the Columbia Oregonian, Limestone Point mountainsnail and the Poplar Oregonian, which are dependent on specialized microhabitats such as talus. Efforts to protect these species focus on restoring native vegetation, improving habitat connectivity, and mitigating the impacts of human development across the ecoregion.

## East Cascades Slopes and Foothills Resource Tables

Externally linked file: Ecoregion -East Cascades SGCN This file has two tabs: one for animal SGCN and the other for plant SGCN.

## Additional Ecoregion Conservation Information

The East Cascades Slopes and Foothills ecoregion is home to 132 species identified as Species of Greatest Conservation Need. Among these, five are federally listed under the Endangered Species Act, and 29 are classified by the state as threatened, endangered or candidate species. Species of Greatest Conservation Need in the East Cascades ecoregion of Washington include the fisher, a secretive forest carnivore that was reintroduced after being extirpated and the western gray squirrel, a threatened species that depends on oak-conifer woodlands and faces competition from non-native squirrels.
Townsend's big-eared bat, sensitive to disturbance and reliant on caves and old buildings, is vulnerable to habitat loss. Black-tailed and white-tailed jackrabbits are indicators of healthy shrubsteppe systems and face similar threats such as habitat connectivity, as are Washington and Townsend's ground squirrels. American pikas are vulnerable to warming temperatures that shrink their alpine habitat. Diverse birds found in this ecoregion include white-tailed ptarmigan, long-billed curlew, sagebrush sparrow and the loggerhead shrike. The Larch Mountain salamander, endemic to the Cascades and found in moist talus slopes, is highly sensitive to changes in microhabitat and forest cover. Fish of note in the East Cascades include mountain sucker, pygmy whitefish and lamprey, as well as three salmon species and bull trout. Invertebrates include endemic caddisflies and sensitive aquatic snails such as shortface lanx and ashy pebblesnail, all dependent on clean, cold streams. Three species of bumble bee are found in the East Cascades ecoregion: Western bumble bee, Morrison's bumble bee and Suckley cuckoo bumble bee. It's striking that one of the threats facing the Suckley cuckoo bumble bee is the dwindling number of its host, the western bumble bee.

## SWAP Planning Recommendations

During this revision, WDFW and conservation partners identified recommendations for SWAP improvement that could be pursued between revisions to improve organization, data collection and processing, synthesis, or information delivery. In addition to the stated needs below, Wildlife Program Science has outlined more technical approaches to many of these and more SWAP planning improvements that could not be captured in time for submittal. Between submittal and the next update, this section will be updated to reflect more of these technical approaches, and many of those may be used to craft specific implementation projects under the topics identified in this section.
Ecoregional Synthesis. A more useful product from this SWAP's work would be to provide ecoregion slices of the implementation resources, conservation action themes from the SGCN work, all tied to species and systems that benefit from those actions. That synthesis could improve implementation and indicate that multiple species in an ecoregion benefit from similar actions. This will be pursued during implementation when we have the SWAP database fully functional (easier to query this large set of information). In the future, WDFW will update the Reporting Progress and Update Opportunities section with the information sources and processes to do that work.
Taxonomy and status database. The Taxo database used in this SWAP version is stewarded and curated in the Wildlife Program and used by several WDFW Divisions in other Programs. While it is often recognized as an agency asset, more consistent, agency-wide acceptance and use would improve species taxonomy and status information tracking and delivery. To address this, the Taxo database modernization project could be prioritized, which would also help WDFW create a clearer relationship between Taxo and national taxonomy and status databases (e.g., ITIS, NatureServe, GBIF). Improve access to and awareness of this database via web services. Provide a mechanism for staff to request changes. Link species information (e.g., species profiles on WDFW webpages) so that any changes to taxonomic records (e.g., a status change) are immediately reflected in all places that consume this information. A more contemporary and useful WDFW taxonomy and status database could also provide non-Linnaean species classifications (e.g., furbearers, soil-dependent prairie species, migratory bird groups) that align with WDFW conservation actions and business needs.
Update NatureServe conservation status S-ranks for a prioritized suite of animal SGCN to ensure that information is available for DNR Heritage Plan updates, western states conservation initiatives, and national pattern recognition for conservation needs (e.g., freshwater turtles, freshwater mussels). Many states use NatureServe as their biodiversity data curation system and conservation planning support. Develop a regularly occurring update between WA DNR WNHP and WDFW for species occurrence information in the NatureServe format. 

## Appendices

Appendix A. Regional Review Team Roadmap
Using the template provided in 2025 by USFWS, this Appendix helps the RRT find specific Required Elements and understand changes from the 2015 SWAP. It has been modified for a grayscale/white accessible color scheme.

## ELEMENT 1

The distribution and abundance of species of wildlife, including low and declining populations as each State fish and wildlife agency deems appropriate, that are indicative of the diversity and health of the State's Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN). SGCN Provide a summary of the methods, criteria, and processes used for selection of your SGCN. SGCN Selection. A WDFW technical team convened to review the 2015 SGCN process, SWAP best practices since 2015, and available information sources that could be used to refine the universe of Washington species into a reasonable list of SWAP species for the 2025 revision. A first set of coarse filters was approved and applied; a second set of filters and information were applied by WDFW SMEs in consultation with their professional networks to arrive at three sets of SWAP species: SGCN, SGIN, and vulnerable concentrations. SGCN selections were entered into the SWAP database with citations supporting their selection. SGCN lists were reviewed internally and externally for changes through November 2025. ## SGCN

Following RRT review and plan approval, SGCN and their distributions will be available in a future dashboard on the WDFW website based on content captured in SWAP database and presented in plan Provide a brief summary of significant Element 1 changes from your previous Plan. In compliance with the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service's guidance from December 2017, this letter serves as notice that Washington Department of Fish and Wildlife will be conducting a comprehensive review and revision of our State Wildlife Action Plan (SWAP). We plan to submit our revised SWAP by October 1, 2025.

## Appendix B. USFWS Administration

We intend to revise the SWAP with improvements toward implementation, utility by others and WDFW, and relevance across the spectrum of conservation practitioners and interested parties:
• Element 1-update the SGCN list to include changes in threat level or conservation status and incorporating plants. • Element 2 -Revise descriptions and status of key habitats to be consistent with the National Vegetation Classification and better align with important Washington conservation products that use a habitat-based framework. • Elements 3 and 4 -update threats and priority actions using lexicons that align well with regional conservation approaches and more directly provide climate adaption guidance. • Element 5 -Include monitoring and adaption actions that feed our understanding of effectiveness. • Element 6 -meet the October 1, 2025, deadline and identify future update timelines.
• Element 7 -conduct meaningful engagement with tribes, stakeholders, conservation partners and groups, communities and planners who can affect species and habitat conservation through their programs and actions. • Element 8 -Conduct public scoping and engagement opportunities for the plan revision and provide an outreach plan for future engagement in implementation.
Lia McLaughlin in your office has provided USFWS guidance resources which we will reference. We also intend to use several Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies guidance documents (
https://www.fishwildlife.org/afwa-informs/state-wildlife-action-plans
) to update our SWAP with improved tribal engagement, climate responsive actions, landscape-scale conservation, and other best practices that have evolved over the last ten years.

Hi Wendyit was great to touch bases today, thank you! Turns out, I just got the approved RRT list today. For WA, your RRT is as follows:
From:
Good morning SWAP Regional Review Team (RRT) colleagues at the Alaska Department of Fish and Game (ADFG) and USFWS Pacific Region Office of Conservation Investment (OCI),
To prepare for your review, Washington Department of Fish and Wildlife is respectfully notifying you that we require an extension of our State Wildlife Action Plan delivery date to December 1, 2025.
The extension will help us deliver a better product that meets the Eight Required Elements and our agency's needs. Our SWAP Coordinator has discussed this option with the Pacific Region US Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) Office of Conservation Investment.
Although we started our revision process in late 2023 -early 2024, several processes and steps have taken longer than expected. Because of the added processes and time, we have not been able to complete plan-writing and chapter development in our expected timeline. The draft will be ready for review and engagement (Tribes, then public) in late September to early October and, as noted above we intend to deliver it to the RRT on December 1, 2025.
The extension to this revision's deadline would not affect Washington's State Wildlife Grant funding as we will continue to operate under our approved 2015 plan until we submit this new version to you for review. We understand that the deadline for the next comprehensive review of our SWAP will still be due by October 1, 2035, unless there are changes to the grant program or legislative requirements. We have made this request as soon as we understood the impact of the various delays. We are making progress on our revision and look forward to delivering a product you will want to explore and review.
We're grateful for the time you will spend reviewing our plan, and would appreciate acknowledgment from the Regional Review Team to document our extension. RRT members, please "reply all" to this email to let us know you have received our notification. Because the previously used ecological systems information is a static, unmaintained 'image' file that few other states used, we reviewed SWAP Best Practices (page 10) and then took additional time to understand agency uses and work with WA DNR NHP to select and apply national and international standards, with better maintained, scientifically supported, and spatially available vegetation classifications for habitat selections o National Vegetation Classification 'Groups' updated for Washington state ( 
2016
-current) for vegetated systems and o Global Ecosystem Typology 2.0 (2020) 'Functional Groups' for freshwater and marine aquatic habitats without a vegetation relationship; To reduce user frustration that we have heard over the last ten years, we took more time than originally anticipated to understand the differences between SWAP species and habitats and WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species to better align those two sources of information and management recommendations that improve conservation delivery in land use planning and environmental review; We will include relevant landscape conservation concepts and information from two major WDFW proviso-driven efforts completed at the end of June 2025 -the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan and Ecological Improvement Framework -and this will take more time; In an effort to better align with another SWAP Best Practice, we also shifted to use the threats and actions vocabulary from the Open Standards of Conservation Practice (2016) i.
The new searchable database is an intake and display structure that can relate to several of our internal agency data systems. It will not be the delivery mechanism for this SWAP review but will provide a much better foundation to transition to webenabled SWAP delivery for updates and improve display in the next year or two, which will help WDFW deliver more contemporary SWAP information over time. Tribal consultation should occur independent of and, where practicable, before any WDFW public meetings, workshops, or formal stakeholder engagement processes, to offer WDFW the opportunity to consider input from interested tribal governments prior to seeking public comment (Guidance for POL 5007, 2020).

Good afternoon SWAP Regional Review Team colleagues, Many thanks again for the prompt and supportive replies after my original, August extension email. I'm writing today to let you know that the Washington Department of Fish and Wildlife requires an additional extension of our State Wildlife Action Plan delivery date to December 31, 2025.
We do not take the timeline for this important document lightly, and have weighed the need for an additional 30 days very carefully. As noted below, one of the major accomplishments of our update is creating a detailed database of the species and habitats included in our plan, which will better support web-based SWAP content and implementation. However, ongoing technical challenges with this underlying databases has delayed some of the final coordination and editing we want to complete before submission.
As below, we understand that this extension will not affect the October 1, 2035 due date for our next update. We are also grateful for your attention and the time you will spend in review of our materials. I remain hopeful that this extension will contribute an easier review for you all, and that the change will not create hardship for you all in planning your work.
If possible, I would appreciate your acknowledgement of this notification. Of course, please let me know if you have any questions or wish to discuss this change.
Many thanks again for your ongoing understanding and support.

The extension will help us deliver a better product that meets the Eight Required Elements and our agency's needs. Our SWAP Coordinator has discussed this option with the Pacific Region US Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) Office of Conservation Investment.
Although we started our revision process in late 2023 -early 2024, several processes and steps have taken longer than expected. Because of the added processes and time, we have not been able to complete plan-writing and chapter development in our expected timeline. The draft will be ready for review and engagement (Tribes, then public) in late September to early October and, as noted above we intend to deliver it to the RRT on December 1, 2025.
The extension to this revision's deadline would not affect Washington's State Wildlife Grant funding as we will continue to operate under our approved 2015 plan until we submit this new version to you for review. We understand that the deadline for the next comprehensive review of our SWAP will still be due by October 1, 2035, unless there are changes to the grant program or legislative requirements. We have made this request as soon as we understood the impact of the various delays. We are making progress on our revision and look forward to delivering a product you will want to explore and review.
We're grateful for the time you will spend reviewing our plan, and would appreciate acknowledgment from the Regional Review Team to document our extension. RRT members, please "reply all" to this email to let us know you have received our notification. file that few other states used, we reviewed SWAP Best Practices (page 10) and then took additional time to understand agency uses and work with WA DNR NHP to select and apply national and international standards, with better maintained, scientifically supported, and spatially available vegetation classifications for habitat selections o National Vegetation Classification 'Groups' updated for Washington state (2016current) for vegetated systems and o Global Ecosystem Typology 2.0 (2020) 'Functional Groups' for freshwater and marine aquatic habitats without a vegetation relationship; To reduce user frustration that we have heard over the last ten years, we took more time than originally anticipated to understand the differences between SWAP species and habitats and WDFW's Priority Habitats and Species to better align those two sources of information and management recommendations that improve conservation delivery in land use planning and environmental review; We will include relevant landscape conservation concepts and information from two major WDFW proviso-driven efforts completed at the end of June 2025 -the Washington Habitat Connectivity Action Plan and Ecological Improvement Framework -and this will take more time;
In an effort to better align with another SWAP Best Practice, we also shifted to use the threats and actions vocabulary from the Open Standards of Conservation Practice ( 
2016
 i.
The new searchable database is an intake and display structure that can relate to several of our internal agency data systems. It will not be the delivery mechanism for this SWAP review but will provide a much better foundation to transition to web-enabled SWAP delivery for updates and improve display in the next year or two, which will help WDFW deliver more contemporary SWAP information over time. ii.
Washington State and WDFW have a unique relationship with Northwest Tribes in shared stewardship, conservation and resource management responsibilities. Twenty-nine (29) federally recognized tribes reside within Washington State of which 21 are treaty tribes and the additional are executive order tribes (or recognized by act of U.S. Congress). Several additional out-of-state tribes have traditional and ceded lands in our state. And, there are at least 8 tribal groups that do not have federal recognition within the boundaries of Washington State. WDFW policy (POL 5007) and intention is to treat Tribes as sovereign governments; coordinate, consult, and collaborate ahead of other entities and the public; and include Tribes in important planning, management, and implementation decisions. iii.
Tribal consultation should occur independent of and, where practicable, before any WDFW public meetings, workshops, or formal stakeholder engagement processes, to offer WDFW the opportunity to consider input from interested tribal governments prior to seeking public comment (Guidance for POL 5007, 2020).
From:
Greetings Regional Review Team for the Washington SWAP: With this email, Washington submits our 2025 SWAP comprehensive revision. Director Susewind, Wendy Connally (SWAP Coordinator) and I look forward to your review! As much work as it is to complete a SWAP Update, we know the review is also a significant investment of your time. Thank you.

## SWAP Materials.

We are providing a website for your access to the SWAP (PDF) and supporting materials (.xlsx tables linked in the SWAP for detailed information): 
https://wdfw.wa.gov/publications/02665
. Contact Us. Please let us know your questions! Wendy Connally, our SWAP Coordinator, will be on leave from December 19 th through January 9 th , so please feel free to place Teams meetings on our calendars following January 9 th to assist and address your questions. Wendy's priority when she returns is facilitating your review and reviewing Montana's SWAP.
Wendy Connally (she/her) State Wildlife Action Plan Coordinator Washington Department of Fish and Wildlife Email wendy.connally@dfw.wa.gov Phone (360) 902-2694 Mail PO Box 43200, Olympia WA 98504-3200
Technical Advice. We recommend that you turn on the PDF bookmarks if they don't automatically pop up for you. These sections may be of particular use to orient your "sufficiency" review:
• Appendix A Regional Review Team Roadmap using the USFWS template • Summary: SWAP Changes 2015 -2025
• Element 'tags' in sections to help you see the Element relationship to that section, if relevant
We have also offered an arcgis experience (email 11/24/2025, reminder 12/10/2025) that will enable an easier view of the range maps resulting from the processes described for the distribution portion of Element 1. The SWAP PDF and related tables are the authoritative source of information for this review; the dashboard is just a helpful visual for the SGCN distribution element. Because we could not get everyone on a meeting ahead of this delivery, a short user guide with screenshots is attached here to help you. The information in the dashboard is from our spring-summer Subject Matter Expert review and is being updated with information that is reflected in the SWAP Tables A, C, G, I, and J we have submitted to you. No changes were made to the range/distribution (Tables 
D
 and
E
) so the dashboard is appropriate to support your review for that element. A streamlined, updated dashboard will be released on our SWAP website following RRT review with the same information you have in the tables.
Once again, we are grateful for your time and attention and look forward to assisting you however possible in your review. With the holidays approaching, Wendy and I will both be most available in the New Year. Thank you in advance for your patience, and we look forward to hearing from you when we return.
Respectfully, *Margen Carlson

Westside Prairie Westside prairies are one of the rarest ecosystems in western Washington. Prior to settlement by nonindigenous people in Washington, prairie ecosystems covered an estimated 180,000 acres of Western Washington. As large areas of prairie were converted to farmland and development, most of that prairie is now gone and much of what is left is degraded. Once common in the South Puget Sound region, only three percent of westside prairies remain. 196 Both wet and dry prairies are included within the Prairie Strategic HGCN. Westside prairie ecosystems extend from Oregon's Willamette Valley through Washington's Puget Sound region north into the Georgia Basin region of southern British Columbia
195 Cramer
2012
For a deeper dive into eastside grasslands and some key past restoration efforts, see WDFW 2011.
